Importantly, drawing a random subsample from an existing dataset is not equivalent to drawing a random sample from every population of interest. The original data-collection procedure determines which population conclusions may reasonably concern.

Application: comparing conversion rates in an A/B test.

Hypothesis testing is widely used in data science to compare products, algorithms, interfaces, and business processes. An A/B test is a common example: users are randomly assigned to two alternatives, and we compare an outcome of interest.

Suppose version A is an existing checkout page and version B is a new design. Let \(p_A\) and \(p_B\) denote the probabilities of conversion for the corresponding user populations under the study design.

If we want to investigate whether the conversion probabilities differ in either direction, we may define \[ \delta=p_B-p_A \] and formulate \[ H_0:\delta=0 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\delta\neq0. \]

If the research question concerns only an improvement from version B, a one-sided alternative may instead be appropriate: \[ H_0:\delta\leq0 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\delta>0. \] This direction should be specified before examining the experimental outcome.

Experiment 27: formulating hypotheses for an A/B test. 

import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

# Simulated independent user-level conversions.
n_a = 1000
n_b = 1000

conversions_a = rng.binomial(
    n=1, p=0.10, size=n_a
)

conversions_b = rng.binomial(
    n=1, p=0.12, size=n_b
)

rate_a = np.mean(conversions_a)
rate_b = np.mean(conversions_b)

estimated_difference = rate_b - rate_a

print(f"Version A conversion rate: {rate_a:.2%}")
print(f"Version B conversion rate: {rate_b:.2%}")

print(
    "Estimated difference: "
    f"{100 * estimated_difference:.2f} percentage points"
)

# Hypotheses:
# H0: p_B - p_A = 0
# H1: p_B - p_A != 0

# The observed difference is not itself
# sufficient for deciding whether to reject H0.

The simulated conversion rates differ, but observing a difference is not the same as establishing evidence against the null. Random variation can produce nonzero differences even when the underlying conversion probabilities are equal.

In a real experiment, the unit of randomization and the unit of analysis matter. For example, treating repeated sessions from the same user as independent observations can produce misleading estimates of uncertainty.

Random assignment helps support causal interpretation, but it does not automatically eliminate problems caused by missing outcomes, noncompliance, inconsistent measurement, or interference between users.

Why observed differences do not automatically contradict a null hypothesis.

Suppose the null hypothesis about a population mean is true: \[ H_0:\mu=100. \] Even under this hypothesis, a random sample will generally not have mean exactly 100. The sample mean fluctuates because the observations are random.

If \[ X_1,\ldots,X_n \overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(100,10^2), \] then \[ \overline{X} \sim N\left(100,\frac{10^2}{n}\right). \] For \(n=25\), the standard deviation of the sample mean is \(10/\sqrt{25}=2\).

We can visualize this variability by simulating many independent studies under the same null hypothesis.

Experiment 28: sampling variability under a true null hypothesis. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

mu_0 = 100
sigma = 10
n = 25
repetitions = 10_000

# Each row represents an independent study.
samples = rng.normal(
    loc=mu_0,
    scale=sigma,
    size=(repetitions, n)
)

sample_means = samples.mean(axis=1)

print(
    "Mean of simulated sample means:",
    round(np.mean(sample_means), 3)
)

print(
    "SD of simulated sample means:",
    round(np.std(sample_means, ddof=1), 3)
)

print(
    "Theoretical standard error:",
    round(sigma / np.sqrt(n), 3)
)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

ax.hist(
    sample_means,
    bins=50,
    density=True,
    alpha=0.7
)

ax.axvline(
    mu_0,
    linestyle="--",
    label="Null population mean"
)

ax.set_xlabel("Sample mean")
ax.set_ylabel("Density")
ax.set_title(
    "Sampling variability when the null is true"
)

ax.legend()

fig.tight_layout()

fig.savefig(
    "hypothesis_sampling_variability.png",
    dpi=180,
    bbox_inches="tight"
)

plt.show()

# Experiment: repeat with n = 100 and n = 400.
# Compare the variability of the sample means.

The simulated sample means cluster around the null population mean, but most do not equal it exactly. This is expected even though the null hypothesis is true.

A statistical test must therefore distinguish ordinary sampling variation from deviations that are sufficiently unusual under the null model. This is the motivation for test statistics, reference distributions, significance levels, and p-values.

Hypotheses, assumptions, and interpretation.

A statistical hypothesis is not the same as a model assumption. For example, testing \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\) using a normal-theory procedure does not automatically verify normality or independence.

Moreover, even a correctly calculated test cannot overcome serious problems with sampling or study design. A small p-value from a biased sample does not establish that the corresponding population conclusion is valid.

It is also important to distinguish statistical evidence from practical importance. A very small difference may be detectable with a sufficiently large sample, even when the difference is of little practical interest.

In contrast, a study with insufficient data may fail to detect a difference that would be important in practice. Estimating the effect size and its uncertainty can therefore be as important as testing a null hypothesis.

Finally, when the goal is to demonstrate that two treatments are sufficiently similar, an ordinary failure to reject a difference is not adequate evidence of equivalence. An equivalence test requires a prespecified margin describing what counts as a practically negligible difference.

Questions to answer before performing a test.

  • Population: Which population or data-generating process is the conclusion intended to describe?
  • Parameter: What unknown numerical quantity is being investigated?
  • Hypotheses: What are \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), and is the alternative one-sided or two-sided?
  • Study design: How were observations collected? Are they independent, paired, or clustered?
  • Model: Which distributional assumptions are needed for the chosen procedure?
  • Practical importance: What magnitude of difference would matter in the application?

Try it yourself. (1) Formulate one-sided and two-sided hypotheses for a company investigating whether average delivery times have changed from a reference value. (2) Explain why \(H_0:\mu=100\) is simple under a normal model with known variance but composite when the variance is unknown. (3) In Experiment 27, double the number of users in both groups and compare the observed conversion-rate differences. (4) In Experiment 28, increase the sample size and investigate how the standard error changes. (5) Explain why failing to reject a difference does not establish practical equivalence.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 9.1, beginning on p. 530, introduces null and alternative hypotheses, simple and composite hypotheses, test statistics, and critical regions. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Section 5.3.1, beginning on p. 189, introduces the hypothesis-testing framework and emphasizes the distinction between rejecting and failing to reject the null. The A/B testing example extends these concepts to a data science application.

Error and Significance Level

A statistical test makes a decision using random data. Even when the test is correctly designed, that decision can be wrong. We distinguish between rejecting a true null hypothesis and failing to reject a false null hypothesis, and we study how frequently these errors occur under repeated sampling. All probabilities below are conditional on a specified statistical model and data-generating process.

Definition (type I and type II errors).

A type I error occurs when a statistical test rejects a true null hypothesis. A type II error occurs when the test fails to reject a false null hypothesis.

Decision \(H_0\) is true \(H_0\) is false
Reject \(H_0\) Type I error (false positive) Correct rejection (true positive)
Fail to reject \(H_0\) Correct non-rejection Type II error (false negative)

For example, suppose a factory tests whether the mean filling weight of a machine differs from \(500\,\mathrm{g}\). We formulate \(H_0:\mu=500\) versus \(H_1:\mu\neq500\). Stopping a correctly calibrated machine because we reject \(H_0\) is a type I error. Failing to detect a genuinely miscalibrated machine is a type II error. The practical costs of these mistakes need not be equal.

Definition (power function).

Let \(X=(X_1,\ldots,X_n)\) denote the random observations, and let \(R\) be the rejection region of a test. For a statistical model \(\{P_\theta:\theta\in\Theta\}\), the power function is \[ \pi(\theta)=P_\theta(X\in R). \] Thus, \(\pi(\theta)\) is the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) when the true parameter is \(\theta\).

Let \(\Theta_0\) and \(\Theta_1\) denote the parameter values covered by the null and alternative hypotheses. The interpretation of the power function depends on which hypothesis is true: \[ \begin{aligned} \theta\in\Theta_0:\quad &\pi(\theta)=P_\theta(\text{type I error}),\\[4pt] \theta\in\Theta_1:\quad &\beta(\theta)=1-\pi(\theta) =P_\theta(\text{type II error}),\\[4pt] \theta\in\Theta_1:\quad &1-\beta(\theta)=\pi(\theta) =P_\theta(\text{correct rejection}). \end{aligned} \]

The quantity \(\beta(\theta)\) is the type II error probability at the specified alternative \(\theta\), and \(1-\beta(\theta)\) is the test's power there. For a composite alternative, power generally depends on the true parameter value. Consequently, saying only that a test has "80% power" is incomplete without specifying the effect of interest, the model, and the test design.

Definition (size and significance level).

The size of a test is its largest rejection probability under the null hypothesis: \[ \operatorname{size}(R) =\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_0}P_\theta(X\in R). \] A test has significance level \(\alpha\) if \[ \operatorname{size}(R)\leq\alpha. \]

Thus, a level-\(0.05\) test controls the probability of rejecting a true null to at most \(5\%\), for every parameter value in the null hypothesis, provided the model assumptions hold. The actual size may be smaller than the chosen nominal level, especially for discrete tests. A test whose size equals \(\alpha\) is called an exact-size \(\alpha\) test.

The significance level is a design property of the testing procedure, normally selected before examining the outcomes. It is not the probability that the null hypothesis is true and does not say that \(5\%\) of all statistically significant findings must be false.

Worked example: detecting a biased coin.

Toss a coin independently ten times, and let \(X\) denote the number of heads. Consider the one-sided hypotheses \[ H_0:p\leq0.5 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:p>0.5, \] where \(p\) is the probability of heads. Under a fixed value of \(p\), \(X\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(10,p)\). Suppose our rejection rule is \(X\geq9\).

The rejection probability is largest under the null at its boundary \(p=0.5\). Therefore the size of this test is \[ \begin{aligned} \sup_{p\leq0.5}P_p(X\geq9) &=P_{0.5}(X\geq9)\\ &=\binom{10}{9}(0.5)^{10}+(0.5)^{10}\\ &=\frac{11}{1024}\approx0.01074. \end{aligned} \] This is a valid level-\(0.05\) test, although its actual size is only about \(1.07\%\). Rejecting at eight heads instead would give \[ P_{0.5}(X\geq8)=\frac{56}{1024}\approx0.05469, \] exceeding the proposed level. The discrete distribution prevents either of these non-randomized threshold rules from attaining size exactly \(0.05\).

Now suppose the coin is genuinely biased, with \(p=0.8\). The power is \[ \begin{aligned} \pi(0.8) &=P_{0.8}(X\geq9)\\ &=10(0.8)^9(0.2)+(0.8)^{10}\\ &\approx0.37581. \end{aligned} \] Hence \(\beta(0.8)\approx0.62419\). Even for this strongly biased coin, the test fails to detect the deviation in approximately \(62.4\%\) of repeated experiments.

Experiment 29: exact size and power of a binomial test. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import binom

n = 10
cutoff = 9

# Reject H0 if the number of heads is at least 9.
size = binom.sf(cutoff - 1, n, p=0.5)
size_if_eight = binom.sf(7, n, p=0.5)
power = binom.sf(cutoff - 1, n, p=0.8)
type_ii_error = 1 - power

print(f"Actual test size: {size:.5f}")
print(f"Size with cutoff 8: {size_if_eight:.5f}")
print(f"Power at p=0.8: {power:.5f}")
print(f"Type II error at p=0.8: {type_ii_error:.5f}")

# Examine the power function for a range of true values.
for p in [0.3, 0.5, 0.6, 0.8, 1.0]:
    rejection_probability = binom.sf(cutoff - 1, n, p=p)
    print(f"p={p:.1f}: P(reject)={rejection_probability:.4f}")

assert np.isclose(size, 11 / 1024)

# Experiment: repeat with 20 tosses and choose a level-0.05 cutoff.

This example illustrates the central trade-off: requiring more evidence before rejecting reduces false alarms but can also increase the chance of missing a genuine effect.

Worked example: power of a one-sided normal-mean test.

Suppose \(X_1,\ldots,X_n\) are independent observations from \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\), with known \(\sigma=10\). We want to test \[ H_0:\mu\leq100 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\mu>100 \] at level \(\alpha=0.05\), using \(n=25\) observations. Since \[ \overline{X}\sim N\left(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right), \qquad \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n}=2, \] the rejection rule is \[ \begin{aligned} Z=\frac{\overline{X}-100}{2} &>z_{0.95}\approx1.64485,\\ \text{equivalently}\quad\overline{X} &>c=100+2z_{0.95}\approx103.2897. \end{aligned} \] Here \(z_{0.95}=\Phi^{-1}(0.95)\), where \(\Phi\) is the standard normal CDF.

At the boundary \(\mu=100\), the rejection probability equals \(0.05\). For every \(\mu<100\), it is smaller. Therefore the test has size \(0.05\) over the entire composite null \(\mu\leq100\).

Theorem (power function of the one-sided z-test).

Under the stated independent normal model with known \(\sigma\), the test rejecting \(H_0:\mu\leq\mu_0\) when \[ \overline{X}>\mu_0+ z_{1-\alpha}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} \] has power function \[ \pi(\mu)=1-\Phi\left( z_{1-\alpha} -\frac{\mu-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt n} \right). \]

Proof. Let \(c=\mu_0+z_{1-\alpha}\sigma/\sqrt n\). Because the sample mean has a normal distribution with mean \(\mu\) and standard deviation \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), \[ \begin{aligned} \pi(\mu) &=P_\mu(\overline{X}>c)\\ &=1-\Phi\left(\frac{c-\mu}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right)\\ &=1-\Phi\left(z_{1-\alpha} -\frac{\mu-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt n}\right). \end{aligned} \]

For the example above, if the true mean is \(\mu=105\), then \[ \begin{aligned} \beta(105) &=P_{105}(\overline{X}\leq103.2897)\\ &=\Phi\left(\frac{103.2897-105}{2}\right) \approx0.19624,\\ \pi(105)&=1-\beta(105)\approx0.80376. \end{aligned} \] In repeated studies of this design, approximately \(80.4\%\) would reject \(H_0\) if the true mean were 105, and approximately \(19.6\%\) would fail to reject it. These are probabilities for repeated decisions, not probabilities that any particular conclusion is correct.

The rejection probability varies with the true mean:

True mean \(\mu\) \(\pi(\mu)\) Interpretation
\(98\) \(0.0041\) Type I error probability inside the null
\(100\) \(0.0500\) Test size at the null boundary
\(102\) \(0.2595\) Low power for a small increase
\(105\) \(0.8038\) About 80% power
\(110\) \(0.9996\) Very high power for a large increase

Experiment 30: simulating type I error and power. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

mu_0, sigma, n, alpha = 100, 10, 25, 0.05
standard_error = sigma / np.sqrt(n)
cutoff = mu_0 + norm.isf(alpha) * standard_error

# Each draw represents the mean of one entire study.
repetitions = 100_000
null_means = rng.normal(100, standard_error, size=repetitions)
alt_means = rng.normal(105, standard_error, size=repetitions)

empirical_size = np.mean(null_means > cutoff)
empirical_power = np.mean(alt_means > cutoff)
theoretical_power = norm.sf((cutoff - 105) / standard_error)

print(f"Critical sample mean: {cutoff:.4f}")
print(f"Simulated type I error: {empirical_size:.4f}")
print(f"Simulated power at mu=105: {empirical_power:.4f}")
print(f"Theoretical power: {theoretical_power:.4f}")

x = np.linspace(92, 113, 500)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

ax.plot(x, norm.pdf(x, 100, standard_error), label="mu = 100")
ax.plot(x, norm.pdf(x, 105, standard_error), label="mu = 105")
ax.axvline(cutoff, linestyle="--", label="Rejection cutoff")
ax.set_xlabel("Sample mean")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability density")
ax.set_title("Sampling distributions and rejection cutoff")
ax.legend()

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig(
    "sampling_distributions.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight"
)
plt.show()

# Experiment: change the alternative mean from 105 to 102.

Simulating the sample means directly is legitimate here because their sampling distributions are known exactly. The rejection cutoff is the same in both cases; what changes is the probability that the statistic falls beyond that cutoff.

The effects of significance level, sample size, and variability.

For a fixed test, sample size, and true effect, increasing \(\alpha\) enlarges the rejection region and generally increases power, but also allows more false rejections. For the normal-mean example with true \(\mu=105\), \(n=25\), and \(\sigma=10\):

Level \(\alpha\) Power at \(\mu=105\) Type II error
\(0.01\) \(0.5689\) \(0.4311\)
\(0.05\) \(0.8038\) \(0.1962\)
\(0.10\) \(0.8885\) \(0.1115\)

Power also depends on the magnitude of the true effect relative to variability. In our example, \(\delta=\mu-\mu_0=5\) and \(\sigma=10\), so the standardized difference is \(d=\delta/\sigma=0.5\). More observations reduce the standard error \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), making a fixed positive effect easier to detect. At \(\alpha=0.05\), power is approximately \(0.4424\) for \(n=9\), \(0.6388\) for \(n=16\), \(0.8038\) for \(n=25\), and \(0.9996\) for \(n=100\).

In regular models, power approaches the test's null rejection probability as the true parameter approaches the null boundary. No fixed sample size guarantees reliable detection of arbitrarily small differences. Increasing sample size or improving the design can increase power without raising the significance level.

Experiment 31: power curves for different sample sizes. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

mu_0, sigma, alpha = 100, 10, 0.05
true_means = np.linspace(96, 110, 300)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

for n in [9, 16, 25, 100]:
    standard_error = sigma / np.sqrt(n)
    power = norm.sf(
        norm.isf(alpha) - (true_means - mu_0) / standard_error
    )
    ax.plot(true_means, power, label=f"n = {n}")

ax.axhline(0.80, linestyle="--", label="80% power")
ax.axvline(mu_0, linestyle=":", label="Null boundary")
ax.set_xlabel("True population mean")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability of rejecting H0")
ax.set_title("Power functions of the one-sided z-test")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1.02)
ax.legend()

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("power_functions.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: replace sigma=10 by sigma=20.
# How much more data is needed for similar power?

Planning sample size before collecting data.

A power analysis starts with a prespecified effect worth detecting, an acceptable type I error level \(\alpha\), and a target power \(1-\beta\). For the right-sided, known-\(\sigma\) normal-mean test, suppose the true mean of interest is \(\mu_1=\mu_0+\delta\), with \(\delta>0\). Solving the power equation gives \[ n\geq\left( \frac{\sigma\left[z_{1-\alpha}+z_{1-\beta}\right]} {\delta} \right)^2. \]

For \(\sigma=10\), \(\delta=5\), \(\alpha=0.05\), and target power \(0.80\), \[ n\geq\left(\frac{10(1.64485+0.84162)}{5}\right)^2 \approx24.73. \] Therefore, we need at least \(n=25\) independent observations under this model. We must round the calculated sample size upward.

This formula applies specifically to a one-sided z-test with known standard deviation. A two-sided alternative, unknown variance, clustering, missing data, or other model features can change the required sample size. In practice, the target \(\delta\) should be chosen for its substantive importance rather than selected because it produces a convenient sample size.

Experiment 32: planning sample size. 

from math import ceil
from scipy.stats import norm
from statsmodels.stats.power import TTestPower

alpha = 0.05
target_power = 0.80
sigma = 10
delta = 5

# One-sided z-test with known sigma.
z_alpha = norm.isf(alpha)
z_power = norm.ppf(target_power)
n_z = ((z_alpha + z_power) * sigma / delta) ** 2

print("One-sided z-test n:", ceil(n_z))

# Two-sided one-sample t-test with estimated variance.
test = TTestPower()
n_t = test.solve_power(
    effect_size=delta / sigma,
    nobs=None,
    alpha=alpha,
    power=target_power,
    alternative="two-sided",
)

n_t_rounded = ceil(n_t)
print("Two-sided t-test n:", n_t_rounded)
print("Power after rounding:", round(
    test.power(
        effect_size=delta / sigma,
        nobs=n_t_rounded,
        alpha=alpha,
        alternative="two-sided",
    ), 4
))

# Experiment: halve delta and compare the required sample sizes.

Sample-size calculations are planning tools, not guarantees that an individual study will reach statistical significance. Their accuracy depends on the assumed variability, effect size, analysis procedure, and study design.

Statistical significance versus practical importance.

A result is called statistically significant at level \(\alpha\) when the chosen test rejects \(H_0\). This does not imply that the detected difference is large or important in practice. With sufficiently large samples, even a tiny departure from a null value may be detected reliably.

For example, suppose \(\sigma=1\) is known, \(n=10{,}000\), and an observed sample mean is \(\bar{x}=0.05\). For the two-sided test \(H_0:\mu=0\), the z-statistic is \[ z=\frac{0.05}{1/\sqrt{10{,}000}}=5, \] with two-sided p-value approximately \(5.73\times10^{-7}\). The result is statistically significant, but the difference is only \(0.05\) population standard deviations. Whether that is important depends on the application.

A corresponding 95% confidence interval is \[ 0.05\pm1.96\left(\frac{1}{100}\right) \approx(0.0304,0.0696). \] Reporting an effect estimate together with its uncertainty is usually more informative than reporting only a binary significance decision. We will define p-values and develop confidence intervals in later sections.

Why a 5% level does not mean 5% of discoveries are false.

Consider 1000 studies: suppose 900 null hypotheses are true and 100 alternatives are true. If each study uses a test of size \(0.05\), and power \(0.80\) at each true alternative, the expected numbers of rejections are \[ \begin{aligned} \text{False rejections: }&900(0.05)=45,\\ \text{Correct rejections: }&100(0.80)=80. \end{aligned} \] Thus, \(45/(45+80)=0.36\) is the ratio of these expected counts. This illustrative calculation shows why a 5% significance level does not imply that only 5% of reported significant results are false. The ratio of expectations is not, in general, the expected realized false-discovery proportion.

Multiple testing raises a related concern. If all 20 null hypotheses are true, 20 tests are independent, and each has size 0.05, the probability of at least one false rejection is \[ 1-(1-0.05)^{20}\approx0.6415. \] This probability is called the family-wise error rate (FWER). The formula requires independence and actual test size 0.05; it does not apply unchanged to arbitrary dependent tests.

A basic method for controlling the FWER is the Bonferroni correction: for \(m\) prespecified tests, test each hypothesis at level \(\alpha/m\). By the union bound, the probability of one or more false rejections is then at most \(\alpha\), without requiring independence. This protection can come at the cost of lower power.

Experiment 33: repeated testing and family-wise error. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

studies = 100_000
tests_per_study = 20
alpha = 0.05

# Under the true null, the two-sided z-statistics are standard normal.
z = rng.standard_normal((studies, tests_per_study))
p_values = 2 * norm.sf(np.abs(z))

# A study has a false alarm if at least one test rejects.
any_unadjusted = np.any(p_values <= alpha, axis=1)
any_bonferroni = np.any(
    p_values <= alpha / tests_per_study, axis=1
)

print("Theoretical unadjusted FWER:", round(
    1 - (1 - alpha) ** tests_per_study, 4
))
print("Simulated unadjusted FWER:", round(
    np.mean(any_unadjusted), 4
))
print("Simulated Bonferroni FWER:", round(
    np.mean(any_bonferroni), 4
))

# Experiment: change the number of tests to 5 and 100.

These simulations assume independent tests with correctly calibrated null p-values. In real A/B testing or feature screening, outcomes may be correlated, and decisions about which results to report may depend on the observed data. Prespecifying hypotheses and using appropriate multiple-testing procedures helps preserve intended error guarantees. More detailed treatment belongs to the multiple-testing topic.

Points to check before interpreting a test.

  • Non-rejection is not proof: failing to reject a null hypothesis does not establish that it is true. A study may have insufficient power to detect a meaningful effect.
  • Power requires a specified alternative: it depends on the effect size, sample size, variability, model, and test procedure.
  • Significance is not a posterior probability: neither a significance level nor a p-value gives \(P(H_0\mid\text{observed data})\) by itself.
  • Model assumptions matter: dependence, selection bias, missing observations, or a wrong sampling distribution can invalidate nominal error calculations.
  • Analysis choices matter: selecting a direction, stopping time, or preferred analysis after looking at outcomes can invalidate error guarantees unless the procedure accounts for it.
  • Significance is not causality: a statistical association, however significant, need not be caused by the variable studied.

Exercises.

Exercise 1. A binomial test rejects when \(X\geq9\), where \(X\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(10,p)\). Calculate its type I error probability at \(p=0.5\) and power at \(p=0.8\). Check: approximately \(0.01074\) and \(0.37581\).

Exercise 2. A test has power \(0.90\) at a particular alternative. What is its type II error probability at that alternative? Check: \(\beta=0.10\).

Exercise 3. For the normal-mean example with \(\mu_0=100\), \(\sigma=10\), \(n=25\), and \(\alpha=0.05\), calculate power at \(\mu=102\). Check: approximately \(0.2595\).

Exercise 4. Why must the sample-size requirement \(n\geq24.73\) be rounded upward rather than to the nearest integer? What would happen to power at \(n=24\)?

Exercise 5. For 20 independent tests under true nulls, calculate the probability of at least one rejection at level \(0.01\). Compare it with the corresponding probability at level \(0.05\).

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Chapter 9, especially Section 9.1, develops tests, rejection regions, and error probabilities. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Chapter 5, especially Section 5.3, gives an applied introduction to significance testing. For additional discussion, see Random Services: Hypothesis Testing. The Python examples use scipy.stats and statsmodels.stats.power.

Critical Value and the p-Value

Once a null hypothesis, an alternative hypothesis, and a test statistic have been specified, we need a rule for deciding whether the observed data provide sufficiently strong evidence against the null. Two equivalent ways to express the decision for a given test are the critical-value approach and the p-value approach. Both depend on the distribution of the test statistic under the null hypothesis.

The test statistic and its null distribution.

Let \(X=(X_1,\ldots,X_n)\) denote the random observations and let \(T=T(X)\) be a test statistic. Before observing the data, \(T\) is a random variable; after observing \(\mathbf{x}\), its value \(t_{\mathrm{obs}}=T(\mathbf{x})\) is a fixed number. A null distribution describes the sampling distribution of \(T\) under a distribution permitted by \(H_0\). For a composite null, this distribution may depend on unknown nuisance parameters, or the test may use a statistic with a null law that does not depend on them.

For example, suppose \(X_1,\ldots,X_n\) are independent and normally distributed with unknown mean \(\mu\) and known standard deviation \(\sigma>0\). Under \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\), the statistic \[ Z=\frac{\overline{X}-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \] follows the standard normal distribution \(N(0,1)\). This exact statement concerns the standardized sample mean, not the individual observations.

Worked example. Let \(n=25\), \(\mu_0=50\), \(\sigma=10\), and suppose the observed sample mean is \(\bar{x}=54.2\). Then \[ z_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{54.2-50}{10/\sqrt{25}}=2.10. \] The sample mean is 2.10 standard errors above the hypothesized mean. Whether this leads to rejection depends on the alternative and the significance level, not just on the sign of the statistic.

Definition (critical value and rejection region).

A rejection region is the set of observed outcomes for which a test rejects \(H_0\). When the decision depends on a statistic \(T\), the rejection region can be specified using values of \(T\). A critical value is a boundary used to define that region. The boundaries are chosen so that the probability of rejection under \(H_0\) is no greater than the chosen significance level \(\alpha\).

Let \(\Phi\) denote the standard normal CDF and \(\Phi^{-1}\) its quantile function. Consider the z-statistic above and a significance level \(\alpha\in(0,1)\). The usual rejection regions are as follows.

Alternative Critical value(s) Reject when
Right-tailed: \(\mu>\mu_0\) \(c_R=\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha)\) \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\geq c_R\)
Left-tailed: \(\mu<\mu_0\) \(c_L=\Phi^{-1}(\alpha)\) \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\leq c_L\)
Two-sided: \(\mu\neq\mu_0\) \(\pm\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\) \(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|\geq\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\)

At \(\alpha=0.05\), the right-tail cutoff is approximately \(1.6449\), the left-tail cutoff is approximately \(-1.6449\), and the two-sided cutoffs are approximately \(-1.9600\) and \(1.9600\). Thus, for \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=2.10\), we reject for the right-tailed and two-sided alternatives but fail to reject for the left-tailed alternative.

The equal-tail two-sided rule is appropriate for this symmetric normal statistic. There is no universal requirement that a two-sided rejection region be symmetric or allocate equal probabilities to both tails; the rejection rule must be defined by the particular statistical test.

Experiment 34: critical regions for normal tests. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

alpha = 0.05
z_obs = 2.10

left = norm.ppf(alpha)
right = norm.isf(alpha)
two_lower = norm.ppf(alpha / 2)
two_upper = norm.isf(alpha / 2)

print(f"Left cutoff: {left:.4f}")
print(f"Right cutoff: {right:.4f}")
print(f"Two-sided cutoffs: {two_lower:.4f}, {two_upper:.4f}")

print("Reject left-tailed:", z_obs <= left)
print("Reject right-tailed:", z_obs >= right)
print("Reject two-sided:", abs(z_obs) >= two_upper)

x = np.linspace(-4, 4, 1000)
y = norm.pdf(x)

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(12, 3.8))
regions = [
    ("Left-tailed", x <= left),
    ("Right-tailed", x >= right),
    ("Two-sided", (x <= two_lower) | (x >= two_upper))
]

for ax, (title, mask) in zip(axes, regions):
    ax.plot(x, y)
    ax.fill_between(x, y, where=mask, alpha=0.35)
    ax.axvline(z_obs, linestyle="--", label="Observed z = 2.10")
    ax.set_title(title)
    ax.set_xlabel("z")
    ax.set_ylabel("Density")
    ax.grid(alpha=0.2)

axes[0].legend(fontsize=8)
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("critical_regions.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: change alpha to 0.01 and compare the regions.

What does a p-value measure?

A p-value measures how incompatible an observed test statistic is with the specified null model, using the notion of extremeness defined by the test. Smaller p-values indicate stronger evidence against the null in that testing procedure. A p-value is not a probability assigned to \(H_0\) itself.

Definition (p-value for a nested family of tests).

Suppose \(\{R_\alpha:0<\alpha<1\}\) is a family of rejection regions for a specified test, with \(R_{\alpha_1}\subseteq R_{\alpha_2}\) whenever \(\alpha_1\leq\alpha_2\). The p-value for the observed outcome \(\mathbf{x}\) is \[ p(\mathbf{x})=\inf\bigl(\{\alpha\in(0,1):\mathbf{x}\in R_\alpha\}\cup\{1\}\bigr), \] so the infimum equals 1 if rejection never occurs at a level below 1. Informally, it is the smallest significance level at which this family of tests rejects the observed outcome (with this convention).

For the usual one-sided tests with a simple continuous null distribution, this leads to a tail probability: the probability under \(H_0\) of a statistic at least as extreme as the observed one in the direction of \(H_1\). For two-sided or discrete tests, what counts as at least as extreme must be specified rather than assumed.

For our standard normal z-test, the conventional p-values are \[ \begin{aligned} p_{\mathrm{right}}&=1-\Phi(z_{\mathrm{obs}}),\\ p_{\mathrm{left}}&=\Phi(z_{\mathrm{obs}}),\\ p_{\mathrm{two}}&=2\bigl[1-\Phi(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|)\bigr]. \end{aligned} \] The factor of 2 in the two-sided formula follows from symmetry of the standard normal distribution; it is not a general formula for every test.

Worked example. With \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=2.10\), we obtain \[ \begin{aligned} p_{\mathrm{right}}&\approx0.01786,\\ p_{\mathrm{left}}&\approx0.98214,\\ p_{\mathrm{two}}&\approx0.03573. \end{aligned} \] The observed data are unchanged, but the p-values differ because the alternatives ask different questions. The direction of the test should therefore be selected before examining the data.

Experiment 35: p-values and tail areas.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

z_obs = 2.10
alpha = 0.05

p_values = {
    "Left-tailed": norm.cdf(z_obs),
    "Right-tailed": norm.sf(z_obs),
    "Two-sided": 2 * norm.sf(abs(z_obs))
}

for name, p_value in p_values.items():
    decision = "Reject H0" if p_value <= alpha else "Fail to reject H0"
    print(f"{name}: p={p_value:.5f} - {decision}")

x = np.linspace(-4, 4, 1000)
y = norm.pdf(x)
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(12, 3.8))

masks = [
    x <= z_obs,
    x >= z_obs,
    (x <= -abs(z_obs)) | (x >= abs(z_obs))
]

for ax, (name, p_value), mask in zip(axes, p_values.items(), masks):
    ax.plot(x, y)
    ax.fill_between(x, y, where=mask, alpha=0.35)
    ax.axvline(z_obs, linestyle="--")
    ax.set_title(f"{name}: p = {p_value:.4f}")
    ax.set_xlabel("z")
    ax.set_ylabel("Density")
    ax.grid(alpha=0.2)

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("p_value_areas.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: repeat with z_obs = -1.80.

Theorem (equivalence of decisions).

For the conventional continuous normal z-tests described above, the critical-value approach rejects at level \(\alpha\) if and only if the corresponding p-value satisfies \(p\leq\alpha\).

Proof. In the right-tailed case, the inequality \(1-\Phi(z_{\mathrm{obs}})\leq\alpha\) is equivalent to \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\geq\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha)\), since \(\Phi\) is strictly increasing. The left-tailed case is analogous. For a two-sided test, \(2[1-\Phi(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|)]\leq\alpha\) is equivalent to \(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|\geq\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\).

More generally, when a p-value is defined from the nested rejection regions of a test, the two approaches express the same rejection rule (with suitable treatment of boundary cases). The p-value is useful because it reports the result independently of a single chosen \(\alpha\): a two-sided p-value of approximately \(0.03573\) rejects at the 5% level but not at the 1% level. The evidence has not changed; only the decision threshold has.

Student's t, chi-square, and F-distributions.

Critical values and p-values are not restricted to normal tests. The relevant null distribution depends on the statistic and on the assumptions used to derive its sampling law. For example:

  • A Student's t-statistic may arise when a normal population mean is tested with unknown standard deviation.
  • A chi-square statistic may arise in a goodness-of-fit test or a test concerning a normal population variance.
  • An F-statistic may arise in an analysis of variance (ANOVA) or when comparing nested regression models.

The reference distribution alone does not determine whether the test is left-tailed, right-tailed, or two-sided. That depends on the hypotheses and on how the statistic is constructed.

Worked examples. For a two-sided t-test with 9 degrees of freedom and \(t_{\mathrm{obs}}=2.30\), the p-value is approximately \(0.04700\). For a right-tailed chi-square test with 4 degrees of freedom and observed statistic \(13.28\), the p-value is approximately \(0.00999\). For a right-tailed F-test with numerator and denominator degrees of freedom 5 and 10 and observed statistic \(3.50\), it is approximately \(0.04349\). Each example rejects at the 5% level for its stated test.

Experiment 36: p-values from other null distributions. 

from scipy.stats import t, chi2, f

alpha = 0.05

# Two-sided Student's t-test, 9 degrees of freedom.
t_obs, t_df = 2.30, 9
t_p = 2 * t.sf(abs(t_obs), df=t_df)
t_cutoff = t.isf(alpha / 2, df=t_df)

# Right-tailed chi-square test, 4 degrees of freedom.
chi_obs, chi_df = 13.28, 4
chi_p = chi2.sf(chi_obs, df=chi_df)
chi_cutoff = chi2.isf(alpha, df=chi_df)

# Right-tailed F-test, degrees of freedom (5, 10).
f_obs, df_num, df_den = 3.50, 5, 10
f_p = f.sf(f_obs, dfn=df_num, dfd=df_den)
f_cutoff = f.isf(alpha, dfn=df_num, dfd=df_den)

print(f"t-test: p={t_p:.5f}, upper cutoff={t_cutoff:.4f}")
print(f"Chi-square: p={chi_p:.5f}, cutoff={chi_cutoff:.4f}")
print(f"F-test: p={f_p:.5f}, cutoff={f_cutoff:.4f}")

assert t_p < alpha
assert chi_p < alpha
assert f_p < alpha

# Experiment: change the degrees of freedom and compare p-values.

Discrete tests and conservative significance levels.

For a discrete statistic, the possible rejection probabilities occur in steps. A non-randomized test may therefore have actual size strictly smaller than the nominal level \(\alpha\). Recall the example of 10 independent fair coin tosses under \(H_0:p=0.5\), with \(X\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(10,0.5)\), against \(H_1:p>0.5\).

If we reject for \(X\geq8\), the type I error probability is \[ P_{0.5}(X\geq8)=\frac{56}{1024}\approx0.05469, \] which exceeds \(0.05\). If instead we reject for \(X\geq9\), the probability is \[ P_{0.5}(X\geq9)=\frac{11}{1024}\approx0.01074. \] Thus, the latter rule is a valid level-0.05 test, but its actual size is approximately 1.07%.

If 8 heads are observed, the right-tailed p-value is \(P_{0.5}(X\geq8)\approx0.05469\), so we fail to reject at the 5% level. With 9 observed heads, the p-value is \(P_{0.5}(X\geq9)\approx0.01074\), so we reject. Notice the importance of including equality: for a discrete variable, \(P(X\geq k)\) and \(P(X>k)\) differ.

A two-sided test requires an additional convention for what counts as more extreme. For 8 observed heads under the fair-coin null, symmetry gives \[ P_{0.5}(X\leq2)+P_{0.5}(X\geq8)=0.109375. \] For an asymmetric binomial null, doubling a one-sided tail is not generally the same as a two-sided exact p-value. For example, SciPy's binomtest two-sided procedure sums the null probabilities of outcomes no more probable than the observed one.

Experiment 37: exact binomial p-values.

from scipy.stats import binom, binomtest

n = 10
p_0 = 0.5
alpha = 0.05

for successes in [8, 9]:
    right_p = binom.sf(successes - 1, n, p_0)
    exact_p = binomtest(
        successes, n, p_0, alternative="greater"
    ).pvalue

    print(f"Observed heads: {successes}")
    print(f"Right-tail p-value: {right_p:.5f}")
    print(f"Exact test p-value: {exact_p:.5f}")
    print("Reject:", exact_p <= alpha)
    print()

print(
    "Two-sided p-value for 8 heads:",
    binomtest(8, n, p_0, alternative="two-sided").pvalue
)

# Experiment: repeat for a null probability p_0 = 0.3.

The relationship with confidence intervals.

A confidence set can be constructed by retaining parameter values that the corresponding tests do not reject. In particular, for a two-sided normal z-test with known \(\sigma\), the null value \(\mu_0\) is rejected at level \(\alpha\) precisely when it lies outside the matching \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval \[ \bar{x}\pm\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}. \] The word matching is essential: different confidence interval procedures need not agree with tests constructed using different assumptions or rejection rules.

Worked example. Suppose \(\bar{x}=54.5\), \(n=25\), \(\sigma=10\), and \(H_0:\mu=50\) is tested against \(H_1:\mu\neq50\). The observed statistic is \(z=2.25\), giving the two-sided p-value \(p\approx0.02445\). The corresponding 95% confidence interval is \[ 54.5\pm1.9600\cdot2\approx(50.58,58.42). \] Because the interval excludes 50, both methods reject at the 5% level. The interval also reports a range of parameter values compatible with the observed data under this model.

Experiment 38: confidence interval and z-test decision. 

from scipy.stats import norm

sample_mean = 54.5
mu_0 = 50.0
sigma = 10.0
n = 25
alpha = 0.05

standard_error = sigma / n**0.5
z_obs = (sample_mean - mu_0) / standard_error
p_value = 2 * norm.sf(abs(z_obs))

margin = norm.isf(alpha / 2) * standard_error
lower = sample_mean - margin
upper = sample_mean + margin

reject_by_p = p_value <= alpha
reject_by_ci = not (lower <= mu_0 <= upper)

print(f"Observed z: {z_obs:.2f}")
print(f"Two-sided p-value: {p_value:.5f}")
print(f"95% CI: ({lower:.2f}, {upper:.2f})")
print("Reject by p-value:", reject_by_p)
print("Reject by confidence interval:", reject_by_ci)

assert reject_by_p == reject_by_ci

# Experiment: repeat with sample_mean = 53.0.

How are p-values distributed under the null hypothesis?

Before the data are collected, the p-value is itself a random variable. For an exactly calibrated test with a continuous null distribution, the p-value is uniformly distributed on \([0,1]\) under the simple null hypothesis.

Theorem (uniformity of the two-sided normal p-value).

Let \(Z\sim N(0,1)\) and define \(P=2[1-\Phi(|Z|)]\). Then \[ P\sim\operatorname{Uniform}(0,1). \]

Proof. For \(0<u<1\), symmetry of the standard normal distribution gives \[ \begin{aligned} \mathbb{P}(P\leq u) &=\mathbb{P}\!\left(|Z|\geq\Phi^{-1}(1-u/2)\right)\\ &=2\left[1-\Phi\!\left(\Phi^{-1}(1-u/2)\right)\right]\\ &=u. \end{aligned} \] Hence the CDF of \(P\) is the uniform CDF on \([0,1]\).

Consequently, under a true null and the stated test assumptions, approximately 5% of independent repetitions produce p-values at most 0.05. This statement does not mean that a particular observed p-value of 0.05 assigns 5% probability to the null hypothesis being true or false.

For a valid conservative or discrete test, the appropriate general property is often super-uniformity: \[ \mathbb{P}_{\theta}(P\leq u)\leq u \qquad\text{for all }\theta\in\Theta_0,\;0\leq u\leq1. \] The inequality may be strict because the p-value cannot assume every possible number between 0 and 1.

Experiment 39: p-value calibration under a true null. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm, binom

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
repetitions = 100_000
alpha = 0.05

# Exactly calibrated continuous normal test.
z = rng.standard_normal(repetitions)
p_normal = 2 * norm.sf(np.abs(z))

# Conservative discrete right-tailed binomial test.
heads = rng.binomial(n=10, p=0.5, size=repetitions)
p_binomial = binom.sf(heads - 1, n=10, p=0.5)

print("Normal rejection frequency:", np.mean(p_normal <= alpha))
print("Binomial rejection frequency:", np.mean(p_binomial <= alpha))

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4))

axes[0].hist(p_normal, bins=20, density=True, edgecolor="white")
axes[0].axhline(1, linestyle="--", label="Uniform density")
axes[0].set_title("Continuous normal null")
axes[0].set_xlabel("p-value")
axes[0].set_ylabel("Density")
axes[0].legend()

axes[1].hist(p_binomial, bins=20, range=(0, 1), edgecolor="white")
axes[1].set_title("Discrete binomial null")
axes[1].set_xlabel("p-value")
axes[1].set_ylabel("Frequency")

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("pvalue_uniformity.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: change alpha to 0.01 and 0.10.

Application: a one-sample test using real observations.

In practice, a statistic is computed from observations rather than supplied as a number. The following example uses a subsample of nonmissing weights from the SOGA-Py students dataset and tests the illustrative benchmark \(\mu_0=70.8\,\mathrm{kg}\). Because the population standard deviation is unknown, the example uses a one-sample t-test. The code illustrates the workflow; its population interpretation still depends on the original sampling design, independence, and the suitability of the t-test assumptions.

Experiment 40: one-sample t-test from data. 

import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import ttest_1samp

url = (
    "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/"
    "soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
)

students = pd.read_csv(url)
weights = students["weight"].dropna()

sample = weights.sample(n=30, random_state=17)
mu_0 = 70.8
alpha = 0.05

result = ttest_1samp(
    sample,
    popmean=mu_0,
    alternative="two-sided"
)

print("Sample size:", len(sample))
print("Sample mean:", round(sample.mean(), 2))
print("t-statistic:", round(result.statistic, 4))
print("p-value:", round(result.pvalue, 5))

if result.pvalue <= alpha:
    print("Reject H0 at the 5% level")
else:
    print("Fail to reject H0 at the 5% level")

# Experiment: change the benchmark or the alternative.
# The CSV must be accessible when this script is run.

This is an example of correct test computation, not automatically a justified inference about all students or another broader population. Selecting rows randomly from an existing dataset does not repair selection bias in the original collection, and deleting missing values does not guarantee that the remaining observations are representative.

Common interpretation errors.

  • A p-value is not \(\mathbb{P}(H_0\text{ is true}\mid\text{data})\). It is calculated from a specified null model. Hypothesis probabilities require additional modeling assumptions.
  • A large p-value does not establish that the null is true. It may reflect an effect too small to detect with the available sample, or a test with low power.
  • A small p-value does not measure effect size. Report an estimate and an uncertainty measure where possible, and assess whether the effect matters in practice.
  • The p-value depends on the entire testing procedure. The null model, alternative, statistic, and definition of extremeness all matter.
  • Selective testing and repeated analyses affect calibration. If many tests are performed and only the smallest p-value is reported without adjustment, the nominal single-test error interpretation can be misleading.

Try it yourself.

  1. For \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=-1.80\), calculate the left-tailed and two-sided normal p-values. Compare the decisions at \(\alpha=0.05\).
  2. Find the two-sided standard normal critical values for \(\alpha=0.01\). Verify them using norm.ppf.
  3. Under \(H_0:p=0.5\) with 10 coin tosses, find the smallest integer \(k\) such that rejecting for \(X\geq k\) has significance level at most 0.05.
  4. For \(\bar{x}=12\), known \(\sigma=4\), \(n=16\), test \(H_0:\mu=10\) against \(H_1:\mu\neq10\), and construct the matching 95% confidence interval.
  5. Explain why the numerical result \(T=2.3\) is insufficient to calculate a p-value without knowing the null distribution and the alternative hypothesis.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Chapter 9, especially Section 9.1, introduces significance tests, rejection regions, and p-values. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Chapter 5, develops hypothesis testing with applied examples. See also Random Services: Introduction to Hypothesis Testing and the SciPy statistics reference. The numerical examples here are illustrative rather than reproduced from the textbooks.

Inference for One Population Mean with Known Sigma

We now apply hypothesis tests and confidence intervals to a specific inference problem: estimating and testing a population mean \(\mu\) when the population standard deviation \(\sigma>0\) is known independently of the current sample. This leads to the one-sample z-test and the z-confidence interval. A genuinely known population standard deviation is uncommon in observational work, but it may be defensible for a stable measurement or manufacturing process with carefully validated historical variability. The model is also a useful foundation for understanding inference when \(\sigma\) must be estimated.

Model, parameter, and standard error.

Definition (known-sigma normal-mean model).

We observe independent random variables \[ X_1,\ldots,X_n\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu,\sigma^2), \] where \(\mu\in\mathbb{R}\) is unknown and \(\sigma>0\) is known. The sample mean is \[ \overline{X}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i. \]

The random variable \(\overline{X}\) is distinguished from its observed value \(\bar{x}\). By linearity of expectation and independence, \[ \begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}(\overline{X})&=\mu,\\ \operatorname{Var}(\overline{X}) &=\frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{Var}(X_i) =\frac{\sigma^2}{n}. \end{aligned} \] Consequently, the standard error of the sample mean is \(\operatorname{SE}(\overline{X})=\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). This is the standard deviation of the sampling distribution of the mean, not the standard deviation of individual measurements.

Theorem (standardized sample mean).

Under the known-sigma normal model, \[ \overline{X}\sim N\!\left(\mu,\frac{\sigma^2}{n}\right), \qquad \frac{\overline{X}-\mu}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}\sim N(0,1). \] Thus, under \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\), \[ Z=\frac{\overline{X}-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \overset{H_0}{\sim}N(0,1). \]

Proof. The sum of independent normal random variables is normal. Its mean is \(n\mu\) and its variance is \(n\sigma^2\), so division by \(n\) gives the asserted distribution of \(\overline{X}\). Standardizing produces a standard normal random variable. Under the null, replace \(\mu\) by \(\mu_0\).

The result is exact for every \(n\geq1\) under the stated normal model. For independent, identically distributed nonnormal observations with finite positive variance, the central limit theorem gives an asymptotic normal approximation: \[ \frac{\sqrt{n}(\overline{X}-\mu)}{\sigma} \xrightarrow{\,d\,}N(0,1). \] Its accuracy depends on the underlying distribution and the sample size; there is no universal rule that \(n\geq30\) guarantees a good approximation. Dependence, strong skewness, or very heavy tails can invalidate or delay it.

One-sided and two-sided z-tests.

Let \(\Phi\) be the CDF of the standard normal distribution and \(z_q=\Phi^{-1}(q)\). For an observed sample mean, define \[ z_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{\bar{x}-\mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}}. \] At significance level \(\alpha\), the standard decision rules are:

Alternative Rejection condition p-value
\(H_1:\mu\neq\mu_0\) \(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|\geq z_{1-\alpha/2}\) \(2[1-\Phi(|z_{\mathrm{obs}}|)]\)
\(H_1:\mu>\mu_0\) \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\geq z_{1-\alpha}\) \(1-\Phi(z_{\mathrm{obs}})\)
\(H_1:\mu<\mu_0\) \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\leq z_{\alpha}\) \(\Phi(z_{\mathrm{obs}})\)

For a one-sided test, the full null hypothesis is typically \(H_0:\mu\leq\mu_0\) (right-sided alternative) or \(H_0:\mu\geq\mu_0\) (left-sided alternative). The boundary \(\mu=\mu_0\) has the largest rejection probability within the composite null, so the corresponding critical values and p-values are computed there. The alternative must be chosen before inspecting the result.

The sign of \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}\) matters for directional claims. For instance, at \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=2.50\), the two-sided, right-sided, and left-sided p-values are approximately \(0.01242\), \(0.00621\), and \(0.99379\), respectively. A two-sided p-value is not the probability that the null hypothesis is true.

Confidence interval for the population mean.

Theorem (known-sigma confidence interval).

For \(0<\alpha<1\), the random interval \[ C(X)=\left[ \overline{X}-z_{1-\alpha/2}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},\, \overline{X}+z_{1-\alpha/2}\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} \right] \] contains the fixed population mean \(\mu\) with probability \(1-\alpha\): \[ \mathbb{P}_{\mu}\bigl(\mu\in C(X)\bigr)=1-\alpha. \]

Proof. Since \((\overline{X}-\mu)/(\sigma/\sqrt{n})\sim N(0,1)\), \[ \mathbb{P}_{\mu}\!\left( -z_{1-\alpha/2}\leq \frac{\overline{X}-\mu}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \leq z_{1-\alpha/2}\right)=1-\alpha. \] Rearranging the inequalities gives the claimed coverage probability.

After observing the data, the interval endpoints are fixed numbers. A 95% frequentist confidence interval does not mean that there is a 95% probability that the already fixed parameter lies in this particular observed interval. Instead, the method covers the true mean in 95% of repeated independent studies under the assumed model.

Property (duality of the z-test and confidence interval).

The two-sided level-\(\alpha\) z-test of \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\) rejects precisely when \(\mu_0\) lies outside the corresponding \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) two-sided z-confidence interval.

Proof. The test rejects exactly when \(|\bar{x}-\mu_0|>z_{1-\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}\), apart from an equality event of probability zero. This is equivalent to \(\mu_0\notin[\bar{x}-z_{1-\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}, \bar{x}+z_{1-\alpha/2}\sigma/\sqrt{n}]\).

For a right-sided alternative, the corresponding one-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) lower confidence bound is \(\bar{x}-z_{1-\alpha}\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). The null \(H_0:\mu\leq\mu_0\) is rejected if this bound exceeds \(\mu_0\). For a left-sided alternative, use the upper confidence bound \(\bar{x}+z_{1-\alpha}\sigma/\sqrt{n}\).

Worked example: a package-filling process.

Suppose the fill weights of a manufacturing process are independent normal observations with a separately calibrated standard deviation \(\sigma=12\) g. A random sample of \(n=36\) packages has mean \(\bar{x}=505\) g. We want to determine whether the current population mean differs from the target \(\mu_0=500\) g, using a preselected level \(\alpha=0.05\). This is a hypothetical example, not a report of factory measurements.

Test statistic. We specify \(H_0:\mu=500\) against \(H_1:\mu\neq500\). Then \[ \operatorname{SE}(\overline{X})=\frac{12}{\sqrt{36}}=2\ \mathrm{g}, \qquad z_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{505-500}{2}=2.50. \]

Critical-value approach. The two-sided critical values are \(\pm z_{0.975}\approx\pm1.960\), corresponding to the sample-mean rejection region \[ \bar{x}<500-1.960(2)\approx496.08 \quad\text{or}\quad \bar{x}>500+1.960(2)\approx503.92. \] Since \(505>503.92\), we reject \(H_0\).

p-value approach. The two-sided p-value is \[ p=\mathbb{P}_{H_0}(|Z|\geq2.50) =2[1-\Phi(2.50)]\approx0.01242. \] The same decision follows because \(p<0.05\).

Confidence interval and effect size. The observed 95% confidence interval is \[ 505\pm1.960\cdot2\approx[501.08,508.92]\ \mathrm{g}. \] It excludes 500 g, agreeing with the test. The estimated difference is \(\bar{x}-\mu_0=5\) g. Whether this shift is operationally important requires a practical tolerance, not merely a statistical threshold.

Experiment 41: test and interval from summary statistics. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import norm

mu_0 = 500.0
sigma = 12.0       # Known from separate calibration
n = 36
sample_mean = 505.0
alpha = 0.05

standard_error = sigma / np.sqrt(n)
z_observed = (sample_mean - mu_0) / standard_error
p_two_sided = 2 * norm.sf(abs(z_observed))
p_right = norm.sf(z_observed)
p_left = norm.cdf(z_observed)

critical = norm.isf(alpha / 2)
ci_low = sample_mean - critical * standard_error
ci_high = sample_mean + critical * standard_error

print(f"Standard error: {standard_error:.3f} g")
print(f"Observed z: {z_observed:.3f}")
print(f"Two-sided p-value: {p_two_sided:.6f}")
print(f"Right-sided p-value: {p_right:.6f}")
print(f"Left-sided p-value: {p_left:.6f}")
print(f"95% CI: [{ci_low:.3f}, {ci_high:.3f}] g")
print(f"Reject H0 at 5%: {p_two_sided < alpha}")

assert np.isclose(p_two_sided, 0.01241933065)
assert (mu_0 < ci_low) == (p_two_sided < alpha)

# Experiment: change the observed mean to 502 g.
# How do the p-value and confidence interval change?

Left-sided example and a small normal sample.

Suppose \(X_i\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu,8^2)\), and we wish to test \(H_0:\mu\geq40\) against \(H_1:\mu<40\). For \(n=64\) and \(\bar{x}=38\), we obtain \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=-2\) and \(p=\Phi(-2)\approx0.02275\). We reject at the 5% level because the observed mean is unusually low relative to the boundary null.

Small sample size does not, by itself, invalidate the known-sigma z-test. For example, if \(11,12,13,12\) are independent realizations from a normal population with known \(\sigma=2\), testing \(H_0:\mu=10\) gives \(\bar{x}=12\), \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=2\), and a two-sided p-value of about \(0.04550\). The calculation is exact under the model, even though \(n=4\). But four observations cannot establish that the assumptions are reasonable.

Experiment 42: directional tests and a small sample.

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import norm

# Left-sided normal-mean test using summary statistics.
mu_0 = 40.0
sigma = 8.0
n = 64
sample_mean = 38.0

z_left = (sample_mean - mu_0) / (sigma / np.sqrt(n))
p_left = norm.cdf(z_left)

print(f"Left-sided z: {z_left:.2f}")
print(f"Left-sided p-value: {p_left:.5f}")

# Small sample; exact because normality and known sigma are assumed.
observations = np.array([11.0, 12.0, 13.0, 12.0])
mu_0 = 10.0
sigma = 2.0

z_small = (
    observations.mean() - mu_0
) / (sigma / np.sqrt(len(observations)))
p_small = 2 * norm.sf(abs(z_small))

print(f"Small-sample z: {z_small:.2f}")
print(f"Small-sample two-sided p-value: {p_small:.5f}")

# Experiment: compare p_left and the two-sided p-value
# for the same observed statistic.

Assumptions and their consequences.

  • Independently known \(\sigma\). The standard deviation in the denominator must be justified independently of the current sample. Replacing it with the sample standard deviation \(s\) is a different statistical procedure, discussed in the next section.
  • Independent sampling. Repeated measurements on one subject, clustered observations, and autocorrelated time series may not have variance \(\sigma^2/n\). Sampling without replacement from a finite population may require a finite-population correction or design-based analysis.
  • Normality or a justified approximation. Independent normal observations yield an exact normal sampling distribution. Otherwise, use a CLT approximation only when its conditions and accuracy are plausible.
  • Representative data and meaningful target. Statistical calibration does not repair selection bias, missing-data bias, or an inappropriate reference value \(\mu_0\).

Counterexample: using the wrong population standard deviation. Suppose the observations really are normal with \(\sigma=12\), but a two-sided 5% z-test is performed with \(\sigma_{\mathrm{used}}=8\). Under the true null, the incorrectly standardized statistic has standard deviation \(12/8=1.5\), rather than 1. Its actual rejection probability is \[ 2\left[1-\Phi\!\left(z_{0.975}\frac{8}{12}\right)\right] \approx0.1913. \] Thus a nominal 5% procedure rejects a true null about 19.1% of the time under this misspecification. The reliability of \(\sigma\) is central to the validity of the test.

Experiment 43: type I error when sigma is misspecified.

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
repetitions = 50_000
n = 36
mu_0 = 500.0
sigma_true = 12.0
sigma_wrong = 8.0
alpha = 0.05

# Under H0, simulate independent sample means directly.
means = rng.normal(
    loc=mu_0,
    scale=sigma_true / np.sqrt(n),
    size=repetitions
)

cutoff = norm.isf(alpha / 2)

z_correct = (means - mu_0) / (sigma_true / np.sqrt(n))
z_wrong = (means - mu_0) / (sigma_wrong / np.sqrt(n))

print("Nominal alpha:", alpha)
print("Correct sigma, empirical size:", np.mean(np.abs(z_correct) >= cutoff))
print("Wrong sigma, empirical size:", np.mean(np.abs(z_wrong) >= cutoff))
print(
    "Wrong sigma, theoretical size:",
    2 * norm.sf(cutoff * sigma_wrong / sigma_true)
)

# Experiment: use a sigma that is too large instead.
# Does the test become conservative?

What if the population is not normal?

Consider independent observations from an exponential distribution with mean 1 and variance 1. The population is strongly right-skewed, but \(\sqrt{n}(\overline{X}-1)\) approaches a standard normal distribution as \(n\) increases. This does not make the approximation exact at a finite sample size, and it does not rescue problems such as strong dependence or infinite variance.

Experiment 44: normal approximation for skewed observations. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
repetitions = 20_000

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

for n in (5, 50):
    samples = rng.exponential(
        scale=1.0, size=(repetitions, n)
    )
    standardized_means = np.sqrt(n) * (
        samples.mean(axis=1) - 1.0
    )

    ax.hist(
        standardized_means,
        bins=65,
        density=True,
        alpha=0.4,
        label=f"Sample size n={n}"
    )

x = np.linspace(-4, 6, 600)
ax.plot(x, norm.pdf(x), linewidth=2, label="Standard normal")
ax.set_xlim(-4, 6)
ax.set_xlabel("Standardized sample mean")
ax.set_ylabel("Density")
ax.set_title("Central limit theorem for skewed observations")
ax.legend()

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig(
    "known_sigma_clt_comparison.png",
    dpi=180,
    bbox_inches="tight"
)
plt.show()

# Experiment: try n=2, 10, 100 and compare the shapes.

Repeated-sampling coverage of confidence intervals.

The coverage statement for a 95% interval concerns repeated studies. With \(\mu=100\), \(\sigma=10\), and \(n=25\), different samples produce different intervals. Most of these intervals contain the same fixed population mean, but a small proportion do not.

Experiment 45: coverage of known-sigma confidence intervals. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
mu = 100.0
sigma = 10.0
n = 25
confidence_level = 0.95
repetitions = 20_000

standard_error = sigma / np.sqrt(n)
critical = norm.ppf((1 + confidence_level) / 2)

sample_means = rng.normal(
    mu, standard_error, size=repetitions
)
low = sample_means - critical * standard_error
high = sample_means + critical * standard_error
covers = (low <= mu) & (mu <= high)

print(f"Nominal coverage: {confidence_level:.1%}")
print(f"Empirical coverage: {covers.mean():.2%}")

# Plot the first 40 simulated confidence intervals.
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 7))

for index in range(40):
    color = "C0" if covers[index] else "C3"
    ax.plot([low[index], high[index]], [index, index], color=color)
    ax.plot(sample_means[index], index, marker="o", color=color, markersize=3)

ax.axvline(mu, linestyle="--", color="black", label="True mean")
ax.set_xlabel("Population mean")
ax.set_ylabel("Simulated study")
ax.set_title("Repeated 95% z-confidence intervals")
ax.legend()

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig(
    "known_sigma_confidence_interval_coverage.png",
    dpi=180,
    bbox_inches="tight"
)
plt.show()

# Experiment: try 90% and 99% confidence levels.
# How do the interval widths and coverage change?

Power of a two-sided z-test.

Under a true population mean \(\mu\), the statistic computed relative to \(\mu_0\) has distribution \[ Z\sim N(\delta,1),\qquad \delta=\frac{\sqrt{n}(\mu-\mu_0)}{\sigma}. \] For a two-sided test with cutoff \(c=z_{1-\alpha/2}\), the power is \[ \begin{aligned} \pi(\mu) &=\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(|Z|\geq c)\\ &=\Phi(-c-\delta)+1-\Phi(c-\delta). \end{aligned} \] The formula follows by splitting the rejection region into two tails and standardizing with \(Z-\delta\sim N(0,1)\). At the null boundary, \(\delta=0\) and \(\pi(\mu_0)=\alpha\).

In the package example, if the true mean is 505 g, then \(\delta=2.5\) for \(n=36\). At the 5% significance level, the probability of rejecting \(H_0\) is about \(0.7054\). This power is a property of a proposed sampling design at a specified alternative; it is not the posterior probability that the alternative is true.

To plan for a meaningful difference \(\Delta=|\mu_1-\mu_0|>0\) and target power \(1-\beta\), a useful approximation to the required sample size is \[ n\approx\left( \frac{\sigma(z_{1-\alpha/2}+z_{1-\beta})}{\Delta} \right)^2. \] For \(\sigma=12\), \(\Delta=5\), \(\alpha=0.05\), and 80% target power, this gives \(n\approx45.21\), suggesting 46 observations after rounding upward. Since the approximation neglects the opposite rejection tail, the exact normal-model power should be checked for the final integer \(n\).

Experiment 46: power and sample-size planning.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm

mu_0 = 500.0
mu_true = 505.0
sigma = 12.0
alpha = 0.05
target_power = 0.80


def two_sided_power(n, mu_1, mu_0, sigma, alpha=0.05):
    critical = norm.isf(alpha / 2)
    delta = np.sqrt(n) * (mu_1 - mu_0) / sigma
    return norm.cdf(-critical - delta) + norm.sf(critical - delta)


for n in (9, 36, 46, 100):
    power = two_sided_power(n, mu_true, mu_0, sigma, alpha)
    print(f"n={n:3d}: power={power:.4f}")

approximate_n = (
    sigma * (norm.isf(alpha / 2) + norm.ppf(target_power))
    / abs(mu_true - mu_0)
) ** 2

required_n = next(
    n for n in range(2, 1000)
    if two_sided_power(n, mu_true, mu_0, sigma, alpha) >= target_power
)

print(f"Approximate sample size: {approximate_n:.2f}")
print(f"Smallest integer n with 80% power: {required_n}")

sample_sizes = np.arange(2, 121)
powers = [
    two_sided_power(n, mu_true, mu_0, sigma, alpha)
    for n in sample_sizes
]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.plot(sample_sizes, powers, label="Power to detect a 5 g shift")
ax.axhline(target_power, linestyle="--", label="80% power target")
ax.axvline(required_n, linestyle=":", label=f"Required n={required_n}")
ax.set_xlabel("Sample size")
ax.set_ylabel("Power")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1.02)
ax.set_title("Two-sided z-test: power versus sample size")
ax.legend()
ax.grid(alpha=0.25)

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig(
    "known_sigma_power_sample_size.png",
    dpi=180,
    bbox_inches="tight"
)
plt.show()

# Experiment: double sigma. How many observations are needed?

Application: interpreting the SOGA-Py student-weight example.

The original SOGA-Py material considers a mean-weight comparison using its students dataset and a reference value of 70.8 kg. It is tempting to treat the standard deviation computed from the entire recorded dataset as a known population \(\sigma\). However, unless that value truly comes from a justified, independent population calibration, it remains an estimate or a descriptive property of the recorded dataset. The calculation below illustrates the shape of a z-test; it should not be taken as a validated known-sigma test for all students or European adults.

Experiment 47: illustrative z-calculation with student weights. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import norm

url = (
    "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/"
    "soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
)
students = pd.read_csv(url)

weights = students["weight"].dropna()
subsample = weights.sample(n=14, random_state=8)

mu_0 = 70.8
sigma_from_dataset = weights.std(ddof=0)
sample_mean = subsample.mean()
n = len(subsample)

z_illustrative = (
    (sample_mean - mu_0) / (sigma_from_dataset / np.sqrt(n))
)
p_illustrative = 2 * norm.sf(abs(z_illustrative))

print(f"Recorded-dataset SD: {sigma_from_dataset:.3f}")
print(f"Subsample mean: {sample_mean:.3f}")
print(f"Illustrative z: {z_illustrative:.3f}")
print(f"Illustrative two-sided p-value: {p_illustrative:.4f}")

# This is NOT an independently calibrated known-sigma test.
# Next section: infer a mean when sigma must be estimated.

The next section develops the one-sample t-test, which replaces the unknown population standard deviation with the sample standard deviation. Inference still requires an appropriate population and study design; changing the test statistic does not remove sampling bias.

Reporting a known-sigma mean analysis.

A good report identifies the population, the parameter and reference value, the source of the known standard deviation, the sample size, the observed mean, the estimated effect, the test statistic, the p-value, and a confidence interval. For the hypothetical package study, an appropriate summary is: In 36 independently sampled packages, the mean fill weight was 505 g. Assuming normal fill weights and a separately calibrated standard deviation of 12 g, a two-sided z-test against 500 g gave z = 2.50 and p = 0.0124. The 95% confidence interval for the mean was [501.08, 508.92] g. Practical acceptability of the deviation is a separate decision.

Exercises with solutions.

Exercise 1 (deriving the statistic). Suppose nine independent normal observations have known \(\sigma=4\) and observed mean \(\bar{x}=12\). Test \(H_0:\mu=10\) against \(H_1:\mu\neq10\) at the 5% level. Solution. The standard error is \(4/3\), so \(z=1.5\) and \(p=2[1-\Phi(1.5)]\approx0.1336\). We fail to reject.

Exercise 2 (direction matters). If \(z_{\mathrm{obs}}=-2.1\), find the left-sided, right-sided, and two-sided p-values. Solution. They are \(\Phi(-2.1)\approx0.01786\), \(1-\Phi(-2.1)\approx0.98214\), and \(2\Phi(-2.1)\approx0.03573\), respectively.

Exercise 3 (confidence interval). Suppose \(n=25\), \(\sigma=10\), and \(\bar{x}=52\). Construct a 95% confidence interval and test \(H_0:\mu=50\) against a two-sided alternative. Solution. The standard error is 2, giving an interval \(52\pm1.960(2)\approx[48.08,55.92]\). Since 50 lies inside, the two-sided 5% test does not reject.

Exercise 4 (wrong standard deviation). A two-sided 5% z-test uses \(\sigma=8\) although the true value is 12. Compute its actual type I error probability under a normal null. Solution. It is \(2[1-\Phi(z_{0.975}\cdot8/12)]\approx0.1913\), not 0.05.

Exercise 5 (designing for power). Suppose \(\sigma=12\), \(\Delta=5\), \(\alpha=0.05\), and the target power is 80%. Approximate the required \(n\) and verify it using the exact power function. Solution. The approximation gives \(n\approx45.21\). The smallest integer achieving at least 80% exact power is 46, with power approximately 0.8068.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Chapter 9, especially the treatment of hypothesis testing and the normal-mean model; OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Chapters 5 and 7, for inference and standard errors; and SOGA-Py, Sigma Is Known, for the original applied example. The normal CDF, survival-function, and quantile methods used in the experiments are documented in the SciPy probability-distribution tutorial.

Inference for One Population Mean with Unknown Sigma

Previously, we considered inference for a population mean when the population standard deviation \(\sigma\) was known. In practice, \(\sigma\) is usually unknown and must be estimated from the same sample used to estimate the mean. This leads to the one-sample Student's t-test and the t-confidence interval.

Statistical model and sample variance.

Definition (unknown-sigma normal-mean model).

Suppose \[ X_1,\ldots,X_n\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu,\sigma^2), \qquad n\geq2, \] where both \(\mu\in\mathbb{R}\) and \(\sigma>0\) are unknown. Define the sample mean and sample variance by \[ \overline{X}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i, \qquad S^2=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i-\overline{X})^2. \] The sample standard deviation is \(S=\sqrt{S^2}\).

The denominator \(n-1\) appears because the deviations from the sample mean sum to zero, leaving only \(n-1\) freely varying deviations. Under independent sampling with finite variance, \(S^2\) is an unbiased estimator of \(\sigma^2\), although \(S\) itself is generally not an unbiased estimator of \(\sigma\).

The population standard deviation of the sample mean is still \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), but it is unknown. We estimate this standard error by \[ \widehat{\operatorname{SE}}(\overline X)=\frac{S}{\sqrt n}. \] Replacing \(\sigma\) by \(S\) introduces additional randomness; the standardized quantity is no longer standard normal in a finite normal sample.

Theorem (Student's t-statistic).

Under the unknown-sigma normal model, with \(\nu=n-1\), \[ T=\frac{\overline X-\mu}{S/\sqrt n}\sim t_{\nu}. \] In particular, under \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\), \[ T_0=\frac{\overline X-\mu_0}{S/\sqrt n} \overset{H_0}{\sim} t_{n-1}. \] The distribution is exact for independent normal observations, not merely a large-sample approximation.

Proof. For a normal sample, \[ Z=\frac{\sqrt n(\overline X-\mu)}{\sigma}\sim N(0,1), \qquad V=\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2}\sim\chi^2_{n-1}, \] and \(Z\) and \(V\) are independent. By the definition of Student's t-distribution, \[ \frac{Z}{\sqrt{V/(n-1)}} =\frac{\overline X-\mu}{S/\sqrt n}\sim t_{n-1}. \]

The degrees of freedom are \(\nu=n-1\). The t-distribution is symmetric around zero and resembles the standard normal distribution, but has heavier tails. These tails account for the uncertainty introduced by estimating \(\sigma\). As \(\nu\to\infty\), the t-distribution approaches \(N(0,1)\). For small \(n\), substituting normal critical values for t-critical values can produce confidence intervals that are too narrow.

Experiment 48: comparing normal and t distributions.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import norm, t

x = np.linspace(-5, 5, 600)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

ax.plot(x, norm.pdf(x), linewidth=2, label="Standard normal")
for df in [2, 5, 15, 50]:
    ax.plot(x, t.pdf(x, df=df), label=f"t, df={df}")

print("Two-sided 95% critical values:")
print(f"Normal: {norm.ppf(0.975):.4f}")
for df in [2, 5, 15, 50]:
    print(f"t, df={df}: {t.ppf(0.975, df):.4f}")

ax.set_xlabel("Standardized statistic")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability density")
ax.set_title("Student's t-distribution and the normal distribution")
ax.legend()
ax.grid(alpha=0.2)
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("unknown_sigma_t_vs_normal.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: compare critical values for n=5, n=20, and n=100.

Two-sided and one-sided t-tests.

Let \(F_\nu\) denote the CDF of the t-distribution with \(\nu=n-1\) degrees of freedom and let \(t_{\nu,q}=F_\nu^{-1}(q)\). The observed statistic is \[ t_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{\bar x-\mu_0}{s/\sqrt n}. \] At a prespecified significance level \(\alpha\), the usual tests are:

Alternative Rejection condition p-value
\(H_1:\mu\neq\mu_0\) \(|t_{\mathrm{obs}}|\geq t_{\nu,1-\alpha/2}\) \(2[1-F_\nu(|t_{\mathrm{obs}}|)]\)
\(H_1:\mu>\mu_0\) \(t_{\mathrm{obs}}\geq t_{\nu,1-\alpha}\) \(1-F_\nu(t_{\mathrm{obs}})\)
\(H_1:\mu<\mu_0\) \(t_{\mathrm{obs}}\leq t_{\nu,\alpha}\) \(F_\nu(t_{\mathrm{obs}})\)

For one-sided tests, the corresponding composite nulls are \(H_0:\mu\leq\mu_0\) and \(H_0:\mu\geq\mu_0\), respectively. Under the normal model, the boundary \(\mu=\mu_0\) is used to calibrate the tests. In Python, use the t-distribution's sf for the upper tail to avoid unnecessary subtraction of probabilities close to 1.

Theorem (exact t-confidence interval).

Under the independent normal model, a two-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval for \(\mu\) is \[ C(X)=\left[ \overline X-t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\frac{S}{\sqrt n},\, \overline X+t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\frac{S}{\sqrt n} \right]. \] It has exact coverage \(\mathbb P_{\mu,\sigma}(\mu\in C(X))=1-\alpha\) for every \(\mu\in\mathbb R\) and \(\sigma>0\).

Proof. The t-statistic \((\overline X-\mu)/(S/\sqrt n)\) has a \(t_{n-1}\) distribution. The probability that it lies between \(-t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\) and \(t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\) equals \(1-\alpha\). Rearranging these inequalities yields the interval.

The margin of error is \(t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}s/\sqrt n\). A 95% confidence interval refers to the repeated-sampling coverage of this procedure: it does not mean that 95% of individual observations lie inside the interval, nor that the fixed parameter has a 95% probability of lying in the particular interval computed from the observed sample.

Property (test and confidence-interval duality).

For the same normal model and confidence level, the two-sided level- \(\alpha\) t-test of \(H_0:\mu=\mu_0\) rejects exactly when \(\mu_0\) lies outside the corresponding two-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) t-confidence interval.

Proof. The inequality \(|t_{\mathrm{obs}}|>t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}\) is equivalent to \(|\bar x-\mu_0|>t_{n-1,1-\alpha/2}s/\sqrt n\), which says that \(\mu_0\) lies outside the interval. With the usual convention of rejecting at equality, the endpoints are handled consistently by treating the interval's acceptance condition as strict or noting that exact equality has probability zero under the model.

Worked example: a mean compared with a reference value.

Suppose \(n=16\) independent observations are modeled as normal with unknown standard deviation. The sample mean is \(\bar x=52.4\) and the sample standard deviation is \(s=8\). We test \(H_0:\mu=50\) against \(H_1:\mu\neq50\) at \(\alpha=0.05\). The estimated standard error and observed statistic are \[ \widehat{\operatorname{SE}}=\frac8{\sqrt{16}}=2, \qquad t_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{52.4-50}{2}=1.2, \qquad \nu=15. \] The two-sided p-value is \(2[1-F_{15}(1.2)]\), approximately \(0.249\). We fail to reject \(H_0\) at level 0.05. This is not evidence that the mean equals 50; the data remain compatible with a range of population means.

The 95% t-critical value is \(t_{15,0.975}\approx2.131\), giving \[ 52.4\pm2.131(2)\approx[48.14,56.66]. \] Since 50 lies in this interval, the conclusion agrees with the two-sided hypothesis test.

Experiment 49: t-test and interval from summary statistics. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import t

n, sample_mean, sample_sd = 16, 52.4, 8.0
mu_0, alpha = 50.0, 0.05

df = n - 1
se = sample_sd / np.sqrt(n)
t_observed = (sample_mean - mu_0) / se
p_two_sided = 2 * t.sf(abs(t_observed), df=df)
critical = t.ppf(1 - alpha / 2, df=df)
ci = (sample_mean - critical * se, sample_mean + critical * se)

print(f"t-statistic: {t_observed:.4f}")
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {df}")
print(f"Two-sided p-value: {p_two_sided:.4f}")
print(f"95% confidence interval: ({ci[0]:.3f}, {ci[1]:.3f})")
print("Reject H0:", p_two_sided <= alpha)
assert (ci[0] <= mu_0 <= ci[1]) == (p_two_sided > alpha)

# Experiment: change the sample size while keeping the
# observed mean and standard deviation fixed.

Using raw observations instead of summary statistics.

SciPy's scipy.stats.ttest_1samp computes the one-sample t-statistic and p-value directly from observations. The sample standard deviation uses \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. We can also compare the result with a manually calculated interval and, in recent SciPy versions, use the result object's confidence_interval method.

Experiment 50: a one-sample t-test with raw data.

import numpy as np
