For continuous differences, the sign test examines whether positive and negative differences are equally likely (equivalently, whether zero is a median under the usual continuity conditions). It ignores magnitudes. The Wilcoxon signed-rank test uses ranks of absolute differences and is especially appropriate for assessing a distribution of paired differences that is symmetric about zero under its null hypothesis. Neither test, in general, is simply a test that the mean difference equals zero.

Experiment 75: sign and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests.

import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

# Most changes are positive, but one negative change is large.
d = np.array([1.2, 2.1, 0.8, 1.7, 1.5, 2.6,
              0.9, 1.8, 2.2, -25.0])

paired_t = stats.ttest_1samp(d, popmean=0)

# For the sign test, exclude exact zeros and count positive signs.
nonzero = d[d != 0]
positive = np.count_nonzero(nonzero > 0)
sign_test = stats.binomtest(
    positive, n=len(nonzero), p=0.5, alternative="two-sided"
)

# Supply the already calculated differences directly.
wilcoxon = stats.wilcoxon(d, alternative="two-sided")

print(f"Mean change: {np.mean(d):.3f}")
print(f"Median change: {np.median(d):.3f}")
print(f"Paired t-test p-value: {paired_t.pvalue:.5f}")
print(f"Sign-test p-value: {sign_test.pvalue:.5f}")
print(f"Wilcoxon signed-rank p-value: {wilcoxon.pvalue:.5f}")

# Experiment: replace -25 by -2.5.
# How do the three methods react to the changed magnitude?

The tests can disagree because they respond differently to magnitude, ranks, and signs. This example demonstrates their sensitivity, not a recommendation to select whichever method gives the smallest p-value. Select the parameter or scientific claim of interest first and choose a suitable procedure based on the study design and its assumptions.

Practical considerations when working with paired data.

The number of complete pairs determines the sample size for paired inference. Always document the direction of subtraction; retain matching identifiers; examine missing pairs and extreme differences; and avoid treating repeated observations from the same unit as independent pairs. For intervention studies, distinguish the existence of a change from evidence that the intervention caused that change.

Try it yourself. (1) Using Experiment 69, calculate the mean paired difference and 95% confidence interval by hand, and verify the results in SciPy. (2) Derive the covariance formula for \(\operatorname{Var}(Y-X)\) and explain when pairing improves precision. (3) In Experiment 72, change the simulated within-pair correlation from positive to negative and investigate the two rejection rates. (4) In Experiment 73, determine the smallest number of pairs that achieves at least 80% theoretical power for the specified one-sided test. (5) Explain why a significant before–after change in one group and an insignificant change in another group do not, by themselves, establish a significant difference between the groups.

Further reading. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Section 7.2, pp. 262–265, develops inference based on paired observations; Exercises 7.15 and following offer paired-data practice. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 9.5, pp. 580–581, gives a paired t-test example using matched injury measurements. The tutorial example follows the topic and dataset used in SOGA-Py; the additional comparison of changes and cautions about causal interpretation expand the original discussion.

Inferences for One Population Standard Deviation

In the preceding sections, we developed tests and confidence intervals for population means. We now investigate the variability of a population. Such questions arise, for example, when checking the precision of a measurement instrument, the consistency of a manufacturing process, or the variability of service response times.

For a random variable with finite variance, the population variance is \(\sigma^2=\mathbb{E}[(X-\mu)^2]\) and the population standard deviation is \(\sigma=\sqrt{\sigma^2}\). Since \(\sigma\geq0\), a hypothesis concerning \(\sigma\) can equivalently be written in terms of \(\sigma^2\). We will calculate inference for the variance and then take square roots to obtain results for the standard deviation.

Definition (sample variance and sample standard deviation).

For a sample \(X_1,\ldots,X_n\) with \(n\geq2\), write \[ \overline{X}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_i, \qquad S^2=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i-\overline{X})^2, \qquad S=\sqrt{S^2}. \] The denominator \(n-1\) is used because the sample mean has been estimated from the same observations. The resulting \(S^2\) is an unbiased estimator of \(\sigma^2\) when the observations are identically distributed, independent, and have finite variance. In general, \(S\) is not an unbiased estimator of \(\sigma\).

Definition (chi-square distribution).

If \(Z_1,\ldots,Z_\nu\) are independent standard normal random variables, then \[ Y=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu}Z_j^2 \] has a chi-square distribution with \(\nu\) degrees of freedom, written \(Y\sim\chi^2_\nu\). Its mean is \(\nu\) and its variance is \(2\nu\).

Theorem (sampling distribution of the normal sample variance).

Suppose \[ X_1,\ldots,X_n\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu,\sigma^2), \qquad n\geq2,\quad \sigma>0. \] Then \[ V=\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2} =\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_i-\overline{X})^2}{\sigma^2} \sim\chi^2_{n-1}. \] This result is exact for independent normal observations, regardless of the unknown population mean \(\mu\).

Proof (outline). Standardize the observations to obtain \(n\) independent standard normal variables. An orthogonal change of coordinates separates the sample-mean component from the \(n-1\) orthogonal residual components. The sum of squared residual components is \((n-1)S^2/\sigma^2\). By the definition of a chi-square distribution, this sum has \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. This is a special case of Cochran's theorem.

The factor \(n-1\) is essential: estimating the sample mean removes one degree of freedom. A commonly encountered alternative variance estimator uses the divisor \(n\), but that estimator must not be substituted for \(S^2\) in this chi-square formula without adjusting the scaling.

Experiment 76: sampling distribution of the sample variance. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

n = 12
mu = 100.0
sigma = 5.0
repetitions = 20_000

samples = rng.normal(mu, sigma, size=(repetitions, n))
sample_variances = np.var(samples, axis=1, ddof=1)
pivots = (n - 1) * sample_variances / sigma**2

df = n - 1
print(f"Empirical pivot mean: {pivots.mean():.3f}")
print(f"Theoretical mean:     {df}")
print(f"Empirical pivot variance: {pivots.var(ddof=1):.3f}")
print(f"Theoretical variance:     {2 * df}")

x = np.linspace(0, chi2.ppf(0.999, df), 400)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.hist(pivots, bins=55, density=True, alpha=0.5,
        label="Simulated chi-square pivots")
ax.plot(x, chi2.pdf(x, df), linewidth=2,
        label=f"Chi-square distribution, df={df}")
ax.set_xlabel("(n - 1) S² / sigma²")
ax.set_ylabel("Density")
ax.set_title("Sampling distribution of the normal sample variance")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("one_variance_chi_square_sampling.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: increase n and compare the simulated distribution.

Definition (one-sample chi-square variance test).

Under independent normal sampling, to test \(H_0:\sigma^2=\sigma_0^2\), where \(\sigma_0>0\) is fixed in advance, use the statistic \[ V_0=\frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma_0^2}. \] Under \(H_0\), \(V_0\sim\chi^2_{n-1}\).

A small value of \(V_0\) indicates less variability than specified by the null hypothesis; a large value indicates more variability. Let \(\nu=n-1\) and write \(c_p=F^{-1}_{\chi^2_\nu}(p)\) for the \(p\)-quantile of the chi-square distribution. For a test at significance level \(\alpha\), the rejection regions and p-values are:

Alternative Rejection region p-value
\(H_1:\sigma<\sigma_0\) \(V_0<c_\alpha\) \(F_{\chi^2_\nu}(v_0)\)
\(H_1:\sigma>\sigma_0\) \(V_0>c_{1-\alpha}\) \(1-F_{\chi^2_\nu}(v_0)\)
\(H_1:\sigma\neq\sigma_0\) \(V_0<c_{\alpha/2}\) or \(V_0>c_{1-\alpha/2}\) \(\min\{1,2\min(F_{\chi^2_\nu}(v_0),1-F_{\chi^2_\nu}(v_0))\}\)

Here \(v_0\) is the observed statistic. The two-sided test shown above is the equal-tailed chi-square test. Because the chi-square distribution is asymmetric, different conventions for two-sided testing can yield different p-values; we will consistently use the equal-tailed convention in this section. For numerical accuracy, the upper-tail probability should be calculated with scipy.stats.chi2.sf, rather than by directly subtracting the CDF from 1.

For a one-sided formulation such as \(H_0:\sigma\geq\sigma_0\) against \(H_1:\sigma<\sigma_0\), the boundary \(\sigma=\sigma_0\) controls the rejection probability under the composite null. The same applies, with the inequalities reversed, to a right-sided alternative.

Worked example: variability of a measurement process.

Suppose a measurement process is expected to have population standard deviation \(\sigma_0=4\) units. We observe \(n=20\) independent measurements from an approximately normal population and obtain \(s=6.1\) units. We want to test \[ H_0:\sigma=4\qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\sigma>4. \] The observed test statistic is \[ v_0=\frac{19(6.1)^2}{4^2}=44.186875. \] Under the null, it is compared with a chi-square distribution having 19 degrees of freedom. The right-tailed p-value is approximately \(0.00089\), providing strong evidence of greater variability under the assumed model. This does not identify the cause of the increased variation.

Experiment 77: calculating variance-test p-values and critical values. 

from scipy.stats import chi2

n = 20
sample_sd = 6.1
sigma_0 = 4.0
alpha = 0.05
df = n - 1

statistic = df * sample_sd**2 / sigma_0**2
p_left = chi2.cdf(statistic, df)
p_right = chi2.sf(statistic, df)
p_two_sided = min(1.0, 2 * min(p_left, p_right))

lower_critical = chi2.ppf(alpha / 2, df)
upper_critical = chi2.isf(alpha / 2, df)

print(f"Statistic: {statistic:.5f}")
print(f"Left-tailed p-value:  {p_left:.6f}")
print(f"Right-tailed p-value: {p_right:.6f}")
print(f"Two-sided p-value:   {p_two_sided:.6f}")
print(f"Two-sided critical values: {lower_critical:.3f}, "
      f"{upper_critical:.3f}")
print("Reject right-sided H0:", p_right < alpha)

# Experiment: change the sample SD while keeping n fixed.

Confidence intervals for the population variance and standard deviation.

The same chi-square sampling result produces an exact confidence interval. Under the normal model, \[ \mathbb{P}\left( c_{\alpha/2}\leq \frac{(n-1)S^2}{\sigma^2} \leq c_{1-\alpha/2} \right)=1-\alpha. \] Solving both inequalities for the positive parameter \(\sigma^2\) gives the following result.

Theorem (exact chi-square confidence intervals).

For independent \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\) observations, a \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval for the population variance is \[ \boxed{\left[ \frac{(n-1)s^2}{c_{1-\alpha/2}}, \frac{(n-1)s^2}{c_{\alpha/2}} \right]}. \] Taking the square root of each positive endpoint gives the corresponding confidence interval for the population standard deviation: \[ \boxed{\left[ s\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{c_{1-\alpha/2}}}, s\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{c_{\alpha/2}}} \right]}. \] Here \(s\) is the observed sample standard deviation and \(c_p=\chi^2_{\nu,p}\), with \(\nu=n-1\), denotes the lower-tail \(p\)-quantile of the chi-square distribution.

Proof. The probability statement preceding the theorem is exact because the standardized sample variance has the \(\chi^2_{n-1}\) distribution. The variable \(\sigma^2\) is positive, so dividing the inequalities by positive terms and reversing the fractions yields the displayed interval. Taking square roots gives the result for \(\sigma\).

Notice that the upper chi-square quantile appears in the lower confidence bound and the lower chi-square quantile in the upper bound. Both bounds also depend on the observed sample standard deviation \(s\); they cannot be calculated from the sample size alone.

As in previous sections, 95% confidence refers to repeated-sampling coverage under the statistical model: 95% of intervals obtained by repeatedly applying the procedure would contain the fixed population parameter. It is not the posterior probability that \(\sigma\) belongs to this particular observed interval.

The interval is generally asymmetric around \(s\), reflecting the asymmetry of the chi-square distribution. A two-sided equal-tailed test of \(H_0:\sigma=\sigma_0\) at level \(\alpha\) rejects precisely when the corresponding \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval excludes \(\sigma_0\).

Experiment 78: exact confidence intervals for variance and standard deviation. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2

n = 20
s = 6.1
alpha = 0.05
df = n - 1

q_lower = chi2.ppf(alpha / 2, df)
q_upper = chi2.ppf(1 - alpha / 2, df)

variance_ci = (
    df * s**2 / q_upper,
    df * s**2 / q_lower
)
sd_ci = tuple(np.sqrt(variance_ci))

print(f"Sample variance: {s**2:.4f}")
print(f"95% CI for variance: {variance_ci}")
print(f"95% CI for SD:       {sd_ci}")

# Verify the connection with the two-sided test.
sigma_0 = 4.0
statistic = df * s**2 / sigma_0**2
p_value = min(1.0, 2 * min(
    chi2.cdf(statistic, df),
    chi2.sf(statistic, df)
))
excluded = not (sd_ci[0] <= sigma_0 <= sd_ci[1])

print(f"Two-sided p-value: {p_value:.6f}")
print("Null excluded by interval:", excluded)
assert excluded == (p_value < alpha)

# Experiment: compare 90%, 95%, and 99% intervals.

Why the normality assumption is unusually important here.

The chi-square test and intervals above are exact only under the independent normal sampling model. Unlike many large-sample methods for means, the chi-square variance procedure can remain badly miscalibrated for nonnormal data, even at moderately large sample sizes. Heavy tails, skewness, and outliers can strongly affect the sample variance because deviations are squared.

A diagnostic histogram and a normal Q-Q plot can help identify pronounced skewness, unusually heavy tails, or extreme observations. However, a visually straight Q-Q plot does not prove normality, especially with a small sample. A formal normality test does not establish normality merely because it fails to reject it.

Experiment 79: confidence-interval coverage and sensitivity to nonnormality. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

n = 30
repetitions = 10_000
sigma = 10.0
alpha = 0.05
df = n - 1

# Both populations have true SD = 10, but different shapes.
normal = rng.normal(0, sigma, size=(repetitions, n))
skewed = rng.exponential(scale=sigma, size=(repetitions, n))

q_lower = chi2.ppf(alpha / 2, df)
q_upper = chi2.isf(alpha / 2, df)

coverages = {}
rejection_rates = {}

for name, samples in [("Normal", normal), ("Exponential", skewed)]:
    s2 = np.var(samples, axis=1, ddof=1)
    lower = df * s2 / q_upper
    upper = df * s2 / q_lower
    coverage = np.mean((lower <= sigma**2) &
                       (sigma**2 <= upper))

    pivot_under_null = df * s2 / sigma**2
    rejects = ((pivot_under_null < q_lower) |
               (pivot_under_null > q_upper))

    coverages[name] = coverage
    rejection_rates[name] = rejects.mean()
    print(f"{name}: CI coverage = {coverage:.3%}, "
          f"type I error = {rejects.mean():.3%}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.7))
ax.bar(list(coverages), list(coverages.values()))
ax.axhline(0.95, linestyle="--", label="Nominal 95% coverage")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1)
ax.set_ylabel("Empirical confidence-interval coverage")
ax.set_title("Chi-square intervals: normal vs. skewed data")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("one_variance_coverage_nonnormal.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: repeat for n=10 and n=200.

Experiment 80: normal Q-Q plots for different distribution shapes. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)

normal_sample = rng.normal(loc=10, scale=2, size=80)
skewed_sample = rng.exponential(scale=2, size=80)

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10.5, 4.5))

for ax, (name, sample) in zip(axes, [
    ("Normal sample", normal_sample),
    ("Exponential sample", skewed_sample)
]):
    stats.probplot(sample, dist="norm", plot=ax)
    ax.set_title(name)
    ax.grid(alpha=0.2)

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("one_variance_normality_qq.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: repeat with a smaller sample of 15 observations.
# Can a small sample hide departures from normality?

When normality is doubtful, a different method may be needed. For example, a bootstrap confidence interval for the sample standard deviation can be useful in some settings, but it is not automatically reliable for very small samples, extreme heavy tails, or dependent observations. In other applications, robust scale summaries such as the interquartile range or median absolute deviation are more appropriate than the standard deviation itself. These statistics describe different population quantities and should not be treated as interchangeable.

Power of the variance test under normal sampling.

The power depends on the true value of \(\sigma\) as well as the sample size. Under a true standard deviation \(\sigma\), the null-based statistic can be written as \[ V_0=\frac{\sigma^2}{\sigma_0^2}Y, \qquad Y\sim\chi^2_{n-1}. \] Thus, for the equal-tailed, two-sided test with critical values \(a=c_{\alpha/2}\) and \(b=c_{1-\alpha/2}\), the power is \[ \begin{aligned} \pi(\sigma) &=\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}(V_0<a) +\mathbb{P}_{\sigma}(V_0>b)\\ &=F_{\chi^2_{n-1}}\!\left(a\frac{\sigma_0^2}{\sigma^2}\right) +1-F_{\chi^2_{n-1}}\!\left(b\frac{\sigma_0^2}{\sigma^2}\right). \end{aligned} \]

At \(\sigma=\sigma_0\), the rejection probability is exactly \(\alpha\). For a specified proportional change in variability, increasing the sample size generally makes the test more powerful. A larger sample also tends to give a narrower confidence interval for \(\sigma\). Neither property removes the need to check the normality and sampling assumptions.

Experiment 81: power and sample size for the variance test. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

alpha = 0.05
sample_sizes = [10, 30, 80]
sd_ratios = np.linspace(0.5, 1.7, 300)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

for n in sample_sizes:
    df = n - 1
    lower_critical = chi2.ppf(alpha / 2, df)
    upper_critical = chi2.isf(alpha / 2, df)

    # Each ratio is sigma_true / sigma_null.
    powers = (
        chi2.cdf(lower_critical / sd_ratios**2, df)
        + chi2.sf(upper_critical / sd_ratios**2, df)
    )

    ax.plot(sd_ratios, powers, label=f"n={n}")

ax.axvline(1.0, linestyle="--", label="Null SD")
ax.axhline(alpha, linestyle=":", label="Significance level")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1.02)
ax.set_xlabel("True SD / null SD")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability of rejecting H0")
ax.set_title("Power of the two-sided chi-square variance test")
ax.legend()
ax.grid(alpha=0.2)
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("one_variance_power_sample_size.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: compare alpha=0.01 with alpha=0.05.

Application: variation in student heights.

We retain the SOGA-Py students dataset from the original notes, but formulate the hypothesis using a prespecified, hypothetical reference standard deviation. Let \(\sigma\) be the standard deviation of height in the population of female students of interest. Suppose, solely for illustration, that the reference value is \(\sigma_0=8\,\mathrm{cm}\). We test \[ H_0:\sigma=8\,\mathrm{cm} \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\sigma<8\,\mathrm{cm}. \]

In the original example, a standard deviation computed from all students in the dataset was used as though it were a known population parameter. That is not generally justified: it is itself an estimate, and the female-student subgroup overlaps with the full dataset. Treating that estimate as fixed would ignore its sampling uncertainty. A comparison of two estimated population variances calls for a different inferential design, discussed in the next section.

Experiment 82: student-height variability and a prespecified reference. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats

students = pd.read_csv(
    "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
)

female_heights = students.loc[
    students["gender"].astype(str).str.strip().eq("Female"),
    "height"
].dropna()

# Sample from the available female-student records.
sample = female_heights.sample(n=30, random_state=12).to_numpy()

sigma_0 = 8.0  # Hypothetical, fixed benchmark in centimeters.
alpha = 0.05
n = len(sample)
df = n - 1
s = np.std(sample, ddof=1)

statistic = df * s**2 / sigma_0**2
p_value = stats.chi2.cdf(statistic, df)

sd_lower = s * np.sqrt(df / stats.chi2.isf(alpha / 2, df))
sd_upper = s * np.sqrt(df / stats.chi2.ppf(alpha / 2, df))

print(f"Sample size: {n}")
print(f"Sample SD: {s:.3f} cm")
print(f"Chi-square statistic: {statistic:.3f}")
print(f"Left-sided p-value: {p_value:.4f}")
print(f"95% two-sided CI for SD: [{sd_lower:.3f}, {sd_upper:.3f}] cm")
print("Reject the left-sided null:", p_value < alpha)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6.7, 5))
stats.probplot(sample, dist="norm", plot=ax)
ax.set_title("Normal Q-Q plot: sampled female heights")
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("one_variance_students_qq.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Interpretation depends on normality and the sampling design.

The sample in Experiment 82 is drawn from an existing educational dataset. It is not automatically a representative random sample of all female students. Consequently, any inferential conclusion must be restricted to a defensible target population, and the exact chi-square calculations additionally require normality and independence. A Q-Q plot alone cannot validate those assumptions.

Choosing a measure and an inferential procedure.

Question Relevant quantity Important consideration
How variable are individual measurements? Population SD \(\sigma\) Use SD in the original measurement units.
Is normal-population variability equal to a fixed benchmark? \(\sigma^2\) or \(\sigma\) Chi-square procedure requires independent normal sampling.
Does a sample include unusually extreme values? SD, IQR, MAD, and plots Investigate the data-generating process before discarding values.
Are two population variances different? \(\sigma_1^2/\sigma_2^2\) Requires a two-population method, not a fixed-benchmark test.

Try it yourself. (1) Derive the confidence-interval endpoints for \(\sigma^2\) by solving the chi-square pivot inequalities. (2) In Experiment 77, find the right-sided critical value and determine the decision directly from the statistic. (3) In Experiment 79, explain why normal and exponential samples have the same true SD but give different observed coverage rates. (4) In Experiment 81, find a sample size giving at least 80% power for an increase in SD from \(\sigma_0\) to \(1.5\sigma_0\). (5) Explain why calculating \(\sigma_0\) from a second sample and then treating it as fixed is not generally valid.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 8.2, pp. 469–473, introduces the chi-square distributions; Section 8.3, pp. 473–479, derives the normal sample-variance distribution; and Section 8.5, pp. 485–495 (particularly Example 8.5.8 and Exercise 5), develops confidence intervals through pivotal quantities. For descriptive comparisons of variability, see OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Sections 2.1.4–2.1.6.

Inferences for Two Population Standard Deviations

We now consider inference about the relative variability of two populations. Comparing population means answers whether their centers differ; comparing their variances or standard deviations answers whether observations in one population are more dispersed than in the other. Examples include comparing the precision of two instruments, the consistency of two manufacturing lines, or the variability of measurements in two independent groups.

Let the population variances be \(\sigma_1^2\) and \(\sigma_2^2\), both positive. The principal parameter in this section is the variance ratio \[ R=\frac{\sigma_1^2}{\sigma_2^2}. \] Equality of the population variances corresponds to \(R=1\). Because both standard deviations are positive, testing \(\sigma_1=\sigma_2\) is equivalent to testing \(R=1\). A confidence interval for \(R\) can also be converted into an interval for \(\sigma_1/\sigma_2=\sqrt{R}\).

Definition (F-distribution).

Let \(U\sim\chi^2_{\nu_1}\) and \(V\sim\chi^2_{\nu_2}\) be independent chi-square random variables with positive integer degrees of freedom \(\nu_1\) and \(\nu_2\). Then \[ W=\frac{U/\nu_1}{V/\nu_2} \sim F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}. \] The F-distribution has a numerator degrees-of-freedom parameter \(\nu_1\) and a denominator degrees-of-freedom parameter \(\nu_2\). Its values are positive and its density is generally right-skewed.

Theorem (reciprocal F-distribution).

If \(W\sim F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}\), then \[ \frac{1}{W}\sim F_{\nu_2,\nu_1}. \] The order of the degrees of freedom matters, and it must be reversed when the numerator and denominator are exchanged.

Proof. Taking the reciprocal exchanges the two independent normalized chi-square variables in the definition of \(W\). The resulting numerator has \(\nu_2\) degrees of freedom and the denominator has \(\nu_1\).

Experiment 83: the F-distribution from independent normal samples. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import f

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
n1, n2 = 12, 24
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1
repetitions = 40_000

# The populations have equal variances but different means.
a = rng.normal(10, 3, size=(repetitions, n1))
b = rng.normal(25, 3, size=(repetitions, n2))

variance_ratios = a.var(axis=1, ddof=1) / b.var(axis=1, ddof=1)

print("Empirical P(F > 2):", np.mean(variance_ratios > 2))
print("Theoretical P(F > 2):", f.sf(2, nu1, nu2))

x = np.linspace(0.01, 5, 500)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.hist(variance_ratios, bins=90, range=(0, 5), density=True,
        alpha=0.45, label="Simulated variance ratios")
ax.plot(x, f.pdf(x, nu1, nu2), linewidth=2.2,
        label=f"F distribution ({nu1}, {nu2})")
ax.set_xlabel("Ratio of sample variances")
ax.set_ylabel("Density")
ax.set_title("Sampling distribution under equal population variances")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_f_distribution.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: swap n1 and n2. How does the distribution change?

Theorem (sampling distribution of a variance ratio).

Suppose the two samples are independent and \[ X_1,\ldots,X_{n_1}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu_1,\sigma_1^2), \qquad Y_1,\ldots,Y_{n_2}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(\mu_2,\sigma_2^2), \] with \(n_1,n_2\geq2\), \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2>0\). Let \(S_1^2\) and \(S_2^2\) denote the usual sample variances, computed with denominators \(n_1-1\) and \(n_2-1\). Then \[ \frac{S_1^2/\sigma_1^2}{S_2^2/\sigma_2^2} =\frac{S_1^2/S_2^2}{\sigma_1^2/\sigma_2^2} \sim F_{n_1-1,n_2-1}. \]

Proof. The normal-sample variance theorem gives \[ \frac{(n_1-1)S_1^2}{\sigma_1^2}\sim\chi^2_{n_1-1}, \qquad \frac{(n_2-1)S_2^2}{\sigma_2^2}\sim\chi^2_{n_2-1}. \] The samples are independent, so the two chi-square variables are independent. Dividing each by its degrees of freedom and taking their ratio gives the claimed F-distribution.

The population means \(\mu_1\) and \(\mu_2\) need not be equal. The theorem concerns the variances, not the difference between means. Crucially, the exact result relies on two independent random samples from normal populations. A Q-Q plot can reveal serious departures from normality, but cannot establish normality as a mathematical fact.

Definition (two-sample F-test statistic).

To test \(H_0:R=R_0\), for a specified \(R_0>0\), use \[ F_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{s_1^2/s_2^2}{R_0}, \qquad \nu_1=n_1-1,\quad\nu_2=n_2-1. \] Under the equality null and the normal-sampling assumptions, the corresponding random statistic has the \(F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}\) distribution. For equality of population variances, \(R_0=1\) and \(F_{\mathrm{obs}}=s_1^2/s_2^2\).

Write \(G\) for the CDF of \(F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}\). The p-value depends on the alternative hypothesis: \[ \begin{aligned} H_1:R>R_0 &:\quad p_{\mathrm{right}}=1-G(F_{\mathrm{obs}}),\\ H_1:R<R_0 &:\quad p_{\mathrm{left}}=G(F_{\mathrm{obs}}),\\ H_1:R\neq R_0 &:\quad p_{\mathrm{two}}=2\min\{G(F_{\mathrm{obs}}),1-G(F_{\mathrm{obs}})\}. \end{aligned} \] For numerical work, the survival function computes \(1-G(x)\) directly. The two-sided expression corresponds to the standard equal-tailed F-test; it is not obtained by using just \(G(F_{\mathrm{obs}})\), and alternative definitions of two-sided extremeness can give different tests.

At significance level \(\alpha\), the equal-tailed two-sided test rejects precisely when \[ F_{\mathrm{obs}}<F_{\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2} \quad\text{or}\quad F_{\mathrm{obs}}>F_{1-\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}, \] where \(F_{q;\nu_1,\nu_2}\) denotes the \(q\)-quantile of that F-distribution. For a right-sided test, reject when \(F_{\mathrm{obs}}>F_{1-\alpha;\nu_1,\nu_2}\); for a left-sided test, reject when \(F_{\mathrm{obs}}<F_{\alpha;\nu_1,\nu_2}\).

Worked example: comparing measurement variability.

Suppose independent normal samples give \(n_1=14\), \(s_1=8.5\), \(n_2=19\), and \(s_2=4.2\), in the same measurement units. We test \(H_0:\sigma_1^2=\sigma_2^2\) against \(H_1:\sigma_1^2\neq\sigma_2^2\). The observed statistic is \[ F_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{8.5^2}{4.2^2}\approx4.0958, \qquad (\nu_1,\nu_2)=(13,18). \] Its equal-tailed two-sided p-value is approximately \(0.00658\). Thus, at the 5% level, the data provide evidence that the population variances differ, provided the test assumptions are appropriate.

Experiment 84: F-tests from summary statistics.

from scipy.stats import f

n1, n2 = 14, 19
s1, s2 = 8.5, 4.2
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1

f_obs = s1**2 / s2**2
left_tail = f.cdf(f_obs, nu1, nu2)
right_tail = f.sf(f_obs, nu1, nu2)
two_sided_p = min(1.0, 2 * min(left_tail, right_tail))

alpha = 0.05
lower_critical = f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
upper_critical = f.isf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)

print(f"F statistic: {f_obs:.5f}")
print(f"Left-sided p-value: {left_tail:.5f}")
print(f"Right-sided p-value: {right_tail:.5f}")
print(f"Two-sided p-value: {two_sided_p:.5f}")
print(f"Rejection limits: {lower_critical:.4f}, {upper_critical:.4f}")
print("Reject H0:", two_sided_p < alpha)

# Experiment: reverse the groups and exchange the degrees
# of freedom. Does the two-sided decision change?

Theorem (confidence interval for a variance ratio).

Under the independent normal-sampling assumptions, let \(r=s_1^2/s_2^2\) and \(\nu_i=n_i-1\). An exact equal-tailed \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval for \(R=\sigma_1^2/\sigma_2^2\) is \[ \boxed{ \left[ \frac{r}{F_{1-\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}},\; \frac{r}{F_{\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}} \right].} \] Taking the square roots of both endpoints gives the corresponding confidence interval for the standard-deviation ratio \(\sigma_1/\sigma_2\).

Proof. By the preceding theorem, \(W=(S_1^2/S_2^2)/R\sim F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}\). Hence \[ \mathbb{P}\left( F_{\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}\leq W \leq F_{1-\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2} \right)=1-\alpha. \] Substituting \(W=(S_1^2/S_2^2)/R\) and solving the two inequalities for \(R>0\) gives the stated interval.

For the worked example, the 95% confidence interval for the variance ratio is approximately \([1.50,12.22]\), while the interval for the standard-deviation ratio is approximately \([1.22,3.50]\). Since neither interval includes 1, its corresponding two-sided equal-tailed test rejects equality at level 0.05. The width of these intervals shows that a significant test does not necessarily yield a precise estimate of the ratio.

Experiment 85: confidence intervals and reciprocal consistency. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import f

n1, n2 = 14, 19
s1, s2 = 8.5, 4.2
alpha = 0.05
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1
ratio = s1**2 / s2**2

variance_ci = (
    ratio / f.isf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2),
    ratio / f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
)
sd_ci = tuple(np.sqrt(variance_ci))

print("Variance ratio:", round(ratio, 4))
print("95% CI for variance ratio:", variance_ci)
print("95% CI for SD ratio:", sd_ci)

# Swapping groups reverses the ratio and its interval.
reverse_ratio = 1 / ratio
reverse_ci = (
    reverse_ratio / f.isf(alpha / 2, nu2, nu1),
    reverse_ratio / f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu2, nu1)
)

assert np.allclose(reverse_ci, (1 / variance_ci[1],
                                1 / variance_ci[0]))
print("CI after swapping groups:", reverse_ci)

# Experiment: increase both sample sizes while holding
# the sample SDs fixed. How does interval width change?

Why normality matters for the F-test.

The F-test assumes independent observations within and between groups and normally distributed populations. Its exact reference distribution depends on both normal-sample variance pivots having chi-square distributions. For skewed or heavy-tailed populations, the distributions of the sample variances can differ substantially from those chi-square results, so a nominal 5% F-test may reject a true null much more or much less often than 5% of the time.

Inspecting histograms, box plots, and Q-Q plots is useful, particularly for detecting pronounced skewness or extreme observations. Such plots cannot prove normality. A failure to reject a normality test is not proof of normality either, especially with small samples.

Experiment 86: type I error under normal and skewed sampling. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import f, levene

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
repetitions = 4_000
n1, n2 = 20, 30
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1
alpha = 0.05

rejection_rates = {}

for name in ["Normal", "Lognormal"]:
    if name == "Normal":
        a = rng.normal(size=(repetitions, n1))
        b = rng.normal(size=(repetitions, n2))
    else:
        # Same lognormal distribution in both groups:
        # population variances are therefore equal.
        a = rng.lognormal(size=(repetitions, n1))
        b = rng.lognormal(size=(repetitions, n2))

    ratios = a.var(axis=1, ddof=1) / b.var(axis=1, ddof=1)
    p_f = np.minimum(
        1.0, 2 * np.minimum(f.cdf(ratios, nu1, nu2),
                            f.sf(ratios, nu1, nu2))
    )
    p_bf = levene(a, b, center="median", axis=1).pvalue
    rejection_rates[name] = (np.mean(p_f < alpha),
                             np.mean(p_bf < alpha))

for name, (rate_f, rate_bf) in rejection_rates.items():
    print(f"{name}: F-test={rate_f:.3f}, Brown-Forsythe={rate_bf:.3f}")

names = list(rejection_rates)
values = np.array(list(rejection_rates.values()))
x = np.arange(len(names))
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.bar(x - 0.18, values[:, 0], width=0.36, label="F-test")
ax.bar(x + 0.18, values[:, 1], width=0.36,
       label="Brown-Forsythe")
ax.axhline(alpha, linestyle="--", label="Nominal 5% level")
ax.set_xticks(x, names)
ax.set_ylabel("Proportion of false rejections")
ax.set_title("Equal variances: normal versus skewed populations")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_type1_nonnormal.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: use heavy-tailed Student t observations
# instead of lognormal observations.

Experiment 87: confidence-interval coverage under nonnormality. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import f

rng = np.random.default_rng(2027)
repetitions = 5_000
n1, n2 = 24, 36
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1
alpha = 0.05
true_ratio = 4.0  # Population SDs are 2 and 1.

coverage = {}

for name in ["Normal", "Lognormal"]:
    if name == "Normal":
        a = rng.normal(scale=2, size=(repetitions, n1))
        b = rng.normal(scale=1, size=(repetitions, n2))
    else:
        # Standardize lognormal samples to known population SD.
        shape = 0.9
        population_sd = np.sqrt(
            (np.exp(shape**2) - 1) * np.exp(shape**2)
        )
        a = 2 * rng.lognormal(sigma=shape,
                             size=(repetitions, n1)) / population_sd
        b = rng.lognormal(sigma=shape,
                          size=(repetitions, n2)) / population_sd

    ratios = a.var(axis=1, ddof=1) / b.var(axis=1, ddof=1)
    lower = ratios / f.isf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
    upper = ratios / f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
    coverage[name] = np.mean((lower <= true_ratio) &
                             (true_ratio <= upper))

print("Target confidence level: 0.95")
for name, rate in coverage.items():
    print(f"{name} coverage: {rate:.3f}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7.5, 4.5))
ax.bar(list(coverage), list(coverage.values()))
ax.axhline(0.95, linestyle="--", label="Nominal 95%")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1)
ax.set_ylabel("Observed interval coverage")
ax.set_title("F confidence-interval coverage")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_ci_coverage.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: increase sample size. Compare the coverage
# when the distribution is not normal.

Alternatives: Bartlett's test, Levene's test, and Fligner–Killeen.

When an exact normal-theory F-test is not justified, tests based on deviations from group centers provide useful alternatives. In particular, Levene's test constructs a comparison using absolute deviations from a center within each group. Choosing the group median produces the Brown–Forsythe variant, which is less sensitive to nonnormality and outliers than the classical F-test in many settings. The Fligner–Killeen test uses ranks of scaled absolute deviations and can also be useful for nonnormal samples.

Bartlett's test is another test for equality of population variances and can accommodate more than two groups. Like the classical F-test, it is sensitive to departures from normality, so it is not a general-purpose robust substitute. These tests report evidence against equal variability; they do not by themselves provide the exact F-based variance-ratio confidence interval derived above.

Alternative tests have their own assumptions and finite-sample behavior. They are not guaranteed to control type I error perfectly under every skewed, heavy-tailed, or unequal-shape distribution. Equality of variances also does not imply equality of distributions or equality of means.

Experiment 88: comparing variance tests and diagnosing outliers. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import bartlett, f, fligner, levene

rng = np.random.default_rng(2027)
a = rng.normal(loc=0, scale=1, size=40)
b = rng.normal(loc=0, scale=1, size=40)
b_with_outlier = b.copy()
b_with_outlier[-1] = 15.0

def report(group1, group2):
    n1, n2 = len(group1), len(group2)
    observed = np.var(group1, ddof=1) / np.var(group2, ddof=1)
    p_f = min(1.0, 2 * min(f.cdf(observed, n1 - 1, n2 - 1),
                           f.sf(observed, n1 - 1, n2 - 1)))
    print(f"SDs: {np.std(group1, ddof=1):.3f}, "
          f"{np.std(group2, ddof=1):.3f}")
    print(f"F-test:        {p_f:.5f}")
    print(f"Bartlett:      {bartlett(group1, group2).pvalue:.5f}")
    print(f"Brown-Forsythe:{levene(group1, group2, center='median').pvalue:.5f}")
    print(f"Fligner:       {fligner(group1, group2).pvalue:.5f}")

print("Before adding an outlier:")
report(a, b)
print("\nAfter adding an outlier:")
report(a, b_with_outlier)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.boxplot([a, b, b_with_outlier], whis=1.5)
ax.set_xticks([1, 2, 3], ["A", "B", "B + outlier"])
ax.set_ylabel("Observation")
ax.set_title("A single extreme value can affect variance tests")
ax.grid(axis="y", alpha=0.25)
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_outlier_diagnostics.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: replace 15 by 5, 10, and 30.
# Do all tests respond in the same way?

Power to detect a variance difference.

Under normal sampling with a true variance ratio \(R\), the observed sample-variance ratio has the representation \[ \frac{S_1^2}{S_2^2}=R\,W, \qquad W\sim F_{\nu_1,\nu_2}. \] For the equal-tailed two-sided test of \(H_0:R=1\), let \(c_L=F_{\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}\) and \(c_U=F_{1-\alpha/2;\nu_1,\nu_2}\). Its power at the true ratio \(R\) is \[ \pi(R)=G\left(\frac{c_L}{R}\right) +1-G\left(\frac{c_U}{R}\right). \] This expression equals \(\alpha\) when \(R=1\), and becomes larger for sufficiently strong differences in variability. The power of a variance test depends on the sample sizes, significance level, and true variance ratio; modest samples may yield imprecise ratios.

Experiment 89: power and sample size for the two-sided F-test. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import f

alpha = 0.05
ratios = np.linspace(0.25, 4, 300)
sample_sizes = [10, 25, 60]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))

for n in sample_sizes:
    nu1 = nu2 = n - 1
    lower_cutoff = f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
    upper_cutoff = f.isf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
    power = (
        f.cdf(lower_cutoff / ratios, nu1, nu2)
        + f.sf(upper_cutoff / ratios, nu1, nu2)
    )
    ax.plot(ratios, power, label=f"n1 = n2 = {n}")
    print(f"n={n}, power at variance ratio 2: "
          f"{f.cdf(lower_cutoff / 2, nu1, nu2) + f.sf(upper_cutoff / 2, nu1, nu2):.3f}")

ax.axhline(alpha, linestyle="--", label="5% significance level")
ax.axvline(1, linestyle=":")
ax.set_xlabel("True variance ratio")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability of rejection")
ax.set_title("Power of the equal-tailed two-sided F-test")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1)
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_f_power.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: determine a sample size giving 80% power
# for a true variance ratio of 2.

Application: comparing height variability between two groups.

We return to the SOGA-Py students dataset from the original notes. Let \(\sigma_1\) be the population standard deviation of height for female students, and \(\sigma_2\) the corresponding value for male students. The two-sided question is \[ H_0:\sigma_1^2=\sigma_2^2 \qquad\text{against}\qquad H_1:\sigma_1^2\neq\sigma_2^2. \] We draw separate random subsamples of 25 available heights from each group and calculate a normal-theory F-test along with more robust alternatives. The grouping and missing-value checks are important: no missing measurements should enter the test, and the groups should represent independent observations.

Experiment 90: SOGA-Py student-height variability.

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import f, levene, fligner, probplot

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
students = pd.read_csv(url)

# Check group labels and choose nonmissing heights.
female_heights = students.loc[
    students["gender"] == "Female", "height"
].dropna().sample(n=25, random_state=8).to_numpy(dtype=float)

male_heights = students.loc[
    students["gender"] == "Male", "height"
].dropna().sample(n=25, random_state=8).to_numpy(dtype=float)

n1, n2 = len(female_heights), len(male_heights)
nu1, nu2 = n1 - 1, n2 - 1
s1 = np.std(female_heights, ddof=1)
s2 = np.std(male_heights, ddof=1)
ratio = (s1 / s2) ** 2

p_f = min(1.0, 2 * min(f.cdf(ratio, nu1, nu2),
                       f.sf(ratio, nu1, nu2)))
p_bf = levene(female_heights, male_heights,
              center="median").pvalue
p_fligner = fligner(female_heights, male_heights).pvalue

alpha = 0.05
variance_ci = (
    ratio / f.isf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2),
    ratio / f.ppf(alpha / 2, nu1, nu2)
)

print(f"Female sample SD: {s1:.3f}")
print(f"Male sample SD:   {s2:.3f}")
print(f"Variance ratio (female/male): {ratio:.4f}")
print(f"Two-sided F-test p-value: {p_f:.5f}")
print(f"Brown-Forsythe p-value: {p_bf:.5f}")
print(f"Fligner-Killeen p-value: {p_fligner:.5f}")
print("95% F-based CI for variance ratio:", variance_ci)
print("95% F-based CI for SD ratio:", tuple(np.sqrt(variance_ci)))

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.5))
probplot(female_heights, dist="norm", plot=axes[0])
probplot(male_heights, dist="norm", plot=axes[1])
axes[0].set_title("Female height sample: normal Q-Q plot")
axes[1].set_title("Male height sample: normal Q-Q plot")
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("two_sd_student_height_qq.png", dpi=180,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Interpretation requires checking how the original
# data were collected, not just comparing p-values.

The original notes used \(F=s_{\mathrm{male}}^2/s_{\mathrm{female}}^2\) but displayed \(G(F)\) as the p-value for a two-sided hypothesis. That quantity alone is a one-sided tail area, not the correct equal-tailed two-sided p-value. The revised example explicitly fixes the numerator order and uses both tails. It also avoids concluding that the populations are normal merely because the Q-Q plots look approximately linear. Using the numerical values printed in the earlier version (\(F=1.39014\), with 24 and 24 degrees of freedom), the corrected two-sided p-value is about \(0.42560\), rather than \(0.78720\). Both calculations lead to non-rejection at the 5% level, but only the former is the appropriate equal-tailed two-sided p-value.

This is an observational comparison of heights within an existing teaching dataset. A random subsample of that dataset is not necessarily a representative random sample of all students or of any broader population. Inference depends on the original sampling and measurement procedures.

Choosing an approach in practice.

Question or setting Approach Important limitation
Ratio of variances from two independent normal populations Two-sample F-test and F-based confidence interval Exact inference requires normality and independence.
Evidence against equal spread with possible skewness Median-centered Levene (Brown–Forsythe) More robust, not universally exact under arbitrary distributions.
Evidence against equal variability with outliers or heavy tails Fligner–Killeen Its interpretation also depends on the underlying group distributions.
Equality of variances under a convincing normal model Bartlett's test Highly sensitive to nonnormality.
Comparing two means when population variances may differ Welch's two-sample t-test A preliminary significant variance test is not required.

In particular, do not use a preliminary variance test as a mechanical switch between a pooled t-test and Welch's t-test for comparing means. Such a data-dependent two-stage procedure does not automatically preserve the claimed error rate. Welch's procedure is commonly a sensible default for comparing independent population means when equal variances cannot be justified. Testing equality of variances is worthwhile when variability itself is the scientific quantity of interest.

Likewise, failing to reject \(H_0:R=1\) does not establish equal population variances. To show that variability is sufficiently similar, specify a practically meaningful interval for the variance ratio and use an equivalence-oriented analysis. The width of the confidence interval is often more informative than a yes/no decision alone.

Try it yourself. (1) Derive the F-statistic from the two independent chi-square sample-variance pivots. (2) Show that the two-sided equal-tailed p-value does not change if the two groups and their degrees of freedom are exchanged. (3) Use Experiment 85 to verify the reciprocal relationship between confidence intervals for \(\sigma_1^2/\sigma_2^2\) and \(\sigma_2^2/\sigma_1^2\). (4) In Experiment 86, explain why nonnormality can invalidate an F-test even when the population variances really are equal. (5) Use Experiment 89 to find the smallest equal group size in \(10,11,\ldots,200\) giving at least 80% power at a true variance ratio of 2, with \(\alpha=0.05\). (6) Explain why two-sided F-test p-values cannot be calculated from only the lower-tail CDF without considering the upper tail.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 9.7, pp. 597–605, develops the F-distribution and tests for comparing two normal population variances. In particular, Theorems 9.7.2–9.7.5 describe reciprocal F-distributions, the variance-ratio statistic, critical regions, and two-sided p-values. The confidence interval above follows by inversion of that exact variance-ratio pivot. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Section 2.1.6, p. 51, discusses the sensitivity of ordinary variability summaries to extreme values. For implementations of the more robust alternative tests, see the SciPy documentation for scipy.stats.levene, scipy.stats.fligner, and scipy.stats.bartlett.

Chi-Square Goodness-of-Fit Test

Earlier tests concerned parameters such as population means and variances. We now consider a different question: does the distribution of a categorical variable agree with a specified probability model? Pearson's chi-square goodness-of-fit test compares the numbers of observations in several categories with the counts expected under a null hypothesis.

Examples include testing whether a die is fair, whether the frequencies of product choices follow a proposed distribution, or whether observed counts agree with probabilities predicted by a model. This is a test about the distribution of one categorical variable. The chi-square test of independence, which investigates two categorical variables, is treated separately in the next section.

Definition (categorical probability model).

Suppose each of the independent observations belongs to exactly one of the mutually exclusive and exhaustive categories \(C_1,\ldots,C_k\), with probabilities \[ p_i=\mathbb{P}(X\in C_i),\qquad p_i\geq0,\qquad\sum_{i=1}^k p_i=1. \] Let \(O_i\) denote the number of observations in category \(C_i\) in a sample of size \(n\). Then \[ (O_1,\ldots,O_k)\sim \operatorname{Multinomial}(n;p_1,\ldots,p_k), \qquad \sum_{i=1}^k O_i=n. \]

Suppose the proposed probabilities \(p_{01},\ldots,p_{0k}\) are fully specified before examining the sample, with every \(p_{0i}>0\) and \(\sum_i p_{0i}=1\). The hypotheses are \[ \begin{aligned} H_0&:p_i=p_{0i}\quad\text{for every }i,\\ H_1&:p_i\neq p_{0i}\quad\text{for at least one }i. \end{aligned} \] The alternative does not specify which category differs or in which direction. A goodness-of-fit test looks for a discrepancy in any of the categories.

Definition (observed and expected counts).

The observed count for category \(C_i\) is \(O_i\). Under \(H_0\), its expected count is \[ E_i=\mathbb{E}_{H_0}[O_i]=np_{0i}. \] Expected counts need not be integers, even though observed counts are.

Definition (Pearson's chi-square statistic).

For positive expected counts \(E_1,\ldots,E_k\), define \[ Q=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(O_i-E_i)^2}{E_i}. \] Each term measures a squared discrepancy relative to the size of the corresponding expected count. The statistic is nonnegative; larger values represent greater disagreement with \(H_0\).

The statistic itself is not exactly chi-square distributed for a finite multinomial sample. The chi-square distribution is an approximation to its sampling distribution under the null hypothesis.

Theorem (Pearson's asymptotic chi-square test).

Under the multinomial model with a fixed number \(k\geq2\) of categories and fixed positive null probabilities, if \(H_0\) is true, then as \(n\to\infty\), \[ Q\xrightarrow{\mathcal D}\chi^2_{k-1}. \] Thus, for sufficiently large expected counts, an approximate right-tail p-value and level-\(\alpha\) rejection rule are \[ \begin{aligned} p_{\mathrm{value}}&\approx \mathbb{P}(\chi^2_{k-1}\geq Q_{\mathrm{obs}}),\\ \text{reject }H_0&\text{ if } Q_{\mathrm{obs}}>\chi^2_{1-\alpha,k-1}. \end{aligned} \]

The proof of asymptotic convergence uses the multivariate central limit theorem for multinomial counts and a quadratic-form argument. The \(k-1\) degrees of freedom arise because the category counts must add to \(n\): once \(k-1\) counts are known, the last is determined. Unlike a symmetric t-test, the chi-square goodness-of-fit test always uses the right tail, even though its alternative is nondirectional.

Worked example: is a six-sided die fair?

Suppose a die is rolled independently \(n=60\) times. The observed counts for faces 1 through 6 are \[ (O_1,\ldots,O_6)=(15,6,13,9,7,10). \] Under the null hypothesis that the die is fair, \(p_{0i}=1/6\) for each face, so every expected count equals 10. The statistic is \[ \begin{aligned} Q&=\frac{(15-10)^2+(6-10)^2+(13-10)^2 +(9-10)^2+(7-10)^2+(10-10)^2}{10}\\ &=\frac{25+16+9+1+9+0}{10}=6. \end{aligned} \] With \(k-1=5\) degrees of freedom, the approximate p-value is \(\mathbb{P}(\chi^2_5\geq6)\approx0.3062\). At significance level \(\alpha=0.05\), we fail to reject the fair-die model. This does not establish that the die is fair; the sample is simply not sufficiently inconsistent with that model.

Experiment 91: chi-square test for a six-sided die.

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chisquare, chi2

observed = np.array([15, 6, 13, 9, 7, 10])
n = observed.sum()
expected = np.full(6, n / 6)

statistic = np.sum((observed - expected) ** 2 / expected)
df = len(observed) - 1
p_value = chi2.sf(statistic, df)

result = chisquare(f_obs=observed, f_exp=expected)

print(f"Pearson statistic: {statistic:.4f}")
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {df}")
print(f"Manual p-value: {p_value:.6f}")
print(f"SciPy p-value: {result.pvalue:.6f}")

assert np.isclose(statistic, result.statistic)
assert np.isclose(p_value, result.pvalue)

# Experiment: repeat using counts [25, 5, 5, 5, 10, 10].
# How does the evidence against a fair die change?

Which categories contribute most to the discrepancy?

The individual terms of Pearson's statistic reveal which categories contribute to the overall disagreement. Define the Pearson residual \[ r_i=\frac{O_i-E_i}{\sqrt{E_i}}, \qquad Q=\sum_{i=1}^k r_i^2. \] A positive residual indicates more observations than expected; a negative residual indicates fewer. Residual plots are useful for understanding a rejection, but an individual large residual is not automatically a separately validated test for that category.

Experiment 92: observed counts and Pearson residuals.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

categories = np.arange(1, 7)
observed = np.array([15, 6, 13, 9, 7, 10])
expected = np.full(6, observed.sum() / 6)

residuals = (observed - expected) / np.sqrt(expected)
contributions = residuals ** 2

for face, residual, contribution in zip(
    categories, residuals, contributions
):
    print(
        f"Face {face}: residual={residual:+.3f}, "
        f"chi-square contribution={contribution:.3f}"
    )

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(11, 4.5))
width = 0.35

axes[0].bar(categories - width / 2, observed, width, label="Observed")
axes[0].bar(categories + width / 2, expected, width, label="Expected")
axes[0].set_xticks(categories)
axes[0].set_xlabel("Die face")
axes[0].set_ylabel("Count")
axes[0].set_title("Observed and expected frequencies")
axes[0].legend()

axes[1].bar(categories, residuals)
axes[1].axhline(0, linestyle="--", linewidth=1)
axes[1].set_xticks(categories)
axes[1].set_xlabel("Die face")
axes[1].set_ylabel("Pearson residual")
axes[1].set_title("Direction of the discrepancies")

fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("gof_counts_residuals.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: compare results for another set of observed counts.

Illustrative example: student-religion categories and a historical reference.

The earlier version of these notes compared categories from the SOGA-Py students dataset with percentages quoted from the European Commission's 2012 Eurobarometer report on discrimination (Special Eurobarometer 393, p. 233). The reported eight reference categories were Catholic (48%), non-believer/agnostic (16%), Protestant (12%), Orthodox (8%), atheist (7%), other Christian (4%), other religion/none stated (3%), and Muslim (2%).

To illustrate the calculation, the original notes combined several reference categories into Other and listed the following counts from one 256-person subsample of the student dataset. These are historical example inputs, not newly verified population estimates.

Category Reference probability Observed count Expected count
Catholic 0.48 80 122.88
Muslim 0.02 12 5.12
Orthodox 0.08 21 20.48
Other 0.30 104 76.80
Protestant 0.12 39 30.72

Every expected count exceeds 5, so the chi-square approximation is reasonable for this specified multinomial calculation. There are \(k=5\) categories and no parameters estimated from these counts under the null, giving \(4\) degrees of freedom.

Experiment 93: reproduce the worked categorical comparison. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import chisquare

categories = ["Catholic", "Muslim", "Orthodox", "Other", "Protestant"]
probabilities = np.array([0.48, 0.02, 0.08, 0.30, 0.12])
observed = np.array([80, 12, 21, 104, 39])

n = observed.sum()
expected = n * probabilities

summary = pd.DataFrame({
    "category": categories,
    "observed": observed,
    "expected": expected,
    "pearson_residual": (observed - expected) / np.sqrt(expected)
})

result = chisquare(observed, expected)
print(summary.to_string(index=False))
print(f"\nChi-square statistic: {result.statistic:.6f}")
print(f"Approximate p-value: {result.pvalue:.10f}")
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {len(categories) - 1}")

assert n == 256
assert np.all(expected >= 5)

# Experiment: compare the contributions of the five categories.

The original example produces \(Q\approx36.09\), with an approximate p-value of \(2.78\times10^{-7}\). The fixed-count calculation rejects the stated distributional null at the 1% level. However, this numerical result does not justify a broad claim about religious affiliation among all European students. The student dataset is not a representative EU-wide sample, the reference percentages are historical, and the category labeled Other may not have identical definitions across the two sources. A valid population comparison requires a defensible sampling design and matching category definitions.

If you wish to reconstruct the example using the live SOGA-Py file, the following independent script reads it, draws a reproducible subsample, and explicitly checks the supported categories. It may give different counts if the underlying file has changed.

Experiment 94: compute category counts from the SOGA-Py dataset. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import chisquare

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
students = pd.read_csv(url)

categories = ["Catholic", "Muslim", "Orthodox", "Other", "Protestant"]
reference = pd.Series(
    [0.48, 0.02, 0.08, 0.30, 0.12], index=categories
)

valid = students["religion"].dropna()
assert len(valid) >= 256, "Not enough non-missing observations"

sample = valid.sample(n=256, random_state=8)
unexpected = set(sample.unique()) - set(categories)
if unexpected:
    raise ValueError(f"Unmatched categories: {sorted(unexpected)}")

observed = sample.value_counts().reindex(categories, fill_value=0)
expected = len(sample) * reference
result = chisquare(observed.to_numpy(), expected.to_numpy())

comparison = pd.DataFrame({
    "observed": observed,
    "expected": expected,
    "reference_probability": reference
})

print(comparison)
print(f"Chi-square statistic: {result.statistic:.4f}")
print(f"Approximate p-value: {result.pvalue:.6g}")

# A statistically significant result would still require
# population and category-comparability checks.

Conditions for the chi-square approximation.

Several requirements are important for a defensible goodness-of-fit test:

  • The categories must be mutually exclusive and exhaustive, with each independent observational unit contributing one count.
  • The observed counts should arise from a sampling or assignment mechanism appropriate to a multinomial model; repeated measures of the same person or cluster can violate independence.
  • The null probabilities must be valid, sum to 1, and give strictly positive expected counts in the analyzed categories. The observed and expected totals must agree.
  • The expected counts must be sufficiently large for the asymptotic chi-square distribution to approximate the statistic's null distribution well.

A convenient conservative rule of thumb is to aim for an expected count of at least 5 in every category. Other guidelines tolerate some smaller values, but none is an exact mathematical boundary separating valid from invalid tests. The quality of the approximation depends on the entire probability vector and sample size. The condition concerns expected, not observed, counts: an observed count of zero can be legitimate when its expected count is positive.

If some categories have very small expected counts, possibilities include collecting more data, combining categories when this makes substantive sense and is specified in advance, or using a Monte Carlo null distribution. Combining categories merely to obtain a smaller p-value is not a valid strategy.

Why does a chi-square distribution appear?

Under the null model, the vector of count differences is constrained by \(\sum_i(O_i-E_i)=0\). The large-sample distribution of these deviations is approximately multivariate normal. Pearson's quadratic statistic is then approximately a sum of \(k-1\) squared independent standard normal components, explaining the \(\chi^2_{k-1}\) limit. We can check this approximation by repeatedly generating samples from a specified multinomial null model.

Experiment 95: sampling distribution under the null.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
probabilities = np.array([0.40, 0.30, 0.20, 0.10])
n = 300
repetitions = 30_000

expected = n * probabilities
counts = rng.multinomial(n, probabilities, size=repetitions)
statistics = ((counts - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum(axis=1)

df = len(probabilities) - 1
alpha = 0.05
critical = chi2.isf(alpha, df)

print(f"Expected counts: {expected}")
print(f"Theoretical mean of chi-square: {df}")
print(f"Simulated mean of statistic: {statistics.mean():.3f}")
print(f"Nominal Type I error rate: {alpha:.3f}")
print(f"Simulated rejection rate: {np.mean(statistics > critical):.3f}")

x = np.linspace(0, 18, 500)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.hist(statistics, bins=70, density=True, alpha=0.55,
        label="Simulated null statistics")
ax.plot(x, chi2.pdf(x, df), linewidth=2,
        label=f"Chi-square density (df={df})")
ax.axvline(critical, linestyle="--", label="5% critical value")
ax.set_xlabel("Pearson chi-square statistic")
ax.set_ylabel("Density")
ax.set_title("Null sampling distribution of Pearson's statistic")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("gof_chi_square_null.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: reduce n from 300 to 30.
# How well does the chi-square approximation work?

What if some expected counts are small?

When the expected counts are sparse, we can approximate the distribution of \(Q\) under \(H_0\) directly by simulation instead of relying on the chi-square approximation. For a fully specified multinomial null, generate many count vectors with the same \(n\) and probabilities \(p_{01},\ldots,p_{0k}\), and calculate \(Q\) for each one.

If \(B\) simulated statistics are \(Q_1^*,\ldots,Q_B^*\), one useful Monte Carlo p-value estimate is \[ \widehat p_{\mathrm{MC}}= \frac{1+\#\{b:Q_b^*\geq Q_{\mathrm{obs}}\}}{B+1}. \] Including the observed statistic in this ranking avoids a reported p-value of zero. With a fully specified null and independent simulations, this provides a valid conservative Monte Carlo test up to the randomization associated with the simulation.

Experiment 96: sparse expected counts and Monte Carlo testing. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
probabilities = np.array([0.80, 0.15, 0.04, 0.01])
observed = np.array([28, 9, 3, 0])

n = observed.sum()
expected = n * probabilities
observed_q = ((observed - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum()

asymptotic_p = chi2.sf(observed_q, df=len(probabilities) - 1)

B = 100_000
null_counts = rng.multinomial(n, probabilities, size=B)
null_q = ((null_counts - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum(axis=1)
monte_carlo_p = (1 + np.count_nonzero(null_q >= observed_q)) / (B + 1)

print("Expected counts:", expected)
print(f"Observed statistic: {observed_q:.4f}")
print(f"Chi-square approximate p-value: {asymptotic_p:.5f}")
print(f"Monte Carlo p-value: {monte_carlo_p:.5f}")

# Examine Type I error for an asymptotic 5% test.
critical = chi2.isf(0.05, df=len(probabilities) - 1)
print(
    "Simulated rejection rate using chi-square cutoff:",
    f"{np.mean(null_q > critical):.3f}"
)

# Experiment: increase n so that every expected count is at least 5.

This simulation uses the same discrepancy statistic as Pearson's test, but obtains its reference distribution from the specified multinomial model. A Monte Carlo test does not fix incorrect category definitions, biased sampling, or dependence between observations. If null-model parameters have been estimated from the data, the simulation must also account for that estimation procedure.

What changes if the null distribution has unknown parameters?

So far, the probabilities \(p_{01},\ldots,p_{0k}\) were completely specified in advance. Suppose instead that the null model expresses category probabilities as functions \(p_i(\theta)\) of \(s\) unknown parameters, which are estimated from the same grouped counts. In the usual regular case, using the multinomial maximum-likelihood estimate \(\widehat\theta\) gives the statistic \[ Q=\sum_{i=1}^{k} \frac{[O_i-np_i(\widehat\theta)]^2} {np_i(\widehat\theta)} \quad\overset{\mathrm{approx.}}{\sim}\quad \chi^2_{k-1-s}\quad\text{under }H_0. \] One degree of freedom is lost because counts add to \(n\), and an additional \(s\) degrees of freedom are typically lost by fitting the parameters. This adjustment requires regularity conditions, identifiable parameters, and a suitable fitting method; it is not an automatic correction for arbitrary data-dependent models.

A simple illustration comes from a population genetics model. Suppose the categories are \(AA\), \(Aa\), and \(aa\), with probabilities \[ p_{AA}=\theta^2,\qquad p_{Aa}=2\theta(1-\theta),\qquad p_{aa}=(1-\theta)^2. \] These are the Hardy–Weinberg proportions under the model. The single unknown parameter \(\theta\) is estimated from the three genotype counts. The grouped-data maximum-likelihood estimator is \[ \widehat\theta= \frac{2O_{AA}+O_{Aa}}{2n}. \] With three categories and one fitted parameter, the chi-square approximation uses \(3-1-1=1\) degree of freedom.

Experiment 97: fitted probabilities and degrees of freedom. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chisquare, chi2

# Illustrative genotype counts, not a real population survey.
observed = np.array([56, 26, 18])  # AA, Aa, aa
n = observed.sum()

theta_hat = (2 * observed[0] + observed[1]) / (2 * n)
expected_probabilities = np.array([
    theta_hat ** 2,
    2 * theta_hat * (1 - theta_hat),
    (1 - theta_hat) ** 2
])
expected = n * expected_probabilities

# One free parameter is fitted from the same category counts.
df = len(observed) - 1 - 1
statistic = ((observed - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum()
p_value = chi2.sf(statistic, df)

# SciPy's ddof accounts for the fitted parameter here.
result = chisquare(observed, expected, ddof=1)

print(f"Estimated allele frequency: {theta_hat:.4f}")
print("Expected counts:", np.round(expected, 3))
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {df}")
print(f"Chi-square statistic: {statistic:.4f}")
print(f"Approximate p-value: {p_value:.6f}")
assert np.isclose(p_value, result.pvalue)

# Experiment: compare with the incorrect choice df=2.

For continuous distributions grouped into bins, estimating parameters from the original unbinned observations rather than the grouped counts can change the null distribution of the statistic. In such cases, blindly subtracting the number of estimated parameters from \(k-1\) need not yield a valid approximation. Simulation or a more specialized goodness-of-fit procedure may be preferable.

How do sample size and the true distribution affect power?

A goodness-of-fit test may fail to detect a real discrepancy when the sample is small. To describe the size of a particular deviation from the null, let \(q=(q_1,\ldots,q_k)\) be a proposed alternative probability distribution. Cohen's goodness-of-fit effect size is \[ w=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^k\frac{(q_i-p_{0i})^2}{p_{0i}}}. \] For fixed \(q\) and \(p_0\), the large-sample noncentrality parameter is \(\lambda=nw^2\). Thus, as the sample size grows, the test generally becomes more capable of detecting a fixed nonzero discrepancy. This does not establish whether the difference matters in practice.

Experiment 98: simulated power and sample size.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
p_null = np.array([0.40, 0.30, 0.20, 0.10])
p_alternative = np.array([0.45, 0.25, 0.20, 0.10])

sample_sizes = [50, 100, 200, 400, 800]
repetitions = 10_000
alpha = 0.05

df = len(p_null) - 1
critical = chi2.isf(alpha, df)
w = np.sqrt(np.sum((p_alternative - p_null) ** 2 / p_null))

false_positive_rates = []
powers = []

for n in sample_sizes:
    expected = n * p_null
    under_null = rng.multinomial(n, p_null, size=repetitions)
    under_alt = rng.multinomial(n, p_alternative, size=repetitions)

    q_null = ((under_null - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum(axis=1)
    q_alt = ((under_alt - expected) ** 2 / expected).sum(axis=1)

    false_positive_rates.append(np.mean(q_null > critical))
    powers.append(np.mean(q_alt > critical))

    print(
        f"n={n:4d} | Type I rate={false_positive_rates[-1]:.3f} "
        f"| power={powers[-1]:.3f}"
    )

print(f"Specified alternative effect size w = {w:.4f}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.plot(sample_sizes, false_positive_rates, marker="o",
        label="Rejection rate under the null")
ax.plot(sample_sizes, powers, marker="s",
        label="Power under the alternative")
ax.axhline(alpha, linestyle="--", label="Nominal alpha = 0.05")
ax.set_xlabel("Sample size")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability of rejection")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1)
ax.set_title("Goodness-of-fit test: Type I error and power")
ax.grid(alpha=0.25)
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("gof_power_sample_size.png", dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

# Experiment: make the alternative probabilities closer to p_null.
# How does the power curve change?

Interpreting a goodness-of-fit result.

A small p-value provides evidence that at least one category probability differs from the specified null model, under the sampling assumptions. It does not identify which probabilities differ, establish causation, or imply that the proposed model is false with a particular posterior probability. The size and practical importance of discrepancies should be assessed separately, using frequencies, residuals, sample size, and subject-matter context.

Failing to reject \(H_0\) does not prove that the proposed distribution is correct. A weak test may miss real but modest deviations. Conversely, very large samples can identify differences that are too small to matter in an application. As with earlier hypothesis tests, data quality and appropriate sampling are essential for interpreting results.

The chi-square goodness-of-fit test compares counts against one probability model. If the question instead concerns whether two categorical variables are associated, we construct a contingency table and use the chi-square test of independence developed in the next section.

Try it yourself. (1) Verify the manual fair-die calculation in Experiment 91 and state the null and alternative hypotheses. (2) In Experiment 92, show algebraically that the squared Pearson residuals sum to the test statistic. (3) Recalculate Experiment 93 after combining two categories using probabilities and observed counts merged consistently; explain how the degrees of freedom change. (4) Use Experiment 96 to compare asymptotic and simulated p-values for several samples with very small expected counts. (5) Explain why Experiment 97 uses one degree of freedom rather than two. (6) In Experiment 98, find a sample size for which the simulated power is at least 80% under the specified alternative.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 10.1, pp. 624–633, develops Pearson's chi-square goodness-of-fit test and its large-sample justification (Theorem 10.1.1). Section 10.2, pp. 633–639, addresses composite null hypotheses and fitted category probabilities (Theorem 10.2.1). OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Chapter 3, introduces categorical probability models and the multinomial setting; Section 3.5 discusses continuous distributions, which require grouping into categories before applying this version of Pearson's statistic. The code uses scipy.stats.chisquare, scipy.stats.chi2, and NumPy's multinomial generator.

The Chi-Square Independence Test

The chi-square test of independence examines whether two categorical variables are statistically independent in a population. Unlike the chi-square goodness-of-fit test, which studies the distribution of one variable, the independence test studies the joint distribution of two categorical variables.

Suppose we record a student's field of study and gender, or a customer's acquisition channel and whether a purchase occurred. The objective is to determine whether the proportions across categories differ more than we would expect from random sampling variation under independence.

Definition (independence of categorical variables).

Let \(X\) take categories \(1,\ldots,r\), and let \(Y\) take categories \(1,\ldots,c\). Write \[ p_{ij}=\mathbb{P}(X=i,Y=j),\quad p_{i+}=\mathbb{P}(X=i),\quad p_{+j}=\mathbb{P}(Y=j). \] The variables are independent if and only if \[ p_{ij}=p_{i+}p_{+j} \quad\text{for every }i,j. \]

Definition (two-way contingency table).

For a sample of \(n\) jointly classified observations, let \(O_{ij}\) be the number belonging to row category \(i\) and column category \(j\). A table of these counts is called an \(r\times c\) contingency table (or cross-tabulation). Its row totals, column totals, and grand total are \[ O_{i+}=\sum_{j=1}^{c}O_{ij},\qquad O_{+j}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}O_{ij},\qquad n=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{c}O_{ij}. \]

The hypotheses for the independence test are \[ \begin{aligned} H_0&:p_{ij}=p_{i+}p_{+j}\quad\text{for all }i,j,\\ H_1&:\text{the variables are not independent}. \end{aligned} \] This is an omnibus test: rejection indicates evidence of some departure from independence, but it does not identify the particular cells responsible or establish a causal relationship.

Lemma (expected counts under independence).

Using the sample marginal proportions as estimates of the unknown marginal probabilities, the fitted expected count in cell \((i,j)\) under \(H_0\) is \[ E_{ij}=n\left(\frac{O_{i+}}{n}\right) \left(\frac{O_{+j}}{n}\right) =\frac{O_{i+}O_{+j}}{n}. \]

Proof. Under independence, the joint probability is the product of the two marginal probabilities. We estimate these probabilities by their sample proportions \(O_{i+}/n\) and \(O_{+j}/n\). Multiplying their product by the total sample size gives the fitted cell count.

An expected count is not a requirement that a cell contain exactly that many observations. It is the count predicted by the fitted independence model. Expected counts may be fractional even though observed counts must be nonnegative integers.

Theorem (Pearson's chi-square independence test).

Let \(O_{ij}\) be the observed counts in an \(r\times c\) table, with positive population marginal probabilities. For independent, identically distributed observations under \(H_0\), define \[ Q=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{c} \frac{(O_{ij}-E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}, \quad E_{ij}=\frac{O_{i+}O_{+j}}{n}. \] Under the usual fixed-category, large-sample conditions, \[ Q\xrightarrow{\mathcal D} \chi^2_{(r-1)(c-1)}\qquad\text{under }H_0. \]

The chi-square distribution is a large-sample approximation to the null distribution of \(Q\); it is not generally an exact finite-sample distribution. Large values of \(Q\) measure a greater discrepancy between observed and independence-model expected counts. For a chi-square reference distribution with \(d=(r-1)(c-1)\) degrees of freedom, the approximate p-value is \[ p\text{-value}=\mathbb{P}(\chi^2_d\geq Q_{\mathrm{obs}}). \]

Why \((r-1)(c-1)\) degrees of freedom? An unrestricted \(r\times c\) joint probability distribution has \(rc-1\) free probabilities because all cell probabilities sum to 1. Under independence, the row probabilities have \(r-1\) free parameters and the column probabilities have \(c-1\). The difference is \[ (rc-1)-[(r-1)+(c-1)]=(r-1)(c-1). \] This parameter-count argument explains the limiting degrees of freedom, but it does not by itself prove the convergence theorem.

Worked example: acquisition channel and purchase status.

Consider the following illustrative counts. Every observation represents one independent customer, classified by acquisition channel and purchase outcome.

Purchase Email Search Ads Total
Yes 45 35 20 100
No 30 40 30 100
Total 75 75 50 200

If the variables were independent, the expected count for customers who purchased after arriving via Email would be \[ E_{11}=\frac{100\cdot75}{200}=37.5. \] The full expected table is \[ E=\begin{pmatrix}37.5&37.5&25\\37.5&37.5&25\end{pmatrix}. \] We calculate \(Q\) from all six cells and compare it with the \(\chi^2\) distribution with \((2-1)(3-1)=2\) degrees of freedom.

Experiment 99: calculating the test statistic manually and with SciPy. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2, chi2_contingency

observed = np.array([
    [45, 35, 20],  # Purchased
    [30, 40, 30]   # Did not purchase
])

n = observed.sum()
row_totals = observed.sum(axis=1)
column_totals = observed.sum(axis=0)

expected = np.outer(row_totals, column_totals) / n
chi_square = np.sum((observed - expected) ** 2 / expected)
df = (observed.shape[0] - 1) * (observed.shape[1] - 1)
p_value = chi2.sf(chi_square, df)

result = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)

print("Expected counts:\n", expected)
print(f"Manual statistic: {chi_square:.5f}")
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {df}")
print(f"Manual p-value: {p_value:.6f}")
print(f"SciPy p-value: {result.pvalue:.6f}")

assert np.isclose(chi_square, result.statistic)
assert np.isclose(p_value, result.pvalue)

# Experiment: change the Email purchase count, while
# considering how the other counts should change.

The correction=False argument is deliberate. For a \(2\times2\) table, SciPy's chi2_contingency otherwise applies Yates' continuity correction by default. The subsequent calculations use the uncorrected Pearson statistic to match the formula.

Application: field of study and gender in the SOGA-Py example.

The earlier version of these notes used a sample of 865 observations from the SOGA-Py students dataset. The reported cross-tabulation is reproduced below, so the calculation can be checked without downloading the original dataset. The category labels reflect the original dataset; the example describes an observed association, not a causal effect or the population distribution of all students.

Experiment 100: a 6-by-2 independence test.

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency

observed = pd.DataFrame(
    {
        "Female": [102, 53, 63, 33, 111, 78],
        "Male":   [66, 82, 88, 93, 61, 35]
    },
    index=[
        "Biology",
        "Economics and Finance",
