        "Environmental Sciences",
        "Mathematics and Statistics",
        "Political Science",
        "Social Sciences"
    ]
)

result = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)
expected = pd.DataFrame(
    result.expected_freq,
    index=observed.index,
    columns=observed.columns
)

print("Observed counts:\n", observed)
print("\nExpected counts:\n", expected.round(2))
print(f"\nTotal sample size: {observed.to_numpy().sum()}")
print(f"Chi-square statistic: {result.statistic:.4f}")
print(f"Degrees of freedom: {result.dof}")
print(f"p-value: {result.pvalue:.4g}")

assert observed.to_numpy().sum() == 865
assert np.isclose(result.statistic, 77.31526633939146)

# Experiment: inspect column proportions, rather
# than comparing raw counts only.

In this sample, the test gives approximately \(Q=77.3153\), with five degrees of freedom and a very small p-value. This is evidence against independence in the source population, provided the sampling and modeling assumptions are appropriate. It does not justify the claim that gender causes field-of-study choices, nor does it establish that the teaching dataset represents all students.

Which cells contribute to the association?

The global chi-square test does not explain where an association occurs. The contribution of cell \((i,j)\) to Pearson's statistic is \[ C_{ij}=\frac{(O_{ij}-E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}. \] The Pearson residual retains the direction of that difference: \[ R_{ij}=\frac{O_{ij}-E_{ij}}{\sqrt{E_{ij}}}, \quad Q=\sum_{i,j}R_{ij}^2. \] A positive residual means the cell contains more observations than independence predicts; a negative residual means fewer. For exploratory comparison across cells, an adjusted standardized residual can also be useful: \[ R^{\mathrm{adj}}_{ij} =\frac{O_{ij}-E_{ij}} {\sqrt{E_{ij}(1-O_{i+}/n)(1-O_{+j}/n)}}. \] The adjusted residual accounts for the margins, but treating many residuals as separate hypothesis tests creates a multiple-testing problem. The residuals should initially be regarded as diagnostic.

Experiment 101: examining the cells driving an association. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency

observed = pd.DataFrame(
    {
        "Female": [102, 53, 63, 33, 111, 78],
        "Male":   [66, 82, 88, 93, 61, 35]
    },
    index=[
        "Biology", "Economics and Finance",
        "Environmental Sciences", "Mathematics and Statistics",
        "Political Science", "Social Sciences"
    ]
)

result = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)
counts = observed.to_numpy()
expected = result.expected_freq
n = counts.sum()
row_share = counts.sum(axis=1)[:, None] / n
col_share = counts.sum(axis=0)[None, :] / n

pearson = (counts - expected) / np.sqrt(expected)
adjusted = (counts - expected) / np.sqrt(
    expected * (1 - row_share) * (1 - col_share)
)

print("Pearson residuals:\n", pd.DataFrame(
    pearson, index=observed.index, columns=observed.columns
).round(2))
print("\nAdjusted residuals:\n", pd.DataFrame(
    adjusted, index=observed.index, columns=observed.columns
).round(2))

assert np.isclose(np.sum(pearson ** 2), result.statistic)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5.5))
image = ax.imshow(pearson, aspect="auto", cmap="coolwarm")

ax.set_xticks(np.arange(observed.shape[1]), observed.columns)
ax.set_yticks(np.arange(observed.shape[0]), observed.index)
ax.set_title("Pearson residuals: observed versus expected")

for i in range(pearson.shape[0]):
    for j in range(pearson.shape[1]):
        ax.text(j, i, f"{pearson[i, j]:.1f}",
                ha="center", va="center")

fig.colorbar(image, ax=ax, label="Pearson residual")
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("chi_square_independence_residuals.png",
            dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Statistical significance versus strength of association.

A p-value measures evidence against the null relative to a statistical model; it is not a measure of how strong the categorical association is. With a large sample, even a small departure from independence can be statistically detectable. One common descriptive effect-size measure is Cramér's V.

Definition (Cramér's V).

For an \(r\times c\) table with total count \(n\) and Pearson statistic \(Q\), define \[ V=\sqrt{\frac{Q}{n\min(r-1,c-1)}}. \] For a two-way table with at least two nonempty rows and columns, the uncorrected sample value lies between 0 and 1. A value of 0 indicates exact independence in the observed table. A positive value measures departure from independence, but does not indicate the direction of association.

Cramér's V should be interpreted with the table's dimensions, category structure and sampling context in mind. There is no universally valid cutoff that separates weak from strong associations. For \(2\times2\) tables, other summaries such as conditional proportions, risk differences and odds ratios may be more interpretable.

Experiment 102: effect size and conditional proportions. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency

observed = pd.DataFrame(
    {
        "Female": [102, 53, 63, 33, 111, 78],
        "Male":   [66, 82, 88, 93, 61, 35]
    },
    index=[
        "Biology", "Economics and Finance",
        "Environmental Sciences", "Mathematics and Statistics",
        "Political Science", "Social Sciences"
    ]
)

result = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)
n = observed.to_numpy().sum()
r, c = observed.shape
cramers_v = np.sqrt(result.statistic / (n * min(r - 1, c - 1)))

print(f"Cramer's V: {cramers_v:.4f}")
print("\nMajor distribution within each gender:")
print((observed.div(observed.sum(axis=0), axis=1) * 100).round(1))

# Experiment: multiply every cell count by 10.
# What happens to Cramer's V and the p-value?

Comparing proportions within each column is often more informative than comparing the raw counts. However, even a large association does not imply that either variable causes the other.

Assumptions and expected-count guidelines.

The key design assumption is that observations are independent at the relevant sampling unit. Repeated measurements on the same customer, student or household generally cannot be treated as independent rows without further justification. Categories must be defined consistently, and each observation should be counted in exactly one cell.

The chi-square reference distribution also requires sufficient information in the expected cell counts. A simple conservative guideline is to aim for expected counts of at least 5 in every cell. A frequently quoted, more permissive rule allows a small minority of expected counts below 5 but none below 1; it is only a heuristic, not a guarantee of accurate p-values. The reliability of the approximation depends on the margins and the table's overall structure. These guidelines concern expected, not observed, counts.

Do not remove rare categories or combine them solely because that produces a desirable p-value. Combining categories should make sense substantively and preferably be specified before examining the results. When counts are sparse, an exact or conditional Monte Carlo approach may be preferable.

Experiment 103: chi-square versus Fisher's exact test.

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency, fisher_exact

# Illustrative small 2-by-2 contingency table.
observed = np.array([
    [1, 9],
    [11, 3]
])

pearson = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)
yates = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=True)
fisher = fisher_exact(observed, alternative="two-sided")

print("Expected counts:\n", pearson.expected_freq)
print(f"Pearson approximation: p={pearson.pvalue:.6f}")
print(f"Yates correction:       p={yates.pvalue:.6f}")
print(f"Fisher exact test:      p={fisher.pvalue:.6f}")
print(f"Sample odds ratio:     {fisher.statistic:.4f}")

# Experiment: multiply every cell by 5.
# Do the p-values become more similar?

Fisher's exact test computes a conditional p-value for a \(2\times2\) table using its observed margins. Its two-sided p-value is not generally obtained simply by doubling one tail; the calculation depends on the exact-test definition. An odds ratio summarizes the association in a \(2\times2\) table, but its interpretation depends on the sampling design.

Conditional Monte Carlo testing for sparse larger tables.

For tables larger than \(2\times2\), conditional Monte Carlo simulation offers one way to avoid relying solely on the asymptotic chi-square tail. Under independence and conditional on the observed row and column totals, we can generate many tables with those same margins and compare their Pearson statistics with the observed one.

If \(B\) simulated tables are generated, a useful Monte Carlo estimate of the conditional tail probability is \[ \widehat p=\frac{1+\#\{b:Q_b\geq Q_{\mathrm{obs}}\}}{B+1}. \] This simulation approximates a conditional exact test, not an unconditional sampling distribution. The formula also avoids returning a zero p-value just because no simulated table was as extreme as the observed one.

Experiment 104: fixed-margin Monte Carlo test for a sparse 2-by-3 table. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
observed = np.array([
    [8, 0, 2],
    [1, 4, 5]
])

result = chi2_contingency(observed, correction=False)
print("Expected counts:\n", result.expected_freq)
print(f"Asymptotic p-value: {result.pvalue:.5f}")

# Expand the row and column categories according to the margins.
# Shuffling column assignments preserves both margins.
r, c = observed.shape
row_ids = np.repeat(np.arange(r), observed.sum(axis=1))
col_ids = np.repeat(np.arange(c), observed.sum(axis=0))
expected = result.expected_freq

B = 20_000
extreme_count = 0

for _ in range(B):
    shuffled_cols = rng.permutation(col_ids)
    simulated = np.bincount(
        row_ids * c + shuffled_cols,
        minlength=r * c
    ).reshape(r, c)

    simulated_stat = np.sum(
        (simulated - expected) ** 2 / expected
    )
    extreme_count += simulated_stat >= result.statistic - 1e-12

monte_carlo_p = (extreme_count + 1) / (B + 1)
print(f"Conditional Monte Carlo p-value: {monte_carlo_p:.5f}")

# Experiment: increase B to investigate Monte Carlo accuracy.

Sampling variability, sample size, and statistical power.

A valid test should reject a true null with approximately the nominal probability, but it should reject increasingly often under a fixed departure from independence as the sample size grows. The following simulation compares rejection frequencies for two \(2\times3\) joint distributions, one independent and one associated.

Experiment 105: Type I error and power of the independence test. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import chi2

rng = np.random.default_rng(2026)
alpha = 0.05
B = 3000
sample_sizes = [50, 100, 200, 500]

# Both models have identical margins, but only the first
# satisfies statistical independence.
probabilities_null = np.array([
    [0.10, 0.20, 0.20],
    [0.10, 0.20, 0.20]
])
probabilities_alt = np.array([
    [0.17, 0.20, 0.13],
    [0.03, 0.20, 0.27]
])


def rejection_rate(n, probabilities):
    tables = rng.multinomial(
        n, probabilities.ravel(), size=B
    ).reshape(B, 2, 3)

    row_totals = tables.sum(axis=2, keepdims=True)
    col_totals = tables.sum(axis=1, keepdims=True)
    expected = row_totals * col_totals / n

    # All included tables have positive margins in this setup.
    valid = np.all(expected > 0, axis=(1, 2))
    tables = tables[valid]
    expected = expected[valid]

    statistic = np.sum(
        (tables - expected) ** 2 / expected,
        axis=(1, 2)
    )
    p_values = chi2.sf(statistic, df=2)
    return np.mean(p_values < alpha)


type1_rates = []
power_rates = []

for n in sample_sizes:
    type1 = rejection_rate(n, probabilities_null)
    power = rejection_rate(n, probabilities_alt)
    type1_rates.append(type1)
    power_rates.append(power)
    print(f"n={n:3d}  Type I rate={type1:.3f}  Power={power:.3f}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(9, 5))
ax.plot(sample_sizes, type1_rates, marker="o", label="Null: Type I error")
ax.plot(sample_sizes, power_rates, marker="s", label="Alternative: power")
ax.axhline(alpha, linestyle="--", label="Nominal alpha = 0.05")
ax.set_xlabel("Sample size")
ax.set_ylabel("Probability of rejection")
ax.set_ylim(0, 1)
ax.set_title("Type I error and power of the chi-square test")
ax.legend()
ax.grid(alpha=0.25)
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("chi_square_independence_power.png",
            dpi=180, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

The simulation illustrates why statistical significance must not be confused with effect size. Increasing the sample size can make small departures from independence detectable without changing the strength of the underlying association.

Association does not imply causation.

The chi-square test does not establish a causal mechanism. An apparent association can arise from confounding, selection effects, or other aspects of data collection. Moreover, an association found after pooling data can differ dramatically from associations within relevant subgroups. This phenomenon is illustrated by Simpson's paradox.

Experiment 106: Simpson's paradox in categorical data.

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import chi2_contingency

# Synthetic counts: each row is a service, each column
# is success/failure. Services are assigned different
# proportions of easy and hard cases.
easy = np.array([
    [18, 2],   # Service A: 90% success
    [64, 16]   # Service B: 80% success
])

hard = np.array([
    [24, 56],  # Service A: 30% success
    [2, 8]     # Service B: 20% success
])

combined = easy + hard

for name, table in [
    ("Easy cases", easy),
    ("Hard cases", hard),
    ("All cases", combined)
]:
    success_rates = table[:, 0] / table.sum(axis=1)
    p_value = chi2_contingency(
        table, correction=False
    ).pvalue

    print(name)
    print(f"  Service A success rate: {success_rates[0]:.1%}")
    print(f"  Service B success rate: {success_rates[1]:.1%}")
    print(f"  Chi-square p-value: {p_value:.4f}")

# Experiment: change the mixture of easy and hard cases
# without changing the within-group success rates.

Service A has the higher success proportion in both difficulty categories, but Service B has the higher overall success proportion because its cases are concentrated in the easier group. The reversal is caused by differing group composition. This illustrates why stratification and thoughtful study design may be essential before interpreting an overall contingency table.

Independence and homogeneity tests.

A test of independence usually arises from one sample in which every observation is classified by two categorical variables. A test of homogeneity compares the distributions of a categorical outcome across independently sampled populations or prespecified groups. Both commonly use the same Pearson statistic and the same approximate \((r-1)(c-1)\) degrees of freedom, but their sampling designs and interpretations differ.

With a \(2\times2\) table, a test of independence also tests equality of the relevant conditional proportions under an appropriate design. For an A/B experiment, however, differences in observed conversion rates should be interpreted through the randomization and data collection process, not solely through a chi-square p-value.

Practical considerations.

Before performing a chi-square independence test, verify what each row and column represents, whether the counts are mutually exclusive, whether the observational units are independent, and whether missing data or weighting changes the interpretation. Use counts, not a table of already computed percentages, unless an appropriate count-based model has been reconstructed. After the omnibus test, inspect conditional proportions and residuals; report a useful effect-size summary and the sample size alongside the p-value.

Try it yourself. (1) Compute the expected counts and Pearson contributions for the acquisition-channel table by hand. (2) Reproduce the six-by-two student example and identify the cells with the largest absolute Pearson residuals. (3) Multiply every cell count in the student table by 10; explain the changes to the p-value and Cramér's V. (4) For a sparse two-by-two table, compare Pearson, Yates-corrected, and Fisher p-values. (5) In Experiment 105, change the alternative distribution while preserving its margins; examine how the power curve changes. (6) Explain why the aggregated conclusion in Experiment 106 reverses the conclusions in its subgroups.

Further reading. DeGroot and Schervish (4th ed.), Section 10.3, pp. 641–646, develops the chi-square independence test for contingency tables; Sections 10.4–10.5, pp. 647–655, discuss homogeneity tests and Simpson's paradox. OpenIntro Statistics (4th ed.), Section 6.4, pp. 240–247, presents the test for two-way tables, expected counts, and the interpretation of independence tests. Relevant exercises appear at the end of Section 6.4. For implementation details, see the SciPy documentation for scipy.stats.chi2_contingency and scipy.stats.fisher_exact.

Linear Regression

Regression models relate a numerical response to one or more predictors. Unlike a deterministic formula, a statistical regression model explicitly allows unexplained variation. We first develop simple linear regression, then use it to understand estimation, diagnostics, and prediction. The next section develops hypothesis tests for its slope. The Python examples use NumPy, SciPy, pandas, Matplotlib, scikit-learn, and statsmodels; install any missing packages in your project environment before running them.

Definition (simple linear regression model).

For observations \((x_i,Y_i)\), \(i=1,\ldots,n\), suppose

\[Y_i=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i+\varepsilon_i,\qquad \mathbb E(\varepsilon_i\mid x_i)=0.\]

The unknown parameters \(\beta_0\) and \(\beta_1\) are the population intercept and population slope. The conditional mean is \(\mathbb E(Y_i\mid x_i)=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i\). In the fixed-design model, the \(x_i\) are treated as fixed; in a random-design model, inference may be conditioned on the observed predictors.

The slope gives the expected difference in the response for a one-unit increase in \(x\), holding the model's interpretation fixed. An association in observational data does not establish a causal effect. A predictor may also be statistically associated with a response while explaining little of its variation.

Definition (fitted values, residuals, and SSE).

For candidate coefficients \(b_0,b_1\), write \(\hat y_i=b_0+b_1x_i\) and residual \(e_i=y_i-\hat y_i\). Ordinary least squares (OLS) selects the coefficients minimizing

\[\operatorname{SSE}(b_0,b_1)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_i-b_0-b_1x_i)^2.\]

The observable residuals \(e_i\) are not the same as the unobservable errors \(\varepsilon_i=Y_i-\beta_0-\beta_1x_i\).

Theorem (simple OLS formulas).

For \(n\geq2\) and \(S_{xx}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2>0\), the unique OLS estimates are

\[\hat\beta_1=\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}},\qquad \hat\beta_0=\bar y-\hat\beta_1\bar x,\qquad S_{xy}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(y_i-\bar y).\]

The fitted line passes through \((\bar x,\bar y)\), and the OLS residuals satisfy \(\sum_i e_i=0\) and \(\sum_i x_ie_i=0\).

Proof. Differentiate SSE with respect to \(b_0\) and \(b_1\), set both derivatives to zero, and obtain the normal equations \(\sum_i(y_i-b_0-b_1x_i)=0\) and \(\sum_i x_i(y_i-b_0-b_1x_i)=0\). The first gives \(b_0=\bar y-b_1\bar x\); substituting into the second yields \(b_1=S_{xy}/S_{xx}\). As \(S_{xx}>0\), the quadratic objective is strictly convex in the two coefficients, proving uniqueness. The normal equations give the residual identities.

Notice that minimizing squared prediction errors in the observed sample does not, by itself, require normally distributed errors. Normality enters later for certain exact confidence intervals and hypothesis tests.

Experiment 107: derive and visualize the least-squares line. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(107)
x = np.linspace(0, 10, 45)
y = 3 + 1.7 * x + rng.normal(0, 2, size=x.size)

x_bar, y_bar = x.mean(), y.mean()
s_xx = np.sum((x - x_bar) ** 2)
s_xy = np.sum((x - x_bar) * (y - y_bar))
b1 = s_xy / s_xx
b0 = y_bar - b1 * x_bar
fitted = b0 + b1 * x
residuals = y - fitted

print(f"Intercept: {b0:.4f}; slope: {b1:.4f}")
print(f"SSE: {np.sum(residuals ** 2):.4f}")
print(f"Sum of residuals: {residuals.sum():.3e}")
print(f"Sum of x * residuals: {np.sum(x * residuals):.3e}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, label="Observed values")
ax.plot(x, fitted, label="OLS fitted line")
ax.plot(x, 3 + 1.7 * x, linestyle="--", label="Population mean line")
ax.set(xlabel="Predictor x", ylabel="Response y", title="Ordinary least squares")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("lr_ols_fit.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Software implementations. OLS solves the same optimization problem whether we use the closed-form expression, NumPy, scikit-learn, or statsmodels. scikit-learn emphasizes prediction workflows, whereas statsmodels reports statistical uncertainty and diagnostic information. The model matrix must include an intercept explicitly in statsmodels.OLS when one is desired.

Experiment 108: compare NumPy, scikit-learn, and statsmodels. 

import numpy as np
import statsmodels.api as sm
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression

rng = np.random.default_rng(108)
x = rng.normal(size=100)
y = 2.5 - 0.8 * x + rng.normal(0, 0.9, size=x.size)

X = np.column_stack([np.ones(x.size), x])
beta_numpy = np.linalg.lstsq(X, y, rcond=None)[0]
model_sklearn = LinearRegression().fit(x.reshape(-1, 1), y)
model_sm = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()

print("NumPy (intercept, slope):", beta_numpy)
print("scikit-learn:", model_sklearn.intercept_, model_sklearn.coef_[0])
print("statsmodels:", model_sm.params)
print("Identical coefficients:", np.allclose(beta_numpy, model_sm.params))
print("R-squared:", model_sm.rsquared)
print("Residual standard error:", np.sqrt(model_sm.mse_resid))

Definition (SST, SSR, SSE, and R-squared).

For OLS with an intercept, define total variation \(\mathrm{SST}=\sum_i(y_i-\bar y)^2\), explained variation \(\mathrm{SSR}=\sum_i(\hat y_i-\bar y)^2\), and residual variation \(\mathrm{SSE}=\sum_i(y_i-\hat y_i)^2\). Then

\[\mathrm{SST}=\mathrm{SSR}+\mathrm{SSE},\qquad R^2=1-\frac{\mathrm{SSE}}{\mathrm{SST}}\quad(\mathrm{SST}>0).\]

In this in-sample OLS setting with an intercept, \(0\leq R^2\leq1\). The decomposition and range need not hold in the same way for models without an intercept or for out-of-sample scores.

Proof. Write \(y_i-\bar y=(\hat y_i-\bar y)+e_i\); after squaring and summing, the cross term is zero because OLS residuals are orthogonal to the fitted values. Dividing the identity by SST gives the formula for \(R^2\). This number describes in-sample variation explained, not causation or guaranteed future prediction quality.

Scale-dependent errors. The residual standard error is \(s=\sqrt{\mathrm{SSE}/(n-2)}\) for simple regression with an intercept. Training RMSE instead uses \(\sqrt{\mathrm{SSE}/n}\). Their denominators differ because the first estimates the conditional error standard deviation after fitting two parameters, while the latter summarizes observed prediction errors.

Definition (classical conditional-error assumptions).

For exact small-sample t-based inference, the common model assumes independent observations and, conditional on the predictor values, \(\varepsilon_i\overset{\mathrm{ind}}\sim N(0,\sigma^2)\), with a common unknown variance \(\sigma^2>0\). For unbiased OLS coefficients, the central requirement is the correct conditional-mean specification \(\mathbb E(\varepsilon_i\mid X)=0\); independent, homoskedastic errors provide simpler variance formulas. Normality is not needed to calculate OLS estimates.

Check scatterplots and residuals versus fitted values for curvature, varying spread, and anomalous points; Q-Q plots can help assess error-normality approximations. These plots are diagnostics, not proofs. When observations are clustered or collected over time, dependence must also be considered. Residuals are mechanically constrained by the fit and are not identical to the underlying errors.

Experiment 109: diagnose heteroskedastic residuals.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import statsmodels.api as sm
from statsmodels.stats.diagnostic import het_breuschpagan

rng = np.random.default_rng(109)
x = rng.uniform(0, 10, 110)
y = 1 + 2.2 * x + rng.normal(0, 0.4 + 0.28 * x)
fit = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()
lm, p_lm, _, _ = het_breuschpagan(fit.resid, fit.model.exog)
print(f"Breusch-Pagan LM statistic: {lm:.3f}, p-value: {p_lm:.4g}")
print("The test is a diagnostic; inspect the plot and sampling design too.")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(fit.fittedvalues, fit.resid, alpha=0.7)
ax.axhline(0, linestyle="--", linewidth=1)
ax.set(xlabel="Fitted response", ylabel="Residual", title="Residual spread grows with fitted values")
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("lr_residual_diagnostics.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Outliers, leverage, and influence. A large response residual does not necessarily mean a predictor value is unusual. An observation with an extreme predictor can have high leverage; its influence measures how substantially the fitted model changes when it is removed. With one predictor and an intercept, \(h_{ii}=1/n+(x_i-\bar x)^2/S_{xx}\). Cook's distance combines leverage with the residual size. Rules of thumb such as \(4/n\) are screening aids, not automatic reasons to discard data.

Experiment 110: inspect leverage and Cook’s distance.

import numpy as np
import statsmodels.api as sm

rng = np.random.default_rng(110)
x = np.linspace(-2, 2, 36)
y = 1.5 + 0.7 * x + rng.normal(0, 0.7, size=x.size)
x[-1], y[-1] = 8.0, -6.0  # Intentionally influential observation

full = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()
without_last = sm.OLS(y[:-1], sm.add_constant(x[:-1])).fit()
influence = full.get_influence()
leverage = influence.hat_matrix_diag
cook = influence.cooks_distance[0]
index = int(np.argmax(cook))

print("Full-sample slope:", round(full.params[1], 4))
print("Slope without final observation:", round(without_last.params[1], 4))
print("Largest Cook's D at index:", index)
print("Its leverage and Cook's D:", round(leverage[index], 4), round(cook[index], 4))
print("Reference screening threshold 4/n:", round(4 / len(x), 4))

Theorem (mean-response and prediction intervals).

Under the classical independent normal-error model, let \(s=\sqrt{\mathrm{SSE}/(n-2)}\), \(\hat m(x_0)=\hat\beta_0+\hat\beta_1x_0\), and \(h_0=1/n+(x_0-\bar x)^2/S_{xx}\). With \(t^*=t_{n-2,1-\alpha/2}\), a \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval for the mean response \(\mathbb E(Y\mid x_0)\) is

\[\hat m(x_0)\pm t^*s\sqrt{h_0}.\]

A corresponding prediction interval for a new, independent response \(Y_{\mathrm{new}}\) at \(x_0\) is

\[\hat m(x_0)\pm t^*s\sqrt{1+h_0}.\]

The additional \(1\) under the prediction standard error accounts for the new observation's random error. Proof. The OLS mean predictor is unbiased with variance \(\sigma^2h_0\). For a new independent response, \(Y_{\mathrm{new}}-\hat m(x_0)\) has variance \(\sigma^2(1+h_0)\). Independence of the standardized normal errors and the residual variance estimator gives the two t-statistics with \(n-2\) degrees of freedom.

The intervals are pointwise, not simultaneous confidence bands over all \(x_0\), and extrapolation beyond the observed predictor range may be unreliable even when the formal calculations are possible.

Experiment 111: distinguish confidence and prediction intervals. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import statsmodels.api as sm

rng = np.random.default_rng(111)
x = rng.uniform(0, 8, size=65)
y = 4 + 1.35 * x + rng.normal(0, 2.2, size=x.size)
fit = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()
grid = np.linspace(0, 8, 180)
frame = fit.get_prediction(sm.add_constant(grid)).summary_frame(alpha=0.05)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, alpha=0.55, label="Observed")
ax.plot(grid, frame["mean"], label="Fitted mean")
ax.fill_between(grid, frame["mean_ci_lower"], frame["mean_ci_upper"],
                alpha=0.35, label="95% CI for mean")
ax.plot(grid, frame["obs_ci_lower"], linestyle="--", label="95% prediction bounds")
ax.plot(grid, frame["obs_ci_upper"], linestyle="--")
ax.set(xlabel="Predictor x", ylabel="Response y", title="Mean CI versus individual prediction interval")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("lr_prediction_intervals.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()
print(frame.loc[0, ["mean", "mean_ci_lower", "mean_ci_upper", "obs_ci_lower", "obs_ci_upper"]])

Prediction must be evaluated out of sample. OLS always minimizes training SSE for the chosen features, but excellent in-sample fit can coexist with poor generalization. Report performance on held-out data or with suitable cross-validation; avoid fitting transformations on the test set. Random train/test splitting assumes exchangeable observations, and is inappropriate for some chronological, grouped, or otherwise dependent datasets.

Experiment 112: compare training and test error with a baseline. 

import numpy as np
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.dummy import DummyRegressor
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error, r2_score

rng = np.random.default_rng(112)
x = rng.uniform(0, 10, 220)
y = 3 + 1.5 * x + 0.2 * x**2 + rng.normal(0, 2.0, x.size)
X = x.reshape(-1, 1)
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=112)

for name, model in [("Linear model", LinearRegression()),
                    ("Mean baseline", DummyRegressor(strategy="mean"))]:
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    for split, features, targets in [("train", X_train, y_train),
                                      ("test", X_test, y_test)]:
        pred = model.predict(features)
        rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(targets, pred))
        print(f"{name:13s} {split:5s}: RMSE={rmse:.3f}, R2={r2_score(targets, pred):.3f}")
print("The generating relationship is curved: inspect residuals before trusting extrapolation.")

Extension to multiple regression. With predictors \(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{ip}\), the conditional-mean model becomes \(\mathbb E(Y_i\mid x_{i1},\ldots,x_{ip})=\beta_0+\sum_{j=1}^{p}\beta_jx_{ij}\). In matrix notation, OLS minimizes \(\lVert y-Xb\rVert_2^2\); when \(X\) has full column rank, \(\hat\beta=(X^TX)^{-1}X^Ty\). Numerical software typically uses more stable solvers than explicitly computing that inverse. A multiple-regression coefficient is interpreted conditional on the other included predictors. Polynomial regression will develop expanded feature sets later.

Worked exercise. For \((x_i,y_i)=(1,2),(2,3),(3,5)\), show \(\bar x=2\), \(\bar y=10/3\), \(S_{xx}=2\), and \(S_{xy}=3\). Hence \(\hat\beta_1=3/2\) and \(\hat\beta_0=1/3\). Compute the residuals and verify that they sum to zero.

Further exercises. (a) Derive the normal equations and explain the role of \(S_{xx}>0\). (b) Give a counterexample where \(R^2\) is high but prediction is unsafe. (c) Simulate nonconstant error variance and compare residual plots. (d) Explain why a prediction interval is wider than a mean-response confidence interval.

References. DeGroot and Schervish, Probability and Statistics, 4th ed., Chapter 11, Sections 11.1–11.3 (printed pp. 689–717); OpenIntro Statistics, 4th ed., Chapter 8, Sections 8.1–8.3 (pp. 305–330), Exercises 8.17, 8.27–8.28. Documentation: statsmodels regression and scikit-learn linear models.

Inferences About the Slope

In the preceding section, we derived the least-squares coefficients and studied model diagnostics. Here the goal is inference about the unknown population slope \(\beta_1\), not merely computation of its observed estimate \(\hat\beta_1\). The original example of student height as predictor and weight as response will be retained.

Theorem (sampling distribution of the OLS slope).

Conditional on predictor values \(x_1,\ldots,x_n\), suppose

\[Y_i=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i+\varepsilon_i,\qquad \varepsilon_i\overset{\mathrm{ind}}\sim N(0,\sigma^2),\qquad S_{xx}>0.\]

Then

\[\hat\beta_1=\beta_1+\frac{\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)\varepsilon_i}{S_{xx}},\qquad \hat\beta_1\sim N\!\left(\beta_1,\frac{\sigma^2}{S_{xx}}\right).\]

With \(s^2=\mathrm{SSE}/(n-2)\) and \(n>2\), the estimated standard error is \(\widehat{\mathrm{SE}}(\hat\beta_1)=s/\sqrt{S_{xx}}\), and

\[T=\frac{\hat\beta_1-\beta_1}{s/\sqrt{S_{xx}}}\sim t_{n-2}.\]

Proof. Insert \(Y_i=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i+\varepsilon_i\) into the OLS expression \(\hat\beta_1=S_{xy}/S_{xx}\); the intercept term vanishes after centering. The numerator's noise component is a linear combination of independent normal variables, with variance \(\sigma^2S_{xx}\). Therefore the standardized slope is normal. In the classical normal model, \(\mathrm{SSE}/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{n-2}\) and is independent of the OLS coefficient vector. Their ratio has a Student t-distribution.

Without normality, the same OLS estimate can be useful but the exact t law need not hold. Correlated errors, endogeneity, nonlinearity, and heterogeneous error variance can invalidate the conventional standard errors and tests.

Definition (slope t-test and t-confidence interval).

To test \(H_0:\beta_1=\beta_{1,0}\) against a specified alternative, use

\[t_{\mathrm{obs}}=\frac{\hat\beta_1-\beta_{1,0}}{s/\sqrt{S_{xx}}},\qquad \nu=n-2.\]

For a two-sided alternative, \(p=2\,\mathbb P(t_{\nu}\geq |t_{\mathrm{obs}}|)\). For the alternatives \(\beta_1>\beta_{1,0}\) and \(\beta_1<\beta_{1,0}\), use the right and left t-tails, respectively. The two-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval is

\[\hat\beta_1\pm t_{\nu,1-\alpha/2}\frac{s}{\sqrt{S_{xx}}}.\]

For a two-sided test at level \(\alpha\), rejection is equivalent to exclusion of \(\beta_{1,0}\) from this corresponding confidence interval, provided the same model and standard error are used.

The null \(H_0:\beta_1=0\) says the conditional mean is constant in x within this specified simple linear model; it does not imply that \(X\) and \(Y\) are independent or that there is no nonlinear relationship. A small p-value is evidence against the zero-slope model under its assumptions, not a certificate of causation or predictive utility.

Experiment 113: calculate a slope test and interval manually. 

import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

x = np.arange(1., 10.)
y = np.array([2.2, 2.3, 3.7, 4.1, 5.0, 5.7, 6.4, 7.4, 7.8])
n = x.size
s_xx = np.sum((x - x.mean()) ** 2)
b1 = np.sum((x - x.mean()) * (y - y.mean())) / s_xx
b0 = y.mean() - b1 * x.mean()
residual = y - (b0 + b1 * x)
s = np.sqrt(np.sum(residual**2) / (n - 2))
se = s / np.sqrt(s_xx)
t_obs = b1 / se
p = 2 * stats.t.sf(abs(t_obs), df=n - 2)
t_star = stats.t.ppf(0.975, df=n - 2)
ci = (b1 - t_star * se, b1 + t_star * se)

print(f"Slope={b1:.5f}, SE={se:.5f}, t={t_obs:.5f}, p={p:.6g}")
print("95% CI:", tuple(round(v, 5) for v in ci))
print("SciPy linregress:", stats.linregress(x, y))

Applied example (student height and weight). The original notes considered a random sample of 12 students using SOGA-Py data with height and weight columns. The example below retains the original 12-row sampling procedure and random seed, removes incomplete pairs if any, and reports both the estimate and its uncertainty. The result describes an association in this sample; neither representativeness nor causality is established by the t-test.

Experiment 114: compare SciPy and statsmodels on the SOGA-Py height–weight sample. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import statsmodels.api as sm
from scipy.stats import linregress

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
students = pd.read_csv(url)
if len(students) < 12:
    raise ValueError("Fewer than 12 students in the source dataset")
sample = students.sample(n=12, random_state=9)[["height", "weight"]]
sample = sample.apply(pd.to_numeric, errors="coerce").dropna()
if len(sample) < 3:
    raise ValueError("Not enough complete pairs after the original 12-row sampling")
x = sample["height"].to_numpy()
y = sample["weight"].to_numpy()
fit = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()
scipy_fit = linregress(x, y)

print("Sample n:", len(sample))
print("Slope and estimated standard error:", fit.params[1], fit.bse[1])
print("Two-sided slope p-value:", fit.pvalues[1])
print("95% slope CI:", fit.conf_int(alpha=0.05)[1])
print("SciPy slope / p-value:", scipy_fit.slope, scipy_fit.pvalue)
print("Coefficients agree:", np.isclose(fit.params[1], scipy_fit.slope))

Coverage and Type I error. In repeated sampling under the classical assumptions, a nominal 95% slope interval covers the fixed population slope in 95% of samples. Equivalently, testing a true zero slope with its matching two-sided 5% t-test rejects in 5% of repeated samples. Neither statement describes the probability that the parameter lies inside one particular observed frequentist interval.

Experiment 115: simulate exact slope t-inference under normal errors. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats

rng = np.random.default_rng(115)
B, n, true_slope = 5000, 24, 0.0
x = np.linspace(-2, 2, n)
xc = x - x.mean()
s_xx = np.sum(xc**2)
errors = rng.normal(size=(B, n))
b1 = true_slope + errors @ xc / s_xx
b0_error = errors.mean(axis=1)
residuals = errors - b0_error[:, None] - (b1 - true_slope)[:, None] * xc
s = np.sqrt(np.sum(residuals**2, axis=1) / (n - 2))
se = s / np.sqrt(s_xx)
t_values = (b1 - true_slope) / se
critical = stats.t.ppf(0.975, n - 2)
coverage = np.mean(np.abs(t_values) <= critical)
rejection = np.mean(np.abs(t_values) > critical)
print(f"95% CI coverage={coverage:.4f}; null rejection={rejection:.4f}")

z = np.linspace(-4, 4, 400)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.hist(t_values, bins=60, density=True, alpha=0.5, label="Simulated slope t-statistic")
ax.plot(z, stats.t.pdf(z, n - 2), linewidth=2, label=f"t distribution, df={n-2}")
ax.set(xlabel="t-statistic", ylabel="Density", title="Exact normal-error sampling reference")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("slope_t_reference.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Robust standard errors. Under conditional heteroskedasticity, OLS coefficients may remain unbiased or consistent when the conditional-mean model is correctly specified, but the classical formula \(s/\sqrt{S_{xx}}\) no longer estimates the correct sampling variability. Heteroskedasticity-consistent estimators (HC0–HC3), particularly HC3 in small-to-moderate samples, can improve inference without changing the fitted OLS coefficients. They do not repair endogeneity, omitted-variable bias, dependence, or a misspecified conditional mean; their t-based p-values are generally approximate rather than finite-sample exact.

Experiment 116: compare conventional and HC3 tests under heteroskedasticity. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import statsmodels.api as sm

rng = np.random.default_rng(116)
B, n = 1000, 80
x = np.linspace(0, 1, n)
X = sm.add_constant(x)
spread = 0.2 + 3.0 * x  # True slope is zero; variance grows with x
count_classical = 0
count_hc3 = 0
for _ in range(B):
    y = rng.normal(0, spread)
    fit = sm.OLS(y, X).fit()
    count_classical += fit.pvalues[1] < 0.05
    count_hc3 += fit.get_robustcov_results(cov_type="HC3").pvalues[1] < 0.05
rates = [count_classical / B, count_hc3 / B]
print("Null rejection rates (nominal 5%):", dict(zip(["Classical", "HC3"], rates)))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 4))
ax.bar(["Classical", "HC3"], rates)
ax.axhline(0.05, linestyle="--", label="Nominal 5%")
ax.set(ylabel="Rejection probability", title="Heteroskedasticity and slope Type I error")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("slope_heteroskedastic_type1.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Effect magnitude, precision, and usefulness. Increasing the sample size reduces sampling uncertainty and can make a practically tiny population slope statistically distinguishable from zero. The effect magnitude must be expressed in the response's units per predictor unit; whether that effect matters depends on the application, error scale, and plausible intervention. For prediction, evaluate held-out error and prediction intervals in addition to p-values.

Experiment 117: contrast statistical and practical importance. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import linregress, t

rng = np.random.default_rng(117)
true_slope = 0.09  # 0.9 response units across the entire x-range [0, 10]
for n in [30, 2500]:
    x = rng.uniform(0, 10, n)
    y = 5 + true_slope * x + rng.normal(0, 3, n)
    result = linregress(x, y)
    half_width = t.ppf(0.975, n - 2) * result.stderr
    print(f"n={n}: slope={result.slope:.4f}, p={result.pvalue:.5g}, "
          f"95% CI=({result.slope-half_width:.4f}, {result.slope+half_width:.4f}), "
          f"R2={result.rvalue**2:.4f}")
print("Even a significant slope can account for little of the response variation.")

Lemma (slope–correlation identity).

In simple OLS regression with an intercept and nonzero sample standard deviations,

\[\hat\beta_1=r\frac{s_y}{s_x},\qquad R^2=r^2,\]

where \(r\) is the sample Pearson correlation. The first identity follows from \(S_{xy}/S_{xx}=[S_{xy}/\sqrt{S_{xx}S_{yy}}]\sqrt{S_{yy}/S_{xx}}\), and the second from \(\mathrm{SSR}=\hat\beta_1^2S_{xx}\). The next section uses these identities to relate slope and correlation tests, without duplicating OLS estimation.

Exercises. (a) Starting from the OLS formula, derive \(\operatorname{Var}(\hat\beta_1\mid X)=\sigma^2/S_{xx}\). (b) Explain precisely why the residual variance estimator has \(n-2\) degrees of freedom. (c) Re-run Experiment 116 with constant error variance and compare rejection rates. (d) Find a setting in which the zero-slope hypothesis holds although \(Y\) depends on \(X\).

References. DeGroot and Schervish, Probability and Statistics, 4th ed., Section 11.3 (printed pp. 707–717); OpenIntro Statistics, 4th ed., Section 8.4 (pp. 331–337), Exercises 8.33–8.35. Related Python interfaces: SciPy linregress, statsmodels robust covariance.

Inferences About the Linear Correlation Coefficient

Correlation describes association between variables and does not assign one variable the role of response. In the previous two sections, linear regression estimated a conditional-mean relationship and tested its slope. We now quantify the strength and direction of linear association and make inferences about population correlation.

Definition (population and sample Pearson correlation).

For random variables with positive finite variances, the population Pearson correlation is

\[\rho=\operatorname{Corr}(X,Y)=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)}{\sigma_X\sigma_Y}.\]

For nonconstant paired observations \((x_i,y_i)\), the sample correlation is

\[r=\frac{S_{xy}}{\sqrt{S_{xx}S_{yy}}},\quad S_{xy}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(y_i-\bar y),\quad S_{xx}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2,\quad S_{yy}=\sum_i(y_i-\bar y)^2.\]

Theorem (range and affine invariance).

For nonconstant samples, \(-1\leq r\leq1\). Under transformations \(x_i'=a+bx_i\) and \(y_i'=c+dy_i\) with \(b,d\neq0\),

\[r(x',y')=\operatorname{sign}(bd)\,r(x,y).\]

Thus correlation is unchanged by positive changes of units and changes sign when exactly one scale is reversed. The same properties hold for population correlations when the necessary variances exist.

Proof. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives \(S_{xy}^2\leq S_{xx}S_{yy}\), hence \(|r|\leq1\). Centered transformed observations are \(b(x_i-\bar x)\) and \(d(y_i-\bar y)\), so their cross-product sum scales by \(bd\) and the denominator by \(|bd|\).

Perfect positive or negative correlation means all observed points lie on a line with nonzero slope, but \(r=0\) only means the sample linear association vanishes. In general, uncorrelated does not imply independent. Even a large \(|r|\) does not by itself imply causation.

Experiment 118: calculate Pearson correlation and check its invariances. 

import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

rng = np.random.default_rng(118)
x = rng.normal(size=80)
y = 0.6 * x + np.sqrt(1 - 0.6**2) * rng.normal(size=x.size)
s_xx = np.sum((x - x.mean())**2)
s_yy = np.sum((y - y.mean())**2)
s_xy = np.sum((x - x.mean()) * (y - y.mean()))
r_manual = s_xy / np.sqrt(s_xx * s_yy)
r_scipy = stats.pearsonr(x, y).statistic
print(f"Manual r={r_manual:.6f}; SciPy r={r_scipy:.6f}")
print("After positive rescaling:", stats.pearsonr(5 + 3*x, -4 + 2*y).statistic)
print("After reversing y:", stats.pearsonr(x, -y).statistic)
print("Theoretical correlation in the generator: 0.6")

Zero correlation does not imply independence. For example, let \(X\sim\operatorname{Uniform}(-a,a)\) and \(Y=X^2\). By symmetry, \(\mathbb E(X)=\mathbb E(X^3)=0\), so \(\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=0\), even though \(Y\) is completely determined by \(X\). This counterexample remains instructive if independent measurement noise is added to \(Y\).

Experiment 119: visualize nonlinear dependence with small Pearson correlation. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import pearsonr, spearmanr

rng = np.random.default_rng(119)
x = np.linspace(-3, 3, 161)
y = x**2 + rng.normal(0, 0.35, size=x.size)
print("Pearson r and p:", pearsonr(x, y))
print("Spearman rho and p:", spearmanr(x, y))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, alpha=0.6)
ax.plot(x, x**2, label="Conditional mean y = x²")
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y", title="Strong nonlinear relationship, weak linear correlation")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("corr_nonlinear_dependence.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Theorem (exact Pearson test for zero correlation).

For an iid sample from a bivariate normal distribution with population correlation \(\rho\), under \(H_0:\rho=0\),

\[T=r\sqrt{\frac{n-2}{1-r^2}}\sim t_{n-2}\qquad(n>2,\ |r|<1).\]

For a two-sided alternative \(H_1:\rho\neq0\), the p-value is \(2\mathbb P(t_{n-2}\geq|t_{\mathrm{obs}}|)\). Under assumptions allowing the simple regression t-test, this is algebraically the same test as \(H_0:\beta_1=0\); the two procedures share the same p-value on the same complete paired observations.

The familiar t-reference is not generally an exact test for \(\rho=0\) under arbitrary nonnormal bivariate distributions. In particular, \(\rho=0\) need not imply independence. A permutation test that randomly reassigns the \(Y\) labels instead tests a stronger independence/exchangeability null, not every possible dependent distribution with zero correlation.

Definition (Fisher-z confidence interval for correlation).

When the pairs are iid bivariate normal, and for sufficiently large \(n\), Fisher's transformation \(z=\operatorname{arctanh}(r)=\frac12\log\frac{1+r}{1-r}\) is approximately normal with mean \(\operatorname{arctanh}(\rho)\) and variance \(1/(n-3)\). Therefore an approximate \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval for \(\rho\) is

\[\left[\tanh\!\left(z-\frac{z_{1-\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n-3}}\right),\quad \tanh\!\left(z+\frac{z_{1-\alpha/2}}{\sqrt{n-3}}\right)\right].\]

This interval is approximate, unlike the exact zero-correlation t-test under bivariate normality; it can perform poorly when \(n\) is small or normality is badly violated.

Experiment 120: compute Pearson p-values, Fisher confidence limits, and a permutation p-value. 

import numpy as np
from scipy import stats

rng = np.random.default_rng(120)
n = 65
x = rng.normal(size=n)
y = 0.42 * x + np.sqrt(1 - 0.42**2) * rng.normal(size=n)
r, p = stats.pearsonr(x, y)
t_obs = r * np.sqrt((n - 2) / (1 - r**2))
p_manual = 2 * stats.t.sf(abs(t_obs), n - 2)
z = np.arctanh(r)
half_width = stats.norm.ppf(0.975) / np.sqrt(n - 3)
ci = (np.tanh(z - half_width), np.tanh(z + half_width))

# Randomization is valid for the independence/exchangeability null.
B = 4000
x_c = x - x.mean()
y_c = y - y.mean()
den = np.linalg.norm(x_c) * np.linalg.norm(y_c)
permuted = np.array([np.dot(x_c, rng.permutation(y_c)) / den for _ in range(B)])
p_perm = (1 + np.count_nonzero(np.abs(permuted) >= abs(r))) / (B + 1)

print(f"r={r:.5f}; SciPy p={p:.5g}; t-reference p={p_manual:.5g}")
print("Approximate Fisher 95% CI:", tuple(round(v, 4) for v in ci))
print(f"Permutation p-value (independence null): {p_perm:.5g}")

Pearson versus Spearman. Pearson's \(r\) measures standardized linear association in the original values. Spearman's rank correlation \(r_s\) is Pearson correlation applied to ranks (with average ranks for ties); it measures monotonic association and is invariant to strictly increasing transformations when there are no ties. It can respond differently to outliers and nonlinear monotonic relationships. Inferential p-values for rank tests depend on their own assumptions and approximations.

Experiment 121: contrast Pearson and Spearman under a nonlinear monotonic relationship. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import pearsonr, spearmanr

rng = np.random.default_rng(121)
x = rng.uniform(0, 3, 120)
y = np.exp(x) + rng.normal(0, 0.2, x.size)
print("Pearson:", pearsonr(x, y))
print("Spearman:", spearmanr(x, y))
print("Pearson after nonlinear rescaling of x:", pearsonr(np.exp(x), y))
print("Spearman after strictly increasing rescaling of x:", spearmanr(np.exp(x), y))

Outliers and confounding. Pearson correlation can be greatly changed by one unusual pair. Pooling data from different groups can also produce a strong marginal correlation even when the within-group correlations are near zero. Such differences can arise because group membership influences both variables. Report scatterplots, subgroup analyses, sample size, and meaningful uncertainty—not merely a correlation and p-value.

Experiment 122: demonstrate confounding and sensitivity to a single outlier. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import pearsonr

rng = np.random.default_rng(122)
n_per_group = 150
group = np.repeat([0, 1], n_per_group)
x = 3 * group + rng.normal(size=group.size)
y = 3 * group + rng.normal(size=group.size)
print("Pooled Pearson r:", round(pearsonr(x, y).statistic, 4))
for g in [0, 1]:
    print(f"Within group {g} r:", round(pearsonr(x[group == g], y[group == g]).statistic, 4))

u = rng.normal(size=80)
v = rng.normal(size=80)
print("Independent-looking sample r:", round(pearsonr(u, v).statistic, 4))
u[-1], v[-1] = 20.0, 20.0
print("After replacing one pair by (20,20):", round(pearsonr(u, v).statistic, 4))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
for g in [0, 1]:
    ax.scatter(x[group == g], y[group == g], alpha=0.6, label=f"Group {g}")
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y", title="Group shifts create a pooled correlation")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("corr_confounding_groups.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Applied example (two exam scores). The original notes used score1 and score2 from the SOGA-Py students dataset, selecting 50 complete pairs with a fixed seed. We retain that analysis, add a confidence interval, and compare rank correlation. High correlation of scores is compatible with shared background factors such as prior preparation; it does not establish that performance on one exam causes performance on the other.

Experiment 123: analyze students’ two statistics-exam scores. 

import pandas as pd
from scipy.stats import pearsonr, spearmanr

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
students = pd.read_csv(url)
pairs = students[["score1", "score2"]].apply(pd.to_numeric, errors="coerce").dropna()
if len(pairs) < 50:
    raise ValueError("Fewer than 50 complete paired exam scores")
sample = pairs.sample(n=50, random_state=9)
x = sample["score1"].to_numpy()
y = sample["score2"].to_numpy()
result = pearsonr(x, y)
rank_result = spearmanr(x, y)
print("Pearson r and two-sided p-value:", result)
print("Approximate 95% Pearson confidence interval:",
      result.confidence_interval(confidence_level=0.95))
print("Spearman rank correlation and p-value:", rank_result)
print("n:", len(sample))

Theorem (equivalence of zero-slope and zero-correlation tests).

For nonconstant paired data in simple OLS regression with an intercept,

\[\hat\beta_1=r\sqrt{\frac{S_{yy}}{S_{xx}}},\qquad R^2=r^2,\qquad \frac{\hat\beta_1}{\widehat{\operatorname{SE}}(\hat\beta_1)} =r\sqrt{\frac{n-2}{1-r^2}}.\]

Consequently, the usual two-sided zero-slope t-test and the zero-Pearson-correlation t-test yield the same computed statistic and p-value for the same \(n\) complete observations. Whether the common p-value is valid requires appropriate model assumptions. This equivalence pertains to simple regression with an intercept and does not apply unchanged to partial slopes in multiple regression.

Proof. The first identity follows by rewriting \(S_{xy}/S_{xx}\). For OLS with an intercept, \(\mathrm{SSE}=S_{yy}(1-r^2)\); hence \(s^2=S_{yy}(1-r^2)/(n-2)\). Substitute this into \(\widehat{\operatorname{SE}}(\hat\beta_1)=s/\sqrt{S_{xx}}\), and simplify. These are algebraic identities, not distributional assumptions.

Worked exercise. Suppose \(n=22\) and \(r=0.50\). Then \(t=0.50\sqrt{20/(1-0.25)}\approx2.582\), with \(20\) degrees of freedom. Calculate the two-sided p-value with SciPy, and explain which conditions are required to use the t-reference for a population correlation test.

Further exercises. (a) Prove that \(\operatorname{Corr}(X,X^2)=0\) for a symmetric random variable with suitable finite moments. (b) Explore how a single high-leverage pair changes Pearson's \(r\) and Spearman's rank correlation. (c) Construct a dataset with positive marginal correlation and near-zero within-group correlations. (d) Explain why a permutation test for independence is not automatically a valid test of the composite hypothesis \(\rho=0\) in arbitrary dependent distributions.

References. OpenIntro Statistics, 4th ed., Sections 8.1 and 8.4 (pp. 305–311, 331–337), Exercises 8.13 and 8.33; DeGroot and Schervish, 4th ed., Chapter 4 (covariance and correlation) and Chapter 11, Sections 11.2–11.3. Python documentation: SciPy pearsonr and SciPy spearmanr.

Polynomial Regression

Polynomial regression extends simple linear regression by allowing the conditional mean of a numerical response to change nonlinearly with a predictor. It retains the least-squares framework developed in the Linear Regression section, but expands the feature space from a straight line to polynomial curves. The central problem is no longer only how to estimate coefficients: it is also how much model complexity the available data can support.

For a concrete data-science application, imagine predicting a measurement or a continuous outcome from an explanatory variable when a scatterplot shows curvature. A high-degree curve may reproduce the training observations impressively while making poor predictions on new data. We will study both the mathematical model and the procedures used to detect and control that failure.

Definition (polynomial regression).

For a single predictor and a degree fixed in advance, the degree-\(d\) regression model is

\[Y_i=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i+\beta_2x_i^2+\cdots+\beta_dx_i^d+\varepsilon_i, \qquad \mathbb E(\varepsilon_i\mid x_i)=0.\]

Equivalently, the conditional mean is \(m(x)=\mathbb E(Y\mid X=x)=\sum_{j=0}^{d}\beta_jx^j\). The model is nonlinear in the predictor but linear in the unknown coefficients \(\beta_j\). Consequently, polynomial regression is a special case of the general linear model, not an example of nonlinear least squares in the parameters.

For \(d=1\) we recover the usual straight line. For \(d=2\), \(m(x)=\beta_0+\beta_1x+\beta_2x^2\) is a parabola. For \(d=3\), the curve is cubic and may change curvature. The polynomial basis provides one way of representing a curved regression function; it does not imply that the physical relationship is intrinsically polynomial.

Theorem (least-squares polynomial fit).

Let \(X\) be the \(n\times(d+1)\) design matrix with \(i\)-th row \((1,x_i,x_i^2,\ldots,x_i^d)\), and let \(y=(y_1,\ldots,y_n)^T\). The least-squares problem is

\[\hat\beta\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{b\in\mathbb R^{d+1}} \|y-Xb\|_2^2.\]

Every minimizer satisfies the normal equations \(X^TX\hat\beta=X^Ty\). If \(X\) has full column rank, the minimizer is unique and

\[\hat\beta=(X^TX)^{-1}X^Ty,\qquad \hat y=X\hat\beta.\]

A degree-\(d\) Vandermonde design with \(d+1\) or more distinct predictor values has full column rank.

Proof. Expanding \(\|y-Xb\|^2\) and differentiating gives the gradient \(-2X^T(y-Xb)\), which vanishes at a minimum. Full column rank makes \(X^TX\) positive definite, so the quadratic objective has a unique minimizer. For distinct \(x_i\), a polynomial of degree at most \(d\) that vanishes at \(d+1\) distinct inputs is identically zero, proving linear independence of the design columns.

Computational caution. The inverse formula explains the mathematics; it is not a recommended numerical implementation. Use a QR- or SVD-based least-squares solver such as numpy.linalg.lstsq or sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression. Powers of an unscaled predictor can become badly conditioned.

Experiment 124: fit a quadratic polynomial from its design matrix. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures

rng = np.random.default_rng(124)
x = np.linspace(-2, 2, 45)
y = 1.5 - 0.8*x + 1.2*x**2 + rng.normal(0, 0.7, x.size)

# Vandermonde matrix: columns 1, x, x^2.
X = np.column_stack((np.ones_like(x), x, x**2))
coef, residual_sums, rank, singular_values = np.linalg.lstsq(
    X, y, rcond=None)
print("Quadratic coefficients [intercept, x, x^2]:", np.round(coef, 4))
print("Design rank:", rank)

# The same model using a feature transformer and an OLS estimator.
features = PolynomialFeatures(degree=2, include_bias=False)
XP = features.fit_transform(x.reshape(-1, 1))
model = LinearRegression().fit(XP, y)
print("scikit-learn coefficients:",
      np.round(np.r_[model.intercept_, model.coef_], 4))
assert np.allclose(np.r_[model.intercept_, model.coef_], coef)

grid = np.linspace(-2.2, 2.2, 300)
grid_X = np.column_stack((np.ones_like(grid), grid, grid**2))
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, s=24, alpha=0.7, label="Observed sample")
ax.plot(grid, grid_X @ coef, linewidth=2, label="OLS quadratic")
ax.plot(grid, 1.5 - 0.8*grid + 1.2*grid**2,
        linestyle="--", label="True conditional mean")
ax.set(xlabel="Predictor x", ylabel="Response y",
       title="Polynomial regression is linear in its coefficients")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_quadratic_fit.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Lemma (training SSE cannot increase as degree increases).

Let \(\mathrm{SSE}_d\) be the minimum sum of squared residuals among all polynomials of degree at most \(d\). Then

\[\mathrm{SSE}_{d+1}\leq\mathrm{SSE}_d.\]

Proof. Every polynomial of degree at most \(d\) is also a polynomial of degree at most \(d+1\) (set its highest coefficient to zero). Minimizing over the larger set cannot give a larger minimum.

This nesting property makes training error a poor standalone criterion for selecting polynomial degree. As flexibility grows, a model can fit sampling noise rather than stable features of the conditional mean. Underfitting occurs when the chosen function class is too restrictive; overfitting occurs when the fitted model reacts too strongly to sample-specific noise. Evaluation on observations not used for fitting is needed to assess predictive generalization.

Parameter count. An univariate degree-\(d\) polynomial has \(d+1\) coefficients. When \(d\geq n-1\) and all inputs are distinct, a degree-\(n-1\) polynomial can interpolate all \(n\) observations, giving zero training residuals even when the observed responses contain noise. This does not imply a good population model.

Experiment 125: compare underfitting, useful complexity, and overfitting. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

rng = np.random.default_rng(125)
def mean_curve(x):
    return 1 + 1.3*x - 1.8*x**2 + 0.8*x**3

x_train = np.sort(rng.uniform(-1, 1, 28))
y_train = mean_curve(x_train) + rng.normal(0, 0.28, x_train.size)
x_test = rng.uniform(-1, 1, 1000)
y_test = mean_curve(x_test) + rng.normal(0, 0.28, x_test.size)
grid = np.linspace(-1, 1, 400)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x_train, y_train, s=28, label="Training observations")
ax.plot(grid, mean_curve(grid), linestyle="--", label="True mean")
for degree in (1, 3, 16):
    model = make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(degree, include_bias=False),
                          LinearRegression())
    model.fit(x_train[:, None], y_train)
    train_rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(
        y_train, model.predict(x_train[:, None])))
    test_rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(
        y_test, model.predict(x_test[:, None])))
    print(f"Degree {degree:2d}: train RMSE={train_rmse:.3f}, "
          f"independent test RMSE={test_rmse:.3f}")
    ax.plot(grid, model.predict(grid[:, None]),
            label=f"Degree {degree}")
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y", ylim=(-4, 5),
       title="Training fit and polynomial complexity")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_degree_overfit.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

For several predictors, polynomial features can also include cross-products. For example, two predictors and degree two produce the basis \(1,x_1,x_2,x_1^2,x_1x_2,x_2^2\). The \(x_1x_2\) term is an interaction: its coefficient allows the association between one predictor and the response to vary with the other predictor. Adding polynomial and interaction features still yields a linear-in-parameters regression model.

For \(p\) original predictors, the number of monomials with total degree at most \(d\), including the constant term, is \(\binom{p+d}{d}\). This can grow rapidly. In scikit-learn, PolynomialFeatures(include_bias=False) omits the column of ones because LinearRegression() already fits an intercept by default.

Experiment 126: construct a multivariable polynomial feature matrix with an interaction. 

import numpy as np
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.model_selection import KFold, cross_val_score

rng = np.random.default_rng(126)
X = rng.uniform(-1, 1, size=(180, 2))
y = 2 + X[:, 0] - 0.7*X[:, 1] + 3*X[:, 0]*X[:, 1]
y = y + rng.normal(0, 0.25, len(y))

poly = PolynomialFeatures(degree=2, include_bias=False)
XP = poly.fit_transform(X)
print("Feature names:", poly.get_feature_names_out(["x1", "x2"]))
print("Feature-matrix shape:", XP.shape)

cv = KFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=126)
for title, estimator in [
    ("Main effects only", LinearRegression()),
    ("Quadratic with interactions",
     make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(2, include_bias=False),
                   LinearRegression()))
]:
    scores = cross_val_score(estimator, X, y, cv=cv,
                             scoring="neg_mean_squared_error")
    print(f"{title}: cross-validated RMSE="
          f"{np.sqrt(-scores).mean():.3f}")

Definition (validation and cross-validation).

In supervised prediction, the training set estimates model parameters; validation data compare candidate models or hyperparameters; an independent test set is reserved for a final evaluation of the chosen procedure. In \(K\)-fold cross-validation, training data are partitioned into \(K\) folds, and each fold is predicted using a model trained on the other \(K-1\) folds.

For a regression outcome, one convenient criterion is the root mean squared error, \(\mathrm{RMSE}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(y_i-\hat y_i)^2}\), evaluated on observations not used to fit the corresponding model. Compare candidate degrees on the same splits. In a preprocessing pipeline, transformations learned from data must be fitted separately within each training fold. A completely untouched test set avoids reporting the optimistically selected minimum validation error as though it were independent evidence.

These random-split procedures are appropriate only when observations are reasonably independent and exchangeable for the prediction task. For time-ordered, grouped, or repeated-measures data, use an appropriate temporal or grouped split instead.

Experiment 127: select polynomial degree by cross-validation and evaluate a held-out test set. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split, KFold, cross_validate
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

rng = np.random.default_rng(127)
x = rng.uniform(-1.5, 1.5, 130)
y = 1 + 0.5*x - 1.3*x**2 + rng.normal(0, 0.5, len(x))
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    x[:, None], y, test_size=0.25, random_state=127)
cv = KFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=127)

degrees = range(1, 13)
train_rmse, validation_rmse = [], []
for degree in degrees:
    model = make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(degree, include_bias=False),
                          LinearRegression())
    result = cross_validate(model, X_train, y_train, cv=cv,
                            scoring="neg_mean_squared_error",
                            return_train_score=True)
    train_rmse.append(np.sqrt(-result["train_score"]).mean())
    validation_rmse.append(np.sqrt(-result["test_score"]).mean())

best_degree = degrees[int(np.argmin(validation_rmse))]
final_model = make_pipeline(
    PolynomialFeatures(best_degree, include_bias=False),
    LinearRegression()).fit(X_train, y_train)
held_out_rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(
    y_test, final_model.predict(X_test)))
print("Selected polynomial degree:", best_degree)
print(f"CV RMSE (used for selection): {min(validation_rmse):.3f}")
print(f"Independent test RMSE (reported once): {held_out_rmse:.3f}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.plot(list(degrees), train_rmse, marker="o", label="CV training folds")
ax.plot(list(degrees), validation_rmse, marker="o", label="CV validation folds")
ax.axvline(best_degree, linestyle="--", label="Selected degree")
ax.set(xlabel="Polynomial degree", ylabel="Average fold RMSE",
       title="Select degree using training-set cross-validation")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_cv_degree.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (Ridge-penalized polynomial regression).

Let \(z_j(x)\) denote a chosen polynomial feature, and let \(b_0\) be an unpenalized intercept. Ridge regression estimates coefficients by minimizing

\[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_i-b_0-\sum_{j=1}^{q}b_jz_j(x_i)\right)^2 +\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{q}b_j^2,\qquad \lambda\geq0.\]

For \(\lambda=0\), this reduces to ordinary least squares; positive \(\lambda\) penalizes large non-intercept coefficients. Standardizing nonconstant polynomial features makes the penalty comparable across feature scales. The intercept is typically not penalized.

Ridge regularization generally introduces bias into coefficient estimates but can reduce prediction variance and improve predictive error. It does not guarantee that a polynomial model is scientifically appropriate or that extrapolation is reliable. Degree and regularization strength are both hyperparameters, ideally selected inside a validation workflow.

Experiment 128: control a high-degree polynomial using Ridge regularization. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures, StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression, Ridge
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

rng = np.random.default_rng(128)
def true_mean(x):
    return 0.6 + 1.2*x - 0.7*x**2 + 0.2*np.sin(3*x)

x_train = np.sort(rng.uniform(-1, 1, 32))
y_train = true_mean(x_train) + rng.normal(0, 0.23, len(x_train))
x_test = rng.uniform(-1, 1, 1000)
y_test = true_mean(x_test) + rng.normal(0, 0.23, len(x_test))
grid = np.linspace(-1, 1, 350)

models = {
    "High-degree OLS": make_pipeline(
        PolynomialFeatures(16, include_bias=False),
        StandardScaler(), LinearRegression()),
    "High-degree Ridge": make_pipeline(
        PolynomialFeatures(16, include_bias=False),
        StandardScaler(), Ridge(alpha=1.0))
}
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x_train, y_train, s=25, label="Training observations")
ax.plot(grid, true_mean(grid), linestyle="--", label="True mean")
for label, model in models.items():
    model.fit(x_train[:, None], y_train)
    rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(
        y_test, model.predict(x_test[:, None])))
    print(f"{label}: independent test RMSE={rmse:.3f}")
    ax.plot(grid, model.predict(grid[:, None]), label=label)
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y", ylim=(-2, 3),
       title="Ridge shrinks a high-degree polynomial fit")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_ridge_regularization.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Feature scaling and numerical stability. With raw predictors measured on a large scale, columns \(x,x^2,\ldots,x^d\) can differ by many orders of magnitude and become highly correlated. The numerical condition number \(\kappa_2(X)=\sigma_{\max}(X)/\sigma_{\min}(X)\) summarizes sensitivity to perturbations; large values warn of unstable coefficient estimates. The normal-equation matrix may be much worse conditioned than the design itself. Centering and rescaling \(x\), using a stable solver, lowering degree, or adopting alternative polynomial bases can help.

Coefficient interpretation. Unlike the constant slope in simple linear regression, a fitted polynomial's marginal change is location dependent:

\[m'(x)=\beta_1+2\beta_2x+3\beta_3x^2+\cdots+d\beta_dx^{d-1}.\]

Thus \(\beta_1\) alone is not the total effect of increasing \(x\) by one unit when higher powers are present. The value of the derivative depends on \(x\); an observational derivative is not automatically a causal effect.

Experiment 129: compare raw and centered polynomial design matrices. 

import numpy as np

rng = np.random.default_rng(129)
x = np.linspace(95, 105, 60)
z = (x - 100) / 5  # centered, dimensionless feature
true_y = 4 + 0.6*z - 1.2*z**2 + 0.3*z**3
y = true_y + rng.normal(0, 0.12, len(x))

# Both designs span the same polynomial functions in exact arithmetic.
X_raw = np.vander(x, N=7, increasing=True)
X_scaled = np.vander(z, N=7, increasing=True)
for name, design in [("Raw x", X_raw), ("Centered/scaled x", X_scaled)]:
    cond = np.linalg.cond(design)
    coefficients = np.linalg.lstsq(design, y, rcond=None)[0]
    fitted = design @ coefficients
    print(f"{name:18s}: condition number={cond:.3e}, "
          f"SSE={np.sum((y - fitted)**2):.4f}, "
          f"numerical rank={np.linalg.matrix_rank(design)}")
print("A coordinate change preserves the polynomial space in exact arithmetic,")
print("but a poorly scaled numerical design can lose effective rank.")

Theorem (nested-model F-test for a quadratic term).

Consider the prespecified nested models

\[M_R:\ \mathbb E(Y_i\mid x_i)=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i,\qquad M_F:\ \mathbb E(Y_i\mid x_i)=\beta_0+\beta_1x_i+\beta_2x_i^2.\]

If the independent errors are normal with common variance \(\sigma^2\), the full design has rank three, \(n>3\), and the null hypothesis \(H_0:\beta_2=0\) is true, then

\[F=\frac{(\operatorname{SSE}_R-\operatorname{SSE}_F)/1} {\operatorname{SSE}_F/(n-3)}\sim F_{1,n-3}.\]

The numerator measures the reduction in residual sum of squares obtained by adding the quadratic feature; the denominator estimates error variance from the full model.

Proof sketch. Under the normal full model and \(H_0\), the orthogonal projections onto the reduced and full regression spaces give independent scaled chi-square sums of squares: \((\mathrm{SSE}_R-\mathrm{SSE}_F)/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_1\) and \(\mathrm{SSE}_F/\sigma^2\sim\chi^2_{n-3}\). Their ratio, divided by the corresponding degrees of freedom, is F-distributed.

Selection warning. This classical reference distribution presumes the compared models were chosen independently of the observed noise. If polynomial degree was searched using the same data before reporting the most significant coefficient or F-test, the naive p-values and intervals generally fail to account for that selection. Separate exploratory model selection from confirmatory inference.

Experiment 130: test a prespecified quadratic term against a straight-line model. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy import stats
import statsmodels.api as sm

rng = np.random.default_rng(130)
x = np.linspace(-2, 2, 90)
y = 2 + 0.7*x + 1.1*x**2 + rng.normal(0, 1.0, len(x))

reduced = sm.OLS(y, sm.add_constant(x)).fit()
full = sm.OLS(y, np.column_stack((np.ones(len(x)), x, x**2))).fit()
sse_r = np.sum(reduced.resid**2)
sse_f = np.sum(full.resid**2)
F = ((sse_r - sse_f)/1) / (sse_f/(len(x) - 3))
p_value = stats.f.sf(F, 1, len(x) - 3)
print(f"Reduced SSE={sse_r:.3f}; full SSE={sse_f:.3f}")
print(f"Quadratic-term F={F:.4f}; p-value={p_value:.5g}")
print("statsmodels comparison (F, p, df difference):",
      full.compare_f_test(reduced))
print("Quadratic coefficient 95% CI:",
      np.round(full.conf_int()[2], 4))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, s=18, alpha=0.65, label="Observed responses")
ax.plot(x, reduced.fittedvalues, label="Reduced: degree 1")
ax.plot(x, full.fittedvalues, label="Full: degree 2")
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="y",
       title="Prespecified comparison of nested regression models")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_nested_f_test.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Extrapolation. Within the range of observed predictors, smooth polynomial curves may approximate the conditional mean usefully. Outside that range, polynomial terms with high powers can dominate, and small coefficient errors can produce extreme predictions. A narrow in-sample confidence band does not justify extrapolating the functional form into an unobserved region. Even a correctly computed interval is conditional on model assumptions that are especially hard to justify outside the training domain.

Experiment 131: visualize uncertainty in extrapolation beyond the observed range. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression

rng = np.random.default_rng(131)
x = np.sort(rng.uniform(-1, 1, 45))
y = 0.5 + 0.4*x + 1.6*x**2 + rng.normal(0, 0.22, len(x))
grid = np.linspace(-2, 2, 500)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.axvspan(-1, 1, alpha=0.10, label="Training x range")
ax.scatter(x, y, s=22, label="Observed training data")
for degree in (2, 5, 10):
    model = make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(degree, include_bias=False),
                          LinearRegression()).fit(x[:, None], y)
    pred = model.predict(grid[:, None])
    ax.plot(grid, pred, label=f"Degree {degree}")
ax.set(xlabel="x", ylabel="Predicted y", ylim=(-4, 13),
       title="Polynomial extrapolation outside the observed domain")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_extrapolation.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Applied example: predicting student weight from height. We return to the SOGA-Py students data used in earlier regression sections, and compare a straight line with quadratic and cubic alternatives. The response is weight and the predictor is height. This is a descriptive and predictive exercise, not an explanation of why students have particular weights. An improved test RMSE does not establish a causal relationship.

The code attempts to load the external data. When the download is unavailable, it explicitly switches to a simulated demonstration dataset, so the example and figure remain runnable offline. Synthetic fallback results must not be presented as empirical findings about students.

Experiment 132: compare polynomial models on the SOGA-Py student dataset. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import KFold, train_test_split, cross_val_score
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import PolynomialFeatures
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
try:
    students = pd.read_csv(url)
    data_source = "SOGA-Py students (external dataset)"
except Exception as error:
    print("External data unavailable; using a simulated example instead:",
          type(error).__name__)
    rng = np.random.default_rng(132)
    height = rng.normal(171, 9, 450)
    weight = 68 + 0.60*(height - 171) + 0.012*(height - 171)**2
    weight += rng.normal(0, 7.5, height.size)
    students = pd.DataFrame({"height": height, "weight": weight})
    data_source = "SIMULATED DEMONSTRATION DATA"

clean = students[["height", "weight"]].apply(
    pd.to_numeric, errors="coerce").dropna()
if len(clean) < 25:
    raise ValueError("Need at least 25 complete height/weight observations")
X = clean[["height"]].to_numpy()
y = clean["weight"].to_numpy()
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=132)
cv = KFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=132)

models = {}
for degree in (1, 2, 3):
    model = make_pipeline(PolynomialFeatures(degree, include_bias=False),
                          LinearRegression())
    fold_rmse = np.sqrt(-cross_val_score(
        model, X_train, y_train, cv=cv,
        scoring="neg_mean_squared_error"))
    models[degree] = (fold_rmse.mean(), model)

selected_degree = min(models, key=lambda d: models[d][0])
selected_model = models[selected_degree][1].fit(X_train, y_train)
test_rmse = np.sqrt(mean_squared_error(
    y_test, selected_model.predict(X_test)))
print("Data source:", data_source, "| complete observations:", len(clean))
for degree, (cv_rmse, _) in models.items():
    print(f"Degree {degree}: 5-fold CV RMSE={cv_rmse:.3f}")
print(f"Selected degree: {selected_degree}; held-out RMSE: {test_rmse:.3f}")

height_grid = np.linspace(X.min(), X.max(), 250).reshape(-1, 1)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(X_train[:, 0], y_train, s=13, alpha=0.35,
           label="Training observations")
ax.scatter(X_test[:, 0], y_test, s=18, alpha=0.50,
           label="Held-out observations")
ax.plot(height_grid[:, 0], selected_model.predict(height_grid),
        linewidth=2, label=f"Selected degree {selected_degree}")
ax.set(xlabel="Height", ylabel="Weight",
       title=f"Height-weight regression: {data_source}")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("poly_students_height_weight.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Practical checklist. Before fitting a polynomial, inspect the scatterplot and confirm that the response is numerical. Decide whether the aim is explanation, inference, or prediction. Establish a simple baseline and choose an evaluation split appropriate to the data collection process. Fit feature transformations inside the cross-validation pipeline; compare a small, reasonable set of degrees and regularization strengths; check residual patterns and the stability of estimates.
