Report performance on the untouched test set and avoid unsupported extrapolation. Remember that all results describe associations unless a design supports causal interpretation.

Worked exercise (derivative and turning point). Suppose \(\hat m(x)=3+2x-x^2\). Then \(\hat m'(x)=2-2x\), so the curve increases for \(x<1\), reaches its maximum at \(x=1\), and decreases for \(x>1\). The fitted peak is \(\hat m(1)=4\). This describes the shape of the fitted function, not a guaranteed property of the population function.

Further exercises. (1) Prove that \(\mathrm{SSE}_{d+1}\leq\mathrm{SSE}_d\) and give a counterexample to the claim that the same holds for test error. (2) Show that a degree-\(n-1\) polynomial interpolates \(n\) observations at distinct predictor values, and explain why this may be undesirable. (3) Reproduce the \(n=10\) parabola example in DeGroot and Schervish, Section 11.1, Example 11.1.3, and compare its fitted curve with the straight line. (4) In Experiment 127, change the random seed and assess how stable the selected degree is; explain why selecting the minimum CV RMSE makes that minimum optimistic. (5) In Experiment 128, vary alpha on a logarithmic grid using cross-validation; compare the test RMSE and coefficient norms. (6) Derive the F-test for adding two polynomial terms simultaneously, including numerator and denominator degrees of freedom. (7) In Experiment 132, interpret the predicted weight differences over a chosen height range, clearly separating predictive associations from causal claims.

References. DeGroot and Schervish, Probability and Statistics, 4th ed., Section 11.1, printed pp. 693–695, treats polynomial least squares and includes Examples 11.1.3–11.1.4; Exercises 10–11 appear on p. 698. Section 11.5, beginning p. 739, develops the general linear model, which also applies to polynomial feature designs. OpenIntro Statistics, 4th ed., Section 8.2 (least-squares regression) and Section 9.2 (model selection), pp. 353–358, provide useful related reading; cross-validation and Ridge are additional modern predictive-modeling topics. Implementation references: PolynomialFeatures, scikit-learn pipelines, and cross_validate.

Logistic Regression

Linear and polynomial regression model the conditional mean of a numerical response. We now consider a binary response, such as whether a customer renews a subscription, a transaction is fraudulent, or an applicant receives a callback. A binary outcome is encoded as \(Y\in\{0,1\}\), where the meaning of 1 must be stated explicitly.

The goal of binary logistic regression is first to estimate a probability \(P(Y=1\mid X=x)\), not to assign a class. A classification rule is a separate decision that turns this probability into 0 or 1 using a threshold. Keeping probability estimation, statistical inference, and decision-making distinct will be important throughout the section.

Definition (binary logistic regression).

For observations \((x_i,Y_i)\), assume that, conditionally on the predictors, the outcomes are independent Bernoulli variables:

\[Y_i\mid x_i\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p_i),\qquad P(Y_i=1\mid x_i)=p_i,\quad 0<p_i<1.\]

For a predictor vector \(x_i=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{ik})^T\), the model specifies a linear predictor \(\eta_i=\beta_0+\sum_{j=1}^k\beta_j x_{ij}\) and the logit link

\[\operatorname{logit}(p_i)=\log\!\left(\frac{p_i}{1-p_i}\right)=\eta_i.\]

Equivalently, with \(\sigma(t)=1/(1+e^{-t})\), the success probability is \(p_i=\sigma(\eta_i)\). This is a Bernoulli generalized linear model (GLM) with canonical link.

Unlike ordinary least squares applied directly to \(Y\in\{0,1\}\), the logistic model always gives probabilities between zero and one. Since \(\mathbb E(Y_i\mid x_i)=p_i\) and \(\operatorname{Var}(Y_i\mid x_i)=p_i(1-p_i)\), the conditional variance is not constant. A Gaussian, constant-variance error model for the binary response is therefore inappropriate.

Lemma (properties of the logistic function).

The function \(\sigma:\mathbb R\to(0,1)\) is strictly increasing and satisfies \(\sigma(-t)=1-\sigma(t)\), \(\sigma(0)=1/2\), and

\[\sigma'(t)=\sigma(t)[1-\sigma(t)]\leq\frac14.\]

Proof. Differentiation gives \(\sigma'(t)=e^{-t}/(1+e^{-t})^2\). Substitute \(\sigma(t)=1/(1+e^{-t})\); the bound follows from \(p(1-p)\leq1/4\) for \(0\leq p\leq1\).

For one predictor, \(p(x)=\sigma(\beta_0+\beta_1x)\) describes an S-shaped probability curve. Positive \(\beta_1\) increases the probability with \(x\), negative \(\beta_1\) decreases it, and \(\beta_1=0\) produces a constant probability. The probability crosses \(1/2\) at \(x=-\beta_0/\beta_1\) when \(\beta_1\ne0\). Its rate of change is \(dp/dx=\beta_1p(1-p)\), so a unit change in \(x\) does not generally produce a constant change in probability.

Experiment 133: visualize the logistic link and simulate binary responses. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.special import expit

rng = np.random.default_rng(133)
intercept, slope = -0.8, 1.4
x = rng.uniform(-3, 3, size=180)
p = expit(intercept + slope*x)
y = rng.binomial(n=1, p=p)

grid = np.linspace(-3.2, 3.2, 400)
probability = expit(intercept + slope*grid)
print("Probability at x=0:", round(expit(intercept), 4))
print("Probability at x=1:", round(expit(intercept + slope), 4))
print("Probability-one-half boundary:", round(-intercept/slope, 4))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y + rng.uniform(-0.035, 0.035, len(y)), s=14,
           alpha=0.40, label="Binary observations (jittered)")
ax.plot(grid, probability, linewidth=2.3, label="True logistic probability")
ax.axhline(0.5, linestyle="--", linewidth=1, label="Probability 0.5")
ax.axvline(-intercept/slope, linestyle=":", linewidth=1)
ax.set(xlabel="Predictor x", ylabel="P(Y=1 | x)", ylim=(-0.1, 1.1),
       title="Binary outcomes and a continuous probability model")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_sigmoid_probability.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (odds, log-odds, and odds ratio).

For an event with probability \(p\), its odds are \(p/(1-p)\), and its log-odds are \(\log[p/(1-p)]\). In a logistic model, increasing predictor \(x_j\) by one unit while holding the remaining predictors fixed changes the log-odds by \(\beta_j\) and multiplies the odds by \(e^{\beta_j}\).

\[\frac{\operatorname{odds}(Y=1\mid x_j+1,\,x_{-j})} {\operatorname{odds}(Y=1\mid x_j,\,x_{-j})}=e^{\beta_j}.\]

The quantity \(e^{\beta_j}\) is the conditional odds ratio (OR) for a one-unit change in predictor \(j\). It is not a probability difference and generally not a relative risk.

Proof. Under the logit model, \(\operatorname{odds}(Y=1\mid x)=e^{\eta(x)}\). Adding one to \(x_j\) increases \(\eta\) by \(\beta_j\), so the new-to-old odds ratio equals \(e^{\eta+\beta_j}/e^\eta=e^{\beta_j}\).

Worked example. For \(\eta(x)=-2+0.8x\), the odds ratio per extra unit of \(x\) is \(e^{0.8}\approx2.23\). However, the predicted probabilities are approximately \(p(0)=0.119\), \(p(1)=0.231\), and \(p(2)=0.401\), so the increases are about 0.112 and 0.170 rather than a fixed number. For a binary predictor, the OR compares the category coded 1 against the reference category coded 0, holding other variables fixed.

Theorem (log-likelihood, score, and curvature).

Let \(X\) be the \(n\times(k+1)\) design matrix, including a column of ones, and let \(p_i=\sigma(x_i^T\beta)\). For conditionally independent Bernoulli responses, the log-likelihood is

\[\ell(\beta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[y_i\log p_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-p_i)\right] =\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[y_i(x_i^T\beta)-\log(1+e^{x_i^T\beta})\right].\]

Its score vector and Hessian are

\[\nabla\ell(\beta)=X^T(y-p),\qquad \nabla^2\ell(\beta)=-X^TWX,\quad W=\operatorname{diag}(p_i[1-p_i]).\]

Thus \(\ell\) is concave. When \(X\) has full column rank, its Hessian is negative definite for every finite \(\beta\), but this does not guarantee that a finite maximizer exists: complete or quasi-complete separation can prevent it.

Proof. For \(\eta=x_i^T\beta\), the first derivative of \(y_i\eta-\log(1+e^\eta)\) is \(y_i-\sigma(\eta)\), and the second is \(-\sigma(\eta)[1-\sigma(\eta)]\). Applying the chain rule and summing yields the stated expressions. For any vector \(v\), \(v^T\nabla^2\ell(\beta)v=-(Xv)^TW(Xv)\leq0\).

Unlike OLS, logistic regression has no general closed-form formula for its maximum-likelihood coefficients. Software uses iterative numerical optimization. Computations should use stable functions such as scipy.special.expit and numpy.logaddexp instead of directly evaluating very large exponentials or logs of probabilities near zero.

Experiment 134: fit the maximum-likelihood coefficients from first principles. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import statsmodels.api as sm
from scipy.special import expit
from scipy.optimize import minimize

rng = np.random.default_rng(134)
x = rng.normal(size=450)
y = rng.binomial(1, expit(-0.6 + 1.1*x))
X = np.column_stack((np.ones_like(x), x))

def negative_log_likelihood(beta):
    eta = X @ beta
    return np.logaddexp(0, eta).sum() - y @ eta

def gradient(beta):
    return X.T @ (expit(X @ beta) - y)

fit_manual = minimize(negative_log_likelihood, x0=[0.0, 0.0],
                      jac=gradient, method="BFGS",
                      options={"gtol": 1e-7})
fit_statsmodels = sm.Logit(y, X).fit(disp=False)
print("Numerical optimization:", np.round(fit_manual.x, 5))
print("statsmodels MLE:     ", np.round(fit_statsmodels.params, 5))
print("Optimizer message:", fit_manual.message)
assert np.allclose(fit_manual.x, fit_statsmodels.params, atol=1e-4)

# Profile the likelihood in the slope, fixing the intercept at its MLE.
beta0_hat, beta1_hat = fit_statsmodels.params
slopes = np.linspace(beta1_hat - 1, beta1_hat + 1, 240)
losses = [negative_log_likelihood([beta0_hat, b]) for b in slopes]
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.plot(slopes, losses)
ax.axvline(beta1_hat, linestyle="--", label="Estimated slope")
ax.set(xlabel="Slope coefficient", ylabel="Negative log-likelihood",
       title="Likelihood-based estimation of logistic coefficients")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_likelihood_profile.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

In a correctly specified finite-dimensional model with identifiable coefficients, suitable regularity conditions, and sufficient information, the maximum-likelihood estimator is approximately normal in large samples:

\[\hat\beta\;\dot\sim\; N\!\left(\beta,\;I(\beta)^{-1}\right),\qquad I(\beta)=X^TWX.\]

Here the information matrix depends on the predictors and the fitted probabilities; the approximation is not a finite-sample identity. Replacing \(\beta\) by \(\hat\beta\) gives the conventional model-based covariance estimate, subject to independent observations and correct model specification. Clustered or otherwise dependent observations require a design-appropriate approach.

Definition (Wald test and confidence interval).

To test \(H_0:\beta_j=\beta_{j,0}\), the usual Wald statistic is

\[Z=\frac{\hat\beta_j-\beta_{j,0}}{\widehat{\operatorname{SE}}(\hat\beta_j)}\;\dot\sim\;N(0,1) \quad\text{under }H_0.\]

An approximate two-sided \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) confidence interval for \(\beta_j\) is \(\hat\beta_j\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\widehat{\operatorname{SE}}(\hat\beta_j)\). Exponentiating both endpoints gives the corresponding Wald interval for its odds ratio. These intervals can be unreliable with small samples, very rare outcomes, or separation.

For a nested-model comparison, a likelihood-ratio test uses \(G^2=2[\ell(\hat\beta_{\rm full})-\ell(\hat\beta_{\rm reduced})]\), which is approximately chi-square with degrees of freedom equal to the number of removed parameters under standard regularity conditions. Neither the Wald nor the likelihood-ratio reference distribution should be treated as exact here.

Experiment 135: interpret estimated odds ratios and confidence intervals. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import statsmodels.api as sm
from scipy.special import expit

rng = np.random.default_rng(135)
n = 700
x = rng.normal(size=n)
z = rng.binomial(1, 0.45, size=n)
p = expit(-1.1 + 0.75*x + 0.9*z)
y = rng.binomial(1, p)

X = sm.add_constant(pd.DataFrame({"x": x, "z": z}))
fit = sm.Logit(y, X).fit(disp=False)
interval = fit.conf_int(alpha=0.05)
summary = pd.DataFrame({
    "log_odds_coef": fit.params,
    "odds_ratio": np.exp(fit.params),
    "OR_lower_95": np.exp(interval.iloc[:, 0]),
    "OR_upper_95": np.exp(interval.iloc[:, 1]),
    "Wald_p_value": fit.pvalues
})
print(summary.round(4))

new = pd.DataFrame({"const": [1, 1], "x": [0.5, 0.5], "z": [0, 1]})
probabilities = np.asarray(fit.predict(new))
print("Predicted probabilities at x=0.5, z=0 vs 1:",
      np.round(probabilities, 4))
print("Probability difference:", round(probabilities[1] - probabilities[0], 4))

Experiment 136: compare Wald and likelihood-ratio inference for a coefficient. 

import numpy as np
import statsmodels.api as sm
from scipy.special import expit
from scipy.stats import chi2, norm

rng = np.random.default_rng(136)
x = rng.normal(size=600)
z = rng.normal(size=600)
y = rng.binomial(1, expit(-0.5 + 0.7*x + 0.4*z))
X_full = sm.add_constant(np.column_stack((x, z)))
X_reduced = sm.add_constant(x)
full = sm.Logit(y, X_full).fit(disp=False)
reduced = sm.Logit(y, X_reduced).fit(disp=False)

# H0: the coefficient of z is zero.
beta_z, se_z = full.params[2], full.bse[2]
z_value = beta_z / se_z
wald_p = 2*norm.sf(abs(z_value))
LR = 2*(full.llf - reduced.llf)
lr_p = chi2.sf(LR, df=1)
ci = full.conf_int()[2]
print(f"Wald z={z_value:.4f}, p={wald_p:.3g}")
print(f"LR statistic={LR:.4f}, p={lr_p:.3g}")
print("95% confidence interval for beta_z:", np.round(ci, 4))
print("95% confidence interval for its OR:", np.round(np.exp(ci), 4))

Complete separation. If a linear combination of predictors perfectly separates all zero outcomes from all one outcomes, increasing the magnitude of some coefficients can keep improving the likelihood without reaching a finite maximum. For example, if every observation with \(x<0\) has \(Y=0\) and every observation with \(x>0\) has \(Y=1\), an unpenalized logistic fit may have no finite slope estimate. Warnings, huge coefficients, and unstable standard errors are symptoms, not evidence of a very precisely estimated effect.

Definition (L2-penalized logistic estimation).

A common predictive alternative minimizes negative log-likelihood with a quadratic penalty on the slope coefficients:

\[\hat\beta_{\lambda}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\beta} \Big[-\ell(\beta)+\frac\lambda2\sum_{j=1}^{k}\beta_j^2\Big],\quad\lambda>0.\]

Depending on software conventions, the penalty and likelihood may be scaled differently. The objective shown illustrates the idea; it is not asserted to be the exact internal objective of every library. In scikit-learn, increasing C means weaker regularization; logistic models are penalized by default. The penalized coefficients should not be analyzed using ordinary unpenalized-MLE Wald intervals.

Experiment 137: demonstrate separation and finite regularized fits. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

x = np.r_[np.linspace(-3, -0.2, 25), np.linspace(0.2, 3, 25)]
y = (x > 0).astype(int)
grid = np.linspace(-3.5, 3.5, 300)[:, None]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(x, y, s=24, alpha=0.7, label="Perfectly separated classes")
for strength in (0.1, 1.0, 100.0):
    clf = LogisticRegression(C=strength, max_iter=1500)
    clf.fit(x[:, None], y)
    ax.plot(grid[:, 0], clf.predict_proba(grid)[:, 1],
            label=f"C={strength}; slope={clf.coef_[0, 0]:.2f}")
    print(f"C={strength}: regularized slope={clf.coef_[0, 0]:.3f}")
ax.set(xlabel="Predictor x", ylabel="Predicted P(Y=1)",
       title="Separation and the role of L2 regularization")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_separation.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

For prediction, sklearn.linear_model.LogisticRegression is convenient, but its default penalty differs from the unpenalized statsmodels.Logit model used for classical coefficient inference. A clean evaluation workflow splits the data before fitting the scaler or classifier, keeps preprocessing in a Pipeline, uses stratification for an ordinary random split when appropriate, and measures performance on untouched test data. Grouped, longitudinal, and temporal observations need grouped or forward-in-time splits instead.

A fitted logistic model produces scores or probabilities. Classification requires an explicit rule, for example \(\hat y=\mathbb 1\{\hat p\geq t\}\) for threshold \(t\in[0,1]\). The conventional \(t=0.5\) is not generally optimal for unequal error costs or imbalanced outcomes. A threshold should be chosen using validation data and the intended decision costs, not tuned on the final test set.

Experiment 138: fit a leakage-safe binary classifier and evaluate held-out predictions. 

import numpy as np
from sklearn.datasets import load_breast_cancer
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import (confusion_matrix, accuracy_score,
                             precision_score, recall_score, roc_auc_score,
                             average_precision_score, log_loss,
                             roc_curve, precision_recall_curve)

# A bundled educational dataset; not a validated clinical diagnostic tool.
dataset = load_breast_cancer()
X = dataset.data
# Original label 0 = malignant, 1 = benign. Choose malignant as class 1.
y = (dataset.target == 0).astype(int)
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.25, random_state=138, stratify=y)

model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(),
                      LogisticRegression(C=1.0, max_iter=3000))
model.fit(X_train, y_train)
prob = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
pred = (prob >= 0.5).astype(int)
print("Positive class: malignant")
print("Confusion matrix [[TN, FP], [FN, TP]]:")
print(confusion_matrix(y_test, pred))
print("Accuracy:", round(accuracy_score(y_test, pred), 4))
print("Precision:", round(precision_score(y_test, pred), 4))
print("Recall:", round(recall_score(y_test, pred), 4))
print("ROC AUC:", round(roc_auc_score(y_test, prob), 4))
print("Average precision:", round(average_precision_score(y_test, prob), 4))
print("Log loss:", round(log_loss(y_test, prob), 4))

fpr, tpr, _ = roc_curve(y_test, prob)
precision, recall, _ = precision_recall_curve(y_test, prob)
fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(10, 4))
axes[0].plot(fpr, tpr, label=f"ROC AUC = {roc_auc_score(y_test, prob):.3f}")
axes[0].plot([0, 1], [0, 1], linestyle="--", label="Random ranking")
axes[0].set(xlabel="False positive rate", ylabel="True positive rate",
            title="ROC curve")
axes[0].legend()
axes[1].plot(recall, precision,
             label=f"Average precision = {average_precision_score(y_test, prob):.3f}")
axes[1].axhline(np.mean(y_test), linestyle="--", label="Positive prevalence")
axes[1].set(xlabel="Recall", ylabel="Precision", ylim=(0, 1.05),
            title="Precision-recall curve")
axes[1].legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_roc_pr.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (confusion-matrix rates).

Let \(TP,FP,TN,FN\) denote true positives, false positives, true negatives, and false negatives at a specified threshold. Then

\[\operatorname{precision}=\frac{TP}{TP+FP},\quad \operatorname{recall}=\frac{TP}{TP+FN},\quad \operatorname{specificity}=\frac{TN}{TN+FP}.\]

The false-positive rate is \(FPR=1-\operatorname{specificity}\), the \(F_1\) score is the harmonic mean of precision and recall when both are defined, and accuracy is \((TP+TN)/n\). Undefined ratios require explicit treatment when their denominators vanish.

A receiver-operating-characteristic (ROC) curve plots recall against FPR over score thresholds; ROC AUC measures ranking discrimination. A precision-recall (PR) curve and average precision are often informative for rare positive classes; their baseline depends on prevalence. Neither AUC nor average precision certifies that output probabilities are calibrated. Additionally, two models can have similar AUC but very different performance at a particular operational threshold.

Experiment 139: choose a decision threshold on validation data, then evaluate once on test data. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.datasets import load_breast_cancer
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import (precision_score, recall_score,
                             confusion_matrix, precision_recall_curve)

source = load_breast_cancer()
X, y = source.data, (source.target == 0).astype(int)
X_trainval, X_test, y_trainval, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.25, stratify=y, random_state=139)
X_train, X_val, y_train, y_val = train_test_split(
    X_trainval, y_trainval, test_size=0.25,
    stratify=y_trainval, random_state=139)
model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression(max_iter=3000))
model.fit(X_train, y_train)
val_prob = model.predict_proba(X_val)[:, 1]

# Choose the threshold on validation data only. For this demonstration,
# maximize recall subject to validation precision >= 0.90.
thresholds = np.linspace(0.05, 0.95, 91)
val_precision = np.array([precision_score(
    y_val, val_prob >= t, zero_division=0) for t in thresholds])
val_recall = np.array([recall_score(
    y_val, val_prob >= t, zero_division=0) for t in thresholds])
valid = np.flatnonzero(val_precision >= 0.90)
if not len(valid):
    raise ValueError("No candidate threshold meets the validation constraint")
chosen = valid[np.argmax(val_recall[valid])]
t_star = float(thresholds[chosen])

# A single final report on untouched test data.
test_prob = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
test_pred = (test_prob >= t_star).astype(int)
print("Threshold selected from validation:", t_star)
print("Test precision:", round(precision_score(y_test, test_pred), 4))
print("Test recall:", round(recall_score(y_test, test_pred), 4))
print("Test confusion matrix:\n", confusion_matrix(y_test, test_pred))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.plot(thresholds, val_precision, label="Validation precision")
ax.plot(thresholds, val_recall, label="Validation recall")
ax.axvline(t_star, linestyle="--", label=f"Chosen threshold = {t_star:.2f}")
ax.set(xlabel="Classification threshold", ylabel="Metric value",
       ylim=(0, 1.05), title="The precision-recall trade-off")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_threshold_tradeoff.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (log loss and Brier score).

For independent binary outcomes \(y_i\) and predicted probabilities \(\hat p_i\), the binary log loss and Brier score are

\[\operatorname{LogLoss}=-\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\left[y_i\log\hat p_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-\hat p_i)\right], \qquad \operatorname{Brier}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n(y_i-\hat p_i)^2.\]

Both are proper scoring rules: in expectation, reporting the true conditional event probability minimizes the score. Small values indicate better probability predictions, though scores also depend on the difficulty and prevalence of the task.

Calibration concerns whether events assigned probability about \(q\) occur about \(q\) of the time. A reliability diagram compares observed outcome rates with mean predicted probabilities within bins. A well-calibrated model need not discriminate strongly, and a high-AUC model need not be calibrated. Reliability diagrams with few observations per bin can be noisy. Calibration must be assessed on held-out data, rather than the same observations used to fit and adjust the model.

Experiment 140: compare calibrated and overconfident probabilities. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.special import expit, logit
from sklearn.calibration import calibration_curve
from sklearn.metrics import brier_score_loss, log_loss, roc_auc_score

rng = np.random.default_rng(140)
score = rng.normal(size=7000)
true_prob = expit(-0.6 + score)
y = rng.binomial(1, true_prob)

# A thought experiment: one model knows the true conditional probability;
# the other reports an overly extreme score, preserving all ranks.
candidates = {
    "Correct conditional probabilities": true_prob,
    "Overconfident probabilities": expit(2*logit(true_prob))
}
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7, 5))
ax.plot([0, 1], [0, 1], linestyle="--", label="Perfect calibration")
for label, prob in candidates.items():
    observed, forecast = calibration_curve(y, prob, n_bins=12,
                                            strategy="quantile")
    ax.plot(forecast, observed, marker="o", label=label)
    print(label)
    print("  Brier:", round(brier_score_loss(y, prob), 4),
          "log loss:", round(log_loss(y, prob), 4),
          "ROC AUC:", round(roc_auc_score(y, prob), 4))
ax.set(xlabel="Mean forecast probability", ylabel="Observed event rate",
       xlim=(0, 1), ylim=(0, 1),
       title="Discrimination and calibration are different")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_calibration.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Experiment 141: compare ROC AUC, average precision, and baseline accuracy for rare events. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.special import expit
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.dummy import DummyClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import (accuracy_score, balanced_accuracy_score,
                             average_precision_score, roc_auc_score,
                             precision_score, recall_score)

rng = np.random.default_rng(141)
X = rng.normal(size=(3500, 3))
true_prob = expit(-3.5 + 1.0*X[:, 0] - 0.5*X[:, 1])
y = rng.binomial(1, true_prob)
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.3, stratify=y, random_state=141)
model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression(max_iter=2000))
model.fit(X_train, y_train)
pred_prob = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
pred_label = (pred_prob >= 0.5).astype(int)
baseline = DummyClassifier(strategy="most_frequent").fit(X_train, y_train)
base_label = baseline.predict(X_test)

print("Positive prevalence:", round(np.mean(y_test), 4))
print("Always-negative baseline accuracy:",
      round(accuracy_score(y_test, base_label), 4))
print("Logistic accuracy:", round(accuracy_score(y_test, pred_label), 4))
print("Logistic balanced accuracy:",
      round(balanced_accuracy_score(y_test, pred_label), 4))
print("Logistic recall at 0.5:", round(recall_score(y_test, pred_label), 4))
print("ROC AUC:", round(roc_auc_score(y_test, pred_prob), 4))
print("Average precision:",
      round(average_precision_score(y_test, pred_prob), 4))
print("PR baseline (prevalence):", round(np.mean(y_test), 4))

Applied example: predicting an examination-score indicator. We return to the SOGA-Py students dataset used in earlier notes. The continuous first-exam result, score1, is the predictor; the second-exam result, score2, supplies a constructed binary response. We define the outcome as whether score2 is at least the training-set median of score2. This is a demonstration of binary modeling, not an official pass/fail threshold. It deliberately discards information from the original numerical score, making it less appropriate than a regression analysis when the exact exam score matters.

The code first attempts the external data download, then explicitly labels a simulated fallback if access fails. After splitting the complete score pairs, it determines the binary-outcome threshold from training observations alone. A fitted probability curve illustrates association between examinations, not a causal effect of taking the first examination on performance in the second.

Experiment 142: apply logistic regression to a constructed exam-score outcome. 

import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import roc_auc_score, brier_score_loss

url = "https://userpage.fu-berlin.de/soga/data/raw-data/students.csv"
try:
    students = pd.read_csv(url)
    data_source = "SOGA-Py students (external data)"
except Exception as error:
    print("Download unavailable; using simulated data:", type(error).__name__)
    rng = np.random.default_rng(142)
    score1 = rng.normal(70, 11, 550)
    score2 = 24 + 0.67*score1 + rng.normal(0, 9, len(score1))
    students = pd.DataFrame({"score1": score1, "score2": score2})
    data_source = "SIMULATED DEMONSTRATION DATA"

clean = students[["score1", "score2"]].apply(
    pd.to_numeric, errors="coerce").dropna()
if len(clean) < 60:
    raise ValueError("At least 60 complete examination-score pairs are needed")
X = clean[["score1"]].to_numpy()
raw_score2 = clean["score2"].to_numpy()
X_train, X_test, s2_train, s2_test = train_test_split(
    X, raw_score2, test_size=0.25, random_state=142)
threshold = float(np.median(s2_train))
y_train = (s2_train >= threshold).astype(int)
y_test = (s2_test >= threshold).astype(int)
if len(np.unique(y_test)) != 2:
    raise ValueError("Need both outcome classes in the test sample")

model = make_pipeline(StandardScaler(), LogisticRegression(max_iter=2000))
model.fit(X_train, y_train)
prob = model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, 1]
print("Data source:", data_source)
print("Training-defined score2 threshold:", round(threshold, 3))
print("Test ROC AUC:", round(roc_auc_score(y_test, prob), 4))
print("Test Brier score:", round(brier_score_loss(y_test, prob), 4))

x_grid = np.linspace(X.min(), X.max(), 300).reshape(-1, 1)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 5))
ax.scatter(X_test[:, 0], y_test, s=15, alpha=0.35,
           label="Held-out binary outcomes")
ax.plot(x_grid[:, 0], model.predict_proba(x_grid)[:, 1],
        linewidth=2, label="Fitted logistic probability")
ax.axhline(0.5, linestyle="--", linewidth=1)
ax.set(xlabel="First examination score", ylabel="P(second score >= training median)",
       ylim=(-0.05, 1.05), title=f"Examination classification: {data_source}")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("logistic_student_exam_outcome.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Assumptions and practical interpretation. Binary logistic regression assumes an appropriate Bernoulli response model and a specified linear relation between predictors and log-odds. Conditional independence of observations is important for the likelihood and ordinary standard errors; matched, repeated, clustered, or time-dependent observations require suitable alternatives. Predictors must contain sufficient variation for identifiable effects, and extreme sparsity or separation can invalidate ordinary maximum-likelihood inference. Check for important nonlinearities and interactions on the logit scale, influential observations, distribution shift, and data leakage. Correlation among predictors may greatly inflate coefficient uncertainty without necessarily ruining predictive discrimination.

Interpretation cautions. An odds ratio does not equal a risk ratio, a statistically significant coefficient does not establish a causal effect, and a predicted probability is not itself a classification. Validation performance depends on the target population, prevalence, and sampling scheme. If outcomes or groups are imbalanced, report the positive-class definition, prevalence, precision, recall, and relevant cost-sensitive performance. For consequential decisions, assess calibration, group-specific error rates, and whether use of the model is appropriate.

Worked exercise (odds versus probability). Consider \(\operatorname{logit}(p)=-1+\log(3)x\) with \(x\in\{0,1\}\). At \(x=0\), the odds are \(e^{-1}\) and \(p\approx0.269\). At \(x=1\), the odds are \(3e^{-1}\) and \(p\approx0.525\). The odds ratio is exactly 3; the probability difference is approximately 0.256, not 3 and not a threefold probability increase.

Further exercises. (1) Differentiate the logistic log-likelihood to recover the score and Hessian, and explain why concavity does not ensure a finite MLE under separation. (2) For \(\eta=-1+0.6x\), compute \(p\) at \(x=0,1,2\), the odds ratio, and the derivatives \(dp/dx\). (3) In Experiment 135, change the coding of binary predictor z from 0/1 to 1/0. Predict the coefficient and odds-ratio change before refitting. (4) Explain why exponentiating an asymptotic coefficient confidence interval gives an OR interval, but exponentiating a probability difference does not. (5) In Experiment 136, compare the Wald and likelihood-ratio p-values for 100 different random samples; identify when the approximation becomes unreliable. (6) In Experiment 139, choose a threshold to prioritize recall instead of precision, and describe how false-positive and false-negative counts change. (7) In Experiment 140, explain why overconfident probabilities can preserve ROC AUC while worsening calibration and proper scoring rules. (8) In Experiment 141, show algebraically why an always-negative classifier can have high accuracy for rare events but zero recall. (9) In Experiment 142, compare the constructed binary task with linear regression on the original numerical score2; discuss information loss.

Textbook exercises. OpenIntro Statistics, 4th ed., Section 9.5, printed pp. 371–378, develops binary logistic regression using a resume-callback example, coefficients and model interpretation. Exercises 9.15–9.18 appear on printed pp. 379–381 and cover possum classification and the Challenger O-ring example; try Exercise 9.15 for coefficient interpretation and Exercise 9.18 for converting a fitted logit into predicted event probabilities. DeGroot and Schervish, Probability and Statistics, 4th ed., Chapter 9 provides general likelihood and hypothesis-testing foundations, while its Chapter 11 treats linear regression; neither is presented here as a dedicated logistic-regression chapter. The likelihood derivatives, separation discussion, and predictive-modeling examples above extend beyond the introductory treatment in OpenIntro.

Software references. statsmodels Logit (maximum likelihood and asymptotic inference); scikit-learn LogisticRegression (regularized prediction); scikit-learn classification metrics; and calibration and probability estimates.

Maximum Likelihood Estimation

Maximum likelihood estimation (MLE) is a general method for estimating parameters of a probabilistic model. It connects the point-estimation theory of mathematical statistics with practical machine learning: fitting a probabilistic model often amounts to minimizing a negative log-likelihood. This section develops the method from first principles and then relates it to regression and classification.

Definition (statistic, estimator, and estimate).

Let \(X\) be observed data taking values in a sample space \(S\). A statistic is a function \(u:S\to T\); the random variable \(u(X)\) depends on the data but not on an unknown parameter. An estimator \(\widehat\theta=u(X)\) is a statistic used to estimate \(\theta\). After observing \(X=x\), the number \(u(x)\) is an estimate.

For a parameter vector \(\theta=(\theta_1,\ldots,\theta_k)\), an estimator can also be vector-valued.

Definition (likelihood and maximum likelihood).

Suppose the distribution of \(X\) is specified by a probability mass function or density \(f_\theta(x)\), where \(\theta\in\Theta\). Fixing the observed data \(x\), the likelihood function is

\[L_x(\theta)=f_\theta(x),\qquad\theta\in\Theta.\]

A maximum likelihood estimate is any global maximizer

\[\widehat\theta(x)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\theta\in\Theta}L_x(\theta).\]

If such a measurable maximizing rule exists, then \(\widehat\theta(X)\) is a maximum likelihood estimator. A maximizer need not exist or be unique.

Interpretation. The data are held fixed and candidate parameter values vary. A likelihood is not a probability distribution over the parameter: in particular, \(L_x(\theta)\) need not integrate to one over \(\Theta\), and a high density is not the probability of observing one particular continuous value.

Lemma (maximizing the log-likelihood).

Whenever \(L_x(\theta)>0\), the natural logarithm preserves the order of likelihood values. Thus maximizers of \(L_x\) and of \(\ell_x(\theta)=\log L_x(\theta)\) coincide. We use the extended convention \(\log0=-\infty\) for impossible parameter values.

Proof. The logarithm is strictly increasing on \((0,\infty)\). Therefore \(L_x(\theta_1)>L_x(\theta_2)\) if and only if \(\log L_x(\theta_1)>\log L_x(\theta_2)\), whenever both values are positive.

Theorem (likelihood of an independent random sample).

If \(X_1,\ldots,X_n\) are independent and identically distributed according to a pmf or density \(g_\theta\), then for observed \(x_1,\ldots,x_n\),

\[L_x(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g_\theta(x_i),\qquad \ell_x(\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log g_\theta(x_i).\]

Proof. Independence gives the product for the joint mass function or density. Taking logarithms converts the product to a sum. Without independence, the joint distribution must be modeled instead; the product formula is not generally valid.

Practical workflow. Specify a model and parameter space, write the likelihood, simplify its logarithm, find candidate maximizers analytically or numerically, and verify boundaries and global optimality. For an interior differentiable optimum, the score \(\nabla\ell(\widehat\theta)\) vanishes; this first-order condition alone is not sufficient for a global maximum.

Theorem (Bernoulli maximum likelihood estimator).

For independent \(X_i\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p)\), let \(K=\sum_iX_i\). If \(\Theta=[0,1]\), then the unique MLE is \(\widehat p=K/n\). For \(0<K<n\) the log-likelihood is

\[\ell(p)=K\log p+(n-K)\log(1-p),\]

with derivative \(K/p-(n-K)/(1-p)\) and negative second derivative \(-K/p^2-(n-K)/(1-p)^2\). The unique critical point is \(p=K/n\). For \(K=0\) or \(K=n\), the maximum is attained at the boundary \(0\) or \(1\), respectively. If instead \(\Theta=(0,1)\), these two boundary samples have no MLE.

For aggregated counts \(K\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(n,p)\), the binomial coefficient \(\binom nk\) multiplies the likelihood. It depends on the data, not \(p\), so it does not affect the maximizing value.

Experiment 143: visualize the likelihood of a Bernoulli success probability. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import binom

n, k = 20, 7
p_grid = np.linspace(0, 1, 501)
likelihood = binom.pmf(k, n, p_grid)
p_hat = k / n

print("MLE p_hat =", p_hat)
print("Likelihood at p_hat =", round(binom.pmf(k, n, p_hat), 6))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.plot(p_grid, likelihood, label="Binomial likelihood (fixed k, n)")
ax.axvline(p_hat, linestyle="--", label=f"MLE = {p_hat:.2f}")
ax.set(xlabel="Candidate success probability p", ylabel="Likelihood L(p)",
       title=f"Observed {k} successes in {n} Bernoulli trials")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_binomial_likelihood.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Theorem (Poisson mean).

For i.i.d. \(X_i\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\lambda)\), \(\lambda\geq0\),

\[\ell(\lambda)=\left(\sum_i x_i\right)\log\lambda-n\lambda-\sum_i\log(x_i!),\quad \lambda>0,\]

so \(\widehat\lambda=\bar X\), with the boundary estimate \(\widehat\lambda=0\) when all observations are zero. If the parameter space excludes zero and all counts are zero, only a supremum exists.

Experiment 144: estimate a Poisson arrival rate analytically and numerically. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize_scalar
from scipy.stats import poisson

counts = np.array([1, 0, 3, 2, 4, 1, 2, 3, 0, 2], dtype=int)
lam_hat = counts.mean()

def nll(lam):
    return -np.sum(poisson.logpmf(counts, mu=lam))

result = minimize_scalar(nll, bounds=(0.001, 10), method="bounded")
print("Analytic MLE:", round(lam_hat, 6))
print("Numerical MLE:", round(result.x, 6))
print("Numerical optimization succeeded:", result.success)
assert abs(result.x - lam_hat) < 1e-4

Theorem (normal mean and variance, both unknown).

For an i.i.d. sample from \(N(\mu,\sigma^2)\), with \(\mu\in\mathbb R\) and \(\sigma^2>0\), the log-likelihood is

\[\ell(\mu,\sigma^2)=-\frac n2\log(2\pi\sigma^2)-\frac1{2\sigma^2}\sum_{i=1}^n(x_i-\mu)^2.\]

If the observations are not all identical, the MLEs are

\[\widehat\mu=\bar X,\qquad \widehat{\sigma}^2_{\rm MLE}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n(X_i-\bar X)^2.\]

Proof. For fixed \(\sigma^2\), maximize by minimizing squared deviations, giving \(\bar X\). Substitution and differentiation with respect to \(\sigma^2\) gives the second formula. If all observations are identical, the likelihood is unbounded as \(\sigma^2\downarrow0\), so no finite MLE exists in the stated parameter space.

The MLE of normal variance divides by \(n\), while the unbiased sample variance divides by \(n-1\). Under normal sampling, \(E(\widehat\sigma^2_{\mathrm{MLE}})=(n-1)\sigma^2/n\). Maximum likelihood does not automatically produce unbiased estimators; bias and mean squared error are different criteria.

Experiment 145: compare normal variance MLE and unbiased sample variance. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(145)
n, repetitions, sigma = 8, 12000, 2.0
samples = rng.normal(3.0, sigma, size=(repetitions, n))
v_mle = np.var(samples, axis=1, ddof=0)
v_unbiased = np.var(samples, axis=1, ddof=1)
truth = sigma**2
for name, estimates in [("MLE", v_mle), ("Unbiased", v_unbiased)]:
    print(name, "mean:", round(estimates.mean(), 4),
          "bias:", round(np.mean(estimates - truth), 4),
          "MSE:", round(np.mean((estimates - truth)**2), 4))
print("Theoretical E[MLE]:", (n-1)/n * truth)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.hist(v_mle, bins=55, density=True, alpha=0.55, label="MLE (divisor n)")
ax.hist(v_unbiased, bins=55, density=True, alpha=0.4,
        label="Unbiased (divisor n-1)")
ax.axvline(truth, linestyle="--", label="True variance")
ax.set(xlabel="Estimated variance", ylabel="Density",
       title="Sampling distributions of two variance estimators")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_normal_variance_bias.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Theorem (exponential rate and invariance).

If \(X_i\) are i.i.d. exponential observations with rate \(\lambda>0\), then

\[\ell(\lambda)=n\log\lambda-\lambda\sum_i x_i,\qquad \widehat\lambda=\frac{n}{\sum_iX_i}=\frac1{\bar X}.\]

If instead the parameter is the mean waiting time \(\tau=1/\lambda\), the invariance property gives \(\widehat\tau=1/\widehat\lambda=\bar X\). In general, when \(\widehat\theta\) is an MLE, \(g(\widehat\theta)\) is an MLE for \(g(\theta)\), when the induced likelihood is defined by profiling over parameter values with the same \(g(\theta)\).

The rate MLE has upward finite-sample bias: for \(n>1\), \(E(\widehat\lambda)=n\lambda/(n-1)\). In contrast, \(\bar X\) is unbiased for the mean waiting time \(\tau\). The invariance of an MLE is therefore not a claim about unbiasedness.

Experiment 146: estimate exponential rate and mean lifetime. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(146)
rate_true, n, repetitions = 0.4, 12, 10000
samples = rng.exponential(scale=1 / rate_true, size=(repetitions, n))
rate_hat = 1 / samples.mean(axis=1)
mean_lifetime_hat = samples.mean(axis=1)
print("Mean estimated rate:", round(rate_hat.mean(), 4))
print("Expected rate MLE:", round(n*rate_true/(n-1), 4))
print("Mean estimated lifetime:", round(mean_lifetime_hat.mean(), 4))

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.hist(rate_hat, bins=65, density=True, alpha=0.75)
ax.axvline(rate_true, linestyle="--", label="True rate")
ax.axvline(rate_hat.mean(), linestyle=":", label="Mean estimated rate")
ax.set(xlabel="Rate MLE", ylabel="Density", title="Small-sample bias of the exponential rate MLE")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_exponential_rate_bias.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Theorem (uniform upper endpoint).

If \(X_i\overset{\rm iid}{\sim}\operatorname{Uniform}(0,\theta)\), \(\theta>0\), and we use the density version supported on \(0\leq x\leq\theta\), then

\[L_x(\theta)=\theta^{-n}\mathbf1\{\theta\geq x_{(n)}\},\qquad \widehat\theta=X_{(n)}=\max_iX_i.\]

Proof. Likelihood is zero below the largest observation and strictly decreases for \(\theta\geq x_{(n)}\); hence the maximum occurs at the smallest admissible endpoint. Since \(E[X_{(n)}]=n\theta/(n+1)\), the MLE is biased downward. The rescaled estimator \((n+1)X_{(n)}/n\) is unbiased, although it is not the MLE.

This is a nonregular model: the support depends on \(\theta\). The score-equals-zero approach is not applicable to its boundary maximum, and ordinary Fisher-information and Wald approximations must not be applied without checking their conditions. Even a choice between strict and non-strict density endpoints can affect mathematical attainment of the maximum, despite leaving the probability distribution unchanged.

Experiment 147: show a boundary MLE in a uniform model.

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(147)
theta_true, n = 5.0, 15
x = rng.uniform(0, theta_true, size=n)
x_max = max(x)
theta_grid = np.linspace(0.5*x_max, 1.7*theta_true, 600)
likelihood = np.where(theta_grid >= x_max, theta_grid**(-n), 0.0)
likelihood /= likelihood.max()

print("Sample maximum (MLE):", round(x_max, 4))
print("Bias-corrected estimate:", round((n+1)/n*x_max, 4))
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.plot(theta_grid, likelihood, label="Likelihood (rescaled to maximum 1)")
ax.axvline(x_max, linestyle="--", label="MLE at sample maximum")
ax.axvline(theta_true, linestyle=":", label="True upper endpoint")
ax.set(xlabel="Candidate upper endpoint", ylabel="Relative likelihood",
       title="A maximum attained at the boundary of the support")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_uniform_boundary.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (score and information).

For a differentiable log-likelihood \(\ell(\theta)\), the score vector and observed information are

\[U(\theta)=\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta),\qquad J(\theta)=-\nabla_\theta^2\ell(\theta).\]

The expected Fisher information is \(I_n(\theta)=E_\theta[J(\theta)]\) under regularity conditions, equivalently \(\operatorname{Var}_\theta[U(\theta)]\) when the information identity holds. For scalar \(\theta\), a typical regular, interior MLE has approximate large-sample distribution

\[\sqrt n(\widehat\theta-\theta_0)\xrightarrow{\mathcal D} N\!\left(0,I_1(\theta_0)^{-1}\right),\]

where \(I_1\) is information in one i.i.d. observation. These claims require identifiability and differentiability, a nonsingular information matrix, and other regularity conditions; they are not universal properties of MLE.

Lemma (Fisher information for Bernoulli observations).

For i.i.d. Bernoulli observations with \(0<p<1\),

\[U(p)=\frac Kp-\frac{n-K}{1-p},\qquad I_n(p)=\frac n{p(1-p)}.\]

Proof. The negative second derivative is \(K/p^2+(n-K)/(1-p)^2\). Its expectation is \(np/p^2+n(1-p)/(1-p)^2=n/[p(1-p)]\). Consequently, the estimated large-sample standard error of \(\widehat p=K/n\) is \(\sqrt{\widehat p(1-\widehat p)/n}\), provided the estimate is away from the boundary.

Experiment 148: check Fisher-information standard errors by simulation. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

rng = np.random.default_rng(148)
p_true, repetitions = 0.35, 5000
sample_sizes = [20, 80, 320]
empirical = []
theoretical = []
for n in sample_sizes:
    estimates = rng.binomial(n, p_true, size=repetitions) / n
    empirical.append(estimates.std(ddof=1))
    theoretical.append(np.sqrt(p_true*(1-p_true)/n))
    print(f"n={n}: empirical SE={empirical[-1]:.5f}; theory={theoretical[-1]:.5f}")

fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.plot(sample_sizes, empirical, "o-", label="Empirical SD of MLE")
ax.plot(sample_sizes, theoretical, "s--", label="Information-based SE")
ax.set(xlabel="Sample size", ylabel="Standard error",
       title="Bernoulli MLE precision increases with sample size")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_fisher_information.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

The original notes already introduce vector parameters and the partial derivative equations \(\partial\ell/\partial\theta_j=0\). With several parameters, the score is a vector and the Hessian is a matrix. Numerical maximization is often unavoidable, but an algorithm returning a stationary point does not prove global optimality. In concave log-likelihood models any stationary point is a global maximum; in nonconcave models several starts or other diagnostics may be needed.

Experiment 149: estimate a two-parameter normal model by numerical optimization. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.optimize import minimize

rng = np.random.default_rng(149)
x = rng.normal(loc=2.3, scale=1.7, size=90)

# Parameterization: theta = (mu, log_sigma) guarantees sigma > 0.
def nll(theta):
    mu, log_sigma = theta
    sigma = np.exp(log_sigma)
    return (len(x) * (0.5*np.log(2*np.pi) + log_sigma)
            + np.sum((x-mu)**2) / (2*sigma**2))

result = minimize(nll, x0=np.array([0.0, 0.0]), method="BFGS",
                  options={"gtol": 1e-7})
mu_mle, sigma_mle = x.mean(), x.std(ddof=0)
print("Analytic MLE (mu, sigma):", np.round([mu_mle, sigma_mle], 5))
print("Numerical MLE (mu, sigma):",
      np.round([result.x[0], np.exp(result.x[1])], 5))
print("Gradient norm:", round(np.linalg.norm(result.jac), 7))
assert np.allclose([result.x[0], np.exp(result.x[1])],
                   [mu_mle, sigma_mle], atol=2e-4)

Numerical tip. Optimize log-standard-deviation rather than standard deviation itself when the parameter must remain positive. For fitted models with constrained parameters, make the constraint explicit and evaluate whether the proposed estimate is on the boundary.

Theorem (likelihood losses for common prediction models).

Under conditional independence, maximizing likelihood is equivalent to minimizing the negative log-likelihood. For predicted means or probabilities \(\mu_i\), \(p_i\), and Poisson intensities \(\lambda_i\), the following objectives arise (up to terms independent of fitted parameters):

  • Gaussian response with fixed common variance: \(\sum_i(y_i-\mu_i)^2\), the squared-error loss.
  • Bernoulli response: \(-\sum_i[y_i\log p_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-p_i)]\), the binary cross-entropy or log loss.
  • Poisson count: \(\sum_i[\lambda_i-y_i\log\lambda_i]\), a Poisson negative log-likelihood.

Proof. Insert the corresponding conditional pmf or density into the independent-observation log-likelihood, sum over observations, negate, and omit additive constants not depending on model parameters. For the Gaussian equivalence, the error variance is held fixed while optimizing model coefficients.

Logistic regression is therefore a special case of maximum likelihood: the predictor variables specify \(p_i=\sigma(x_i^T\beta)\), and binary cross-entropy optimizes its Bernoulli likelihood. A modern prediction system may minimize the average per-observation loss instead of its sum, which has the same unpenalized minimizer but changes how a regularization penalty should be scaled.

Experiment 150: verify Gaussian squared error and Bernoulli cross-entropy. 

import numpy as np
from scipy.stats import norm
from scipy.special import expit, xlogy

x = np.array([-1., 0., 1., 2.])
y_real = np.array([-0.5, 1.3, 2.7, 4.1])
mu = 0.9 + 1.5*x
sigma = 1.4
normal_nll = -np.sum(norm.logpdf(y_real, loc=mu, scale=sigma))
squared_error = np.sum((y_real - mu)**2)
constant = len(x) * (0.5*np.log(2*np.pi) + np.log(sigma))
assert np.isclose(normal_nll, constant + squared_error/(2*sigma**2))

y_binary = np.array([0, 0, 1, 1])
p = expit(-0.4 + 1.2*x)
# xlogy handles 0*log(0) and is safe for exact zero or one observations.
bernoulli_nll = -np.sum(xlogy(y_binary, p)
                         + xlogy(1-y_binary, 1-p))
print("Gaussian NLL:", round(normal_nll, 5))
print("SSE scaled by 1/(2*sigma^2):", round(squared_error/(2*sigma**2), 5))
print("Bernoulli NLL (total log loss):", round(bernoulli_nll, 5))
print("Bernoulli mean log loss:", round(bernoulli_nll/len(y_binary), 5))

Definition (maximum a posteriori estimation).

Given a prior density \(\pi(\theta)\), a maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimate maximizes the posterior density, or equivalently \(L_x(\theta)\pi(\theta)\), when the posterior is well defined. Thus

\[\widehat\theta_{\rm MAP}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\theta} \big[-\ell_x(\theta)-\log\pi(\theta)\big].\]

MLE does not include a prior. MAP adds a prior-dependent term, which often resembles a regularization penalty in machine learning. Unlike MLE, a posterior density mode generally changes nontrivially under nonlinear reparameterization.

Worked example. With \(K=k\) successes out of \(n\) trials and a \(\operatorname{Beta}(a,b)\) prior, the posterior is \(\operatorname{Beta}(k+a,n-k+b)\). When its two parameters exceed 1, the posterior mode is \((k+a-1)/(n+a+b-2)\), whereas the MLE is \(k/n\) and posterior mean is \((k+a)/(n+a+b)\). These are three different estimators with different interpretations.

Experiment 151: compare Bernoulli MLE, MAP, and the posterior distribution. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.stats import beta, binom

n, k = 12, 1
a, b = 3, 3
p_mle = k/n
p_map = (k+a-1)/(n+a+b-2)
p_mean = (k+a)/(n+a+b)
grid = np.linspace(0.001, 0.999, 500)
likelihood = binom.pmf(k, n, grid)
posterior = beta.pdf(grid, k+a, n-k+b)

print("MLE:", round(p_mle, 4), "MAP:", round(p_map, 4),
      "Posterior mean:", round(p_mean, 4))
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.plot(grid, likelihood/likelihood.max(), label="Relative likelihood")
ax.plot(grid, posterior/posterior.max(), label="Posterior density (rescaled)")
ax.axvline(p_mle, linestyle="--", label="MLE")
ax.axvline(p_map, linestyle=":", label="MAP")
ax.set(xlabel="Candidate success probability", ylabel="Relative height",
       title="Data likelihood and prior-adjusted estimation")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_vs_map.png", dpi=170, bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()

Definition (likelihood-ratio interval).

For a scalar parameter \(\theta\), a likelihood-ratio confidence set is obtained by retaining parameter values for which the log-likelihood drop from its maximum is not too large:

\[\mathcal C_{1-\alpha}=\big\{\theta:\ 2[\ell(\widehat\theta)-\ell(\theta)]\leq\chi^2_{1,1-\alpha}\big\}.\]

Under suitable regularity conditions and for large samples, the likelihood-ratio statistic is approximately chi-square with one degree of freedom. This is not an exact finite-sample confidence statement, and the approximation can fail for small samples, parameters on the boundary, or nonregular models. With nuisance parameters, use the profile log-likelihood, maximizing over those parameters at each fixed parameter of interest.

Experiment 152: construct a profile-likelihood confidence interval for Bernoulli p. 

import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from scipy.special import xlogy
from scipy.optimize import brentq
from scipy.stats import chi2, norm

n, k, alpha = 40, 8, 0.05
p_hat = k/n

def loglik(p):
    if p < 0 or p > 1:
        return -np.inf
    return xlogy(k, p) + xlogy(n-k, 1-p)

cutoff = chi2.ppf(1-alpha, df=1)
def lr_stat(p):
    return 2*(loglik(p_hat) - loglik(p))

lower = 0 if k == 0 else brentq(lambda p: lr_stat(p)-cutoff, 0, p_hat)
upper = 1 if k == n else brentq(lambda p: lr_stat(p)-cutoff, p_hat, 1)
wald_se = np.sqrt(p_hat*(1-p_hat)/n)
wald_low = p_hat - norm.ppf(1-alpha/2)*wald_se
wald_high = p_hat + norm.ppf(1-alpha/2)*wald_se
print("MLE:", p_hat)
print("Approximate LR interval:", tuple(round(v, 4) for v in (lower, upper)))
print("Approximate Wald interval:", tuple(round(v, 4) for v in (wald_low, wald_high)))

p_grid = np.linspace(0.001, 0.999, 500)
values = np.array([lr_stat(p) for p in p_grid])
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(8, 4.5))
ax.plot(p_grid, values, label="Likelihood-ratio statistic")
ax.axhline(cutoff, linestyle="--", label="Asymptotic 95% cutoff")
ax.axvspan(lower, upper, alpha=0.18, label="LR confidence set")
ax.set(xlabel="Candidate success probability p", ylabel="2 × log-likelihood drop",
       ylim=(0, 15), title="Likelihood-based uncertainty for a binomial proportion")
ax.legend()
fig.tight_layout()
fig.savefig("mle_likelihood_ratio_interval.png", dpi=170,
            bbox_inches="tight")
plt.show()
  • Model adequacy: an optimizer can maximize a badly misspecified model perfectly. Check observed-versus-predicted behavior, independence assumptions, support, and outliers.
  • Identifiability: distinct parameters can sometimes produce the same data distribution; the MLE can then be nonunique.
  • Existence: a supremum is not necessarily attained. Bernoulli endpoints, uniform support parameters, and complete separation in logistic regression illustrate this issue.
  • Numerics: use log-likelihoods for stability, enforce parameter constraints, and check convergence, objective values, and curvature rather than trusting an optimizer's reported point uncritically.
  • Uncertainty: likelihood curvature, bootstrap procedures, and likelihood-ratio sets require assumptions; regular asymptotic formulas should not be used mechanically at boundaries.
  • Machine learning: avoid leakage between training and evaluation data; optimization of a likelihood-based training loss does not guarantee good generalization or calibrated predictions.
  1. Bernoulli: Observe \(x=(1,0,1,1,0,0,1,0)\). Write the likelihood and log-likelihood; derive the MLE for \(p\in[0,1]\). Repeat when the permissible parameter space is \([0,0.4]\).
  2. Boundary case: Assume \(X_i\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p)\) with \(p\in(0,1)\), and every observation is zero. Prove that the likelihood has a supremum but no maximizer. Compare with \(p\in[0,1]\).
  3. Poisson: Derive the MLE for \(\lambda\) from first principles, including a sample of all zeros. State how your answer changes when the parameter space is \((0,\infty)\).
  4. Normal: Derive the joint MLEs of \(\mu\) and \(\sigma^2\). Show that \(E(\widehat\sigma^2_{\rm MLE})=(n-1)\sigma^2/n\), and compare its mean squared error to the unbiased variance estimator.
  5. Invariance: For exponential waiting times, parameterize the distribution by rate \(\lambda\), then by mean \(\tau\). Show that maximizing either parameterization gives the same fitted distribution.
  6. Nonregular model: Determine the MLE of the upper endpoint \(\theta\) for a sample from \(\operatorname{Uniform}(0,\theta)\). Explain why setting a derivative to zero does not find it.
  7. Machine learning: Starting from independent Bernoulli observations with different probabilities \(p_i\), derive binary cross-entropy. Explain why the same loss applies to logistic regression despite its predictors not being identically distributed.
  8. Likelihood inference: For \(n=12\) and \(k=1\), construct the approximate 95% likelihood-ratio interval numerically. Compare it with a simple Wald interval and discuss why small-sample coverage must be checked rather than assumed.

Random Services: Kyle Siegrist, Point Estimation — Maximum Likelihood. Its Basic Theory and examples treat the same definitions as the author's original draft, the invariance property, and Bernoulli, normal, exponential, and uniform models. For the broader chapter, see Point Estimation.

Connection to these notes: the earlier sections on sampling distributions, hypothesis tests, linear regression, and logistic regression use ideas developed here. In particular, Bernoulli negative log-likelihood is binary cross-entropy, Gaussian negative log-likelihood with fixed variance reduces to squared-error fitting, and likelihood-ratio inference provides an alternative to Wald-type procedures.
